3. let  $(\overline{x},\overline{y},\overline{s})$  be primal dual vectors of the modified lp with  $\overline{x}\cdot \overline{s}\approx_{0.5}\mu \cdot \tau (\overline{x},\overline{s})$  for  $\mu <  \delta^2 /(8d)$  and small enough  $\phi_b\coloneqq \frac{1}{\mu}\cdot \| \overline{\mathbf{a}}^\top \overline{x} -\overline{b}\|_{(\overline{\mathbf{a}}^\top \overline{\mathbf{x}}\mathbf{s}^{-1}\overline{\mathbf{a}})^{-1}}^2 = o(1)$  (i.e.  $\overline{x}$  does not have to be feasible). the vector  $\widehat{x}\stackrel {\mathrm{def}}{=}r\cdot \overline{x}_{1:n}$  where  $\overline{x}_{1:n}$  is the first  $n$  coordinates of  $\overline{x}$  is an approximate solution to the original linear program in the following sense

$$
c ^ {\top} \hat {x} \leq \min  _ {\mathbf {a} ^ {\top} x = b, x \geq 0} c ^ {\top} x + o (n l r) \cdot (\sqrt {\phi_ {b}} + \delta),
$$

$$
\left. \left\| \mathbf {a} ^ {\top} \hat {x} - b \right\| _ {2} \leq o (n ^ {2}) \cdot \left(\left\| \mathbf {a} \right\| _ {f} r + \| b \| _ {2}\right) \cdot \left(\sqrt {\phi_ {b}} + \delta\right)\right),
$$

$$
\hat {x} \geq 0.
$$

as outlined before, the initial points given in theorem 12 do not satisfy

$$
x s \approx_ {\epsilon} \mu \cdot (\sigma ({\bf s} ^ {- 1 / 2 - \alpha} {\bf x} ^ {1 / 2 - \alpha} {\bf a}) + \frac {d}{n} 1).
$$

to satisfy this condition we pick  $x = 1$  and  $\mu = 1$ , and pick the initial slack vector  $s$  a to satisfy  $s \approx_{\epsilon} \sigma(\mathbf{s}^{-\frac{1}{2} - \alpha}\mathbf{a}) + \frac{d}{n}1$ , which is exactly the condition for  $\ell_p$  lewis weight with  $p = \frac{1}{1 + \alpha}$ . the following theorem shows that such a vector  $s$  can be found efficiently as long as  $p \in (0,4)$ . this vector  $s$  might not be a valid slack vector, so as outlined before, the algorithm runs in two phases: first using a cost vector  $c^{(tmp)}$  for which the initial  $s$  is feasible, and then switching to the correct  $c$ .

theorem 13 ([cp15]). given  $p \in (0,4)$ ,  $\eta > 0$  and non-degenerate  $\mathbf{a} \in \mathbb{r}^{n \times d}$  w.h.p. in  $n$ , we can compute  $w \in \mathbb{r}_{>0}^n$  with  $w \approx_{\epsilon} \sigma(\mathbf{w}^{\frac{1}{2} - \frac{1}{p}}\mathbf{a}) + \eta 1$  in  $\widetilde{\mathrm{o}}((\mathrm{nnz}(\mathbf{a}) + d^{\omega})\mathrm{poly}(1 / \epsilon))$  time.

proof. our proof is similar to [cp15], which proved a variant when  $\eta = 0$  consider the map  $t(w)\stackrel {\mathrm{def}}{=}(\mathbf{w}^{\frac{2}{p} -1}(\sigma (\mathbf{w}^{\frac{1}{2} -\frac{1}{p}}\mathbf{a}) + \eta 1))^{p / 2}$  . further, fix any positive vectors  $v,w\in \mathbb{r}^n$  such that  $v\approx_{\alpha}w$  . we have that  $\mathbf{a}^\top \mathbf{v}^{1 - \frac{1}{p}}\mathbf{a}\approx_{|1 - \frac{2}{p} |\alpha}\mathbf{a}^\top \mathbf{w}^{1 - \frac{2}{p}}\mathbf{a}$  and hence

$$
a _ {i} ^ {\top} \left(\mathbf {a} ^ {\top} \mathbf {v} ^ {1 - \frac {2}{p}} \mathbf {a}\right) ^ {- 1} a _ {i} \approx_ {| 1 - \frac {2}{p} | \alpha} a _ {i} ^ {\top} \left(\mathbf {a} ^ {\top} \mathbf {w} ^ {1 - \frac {2}{p}} \mathbf {a}\right) ^ {- 1} a _ {i}. \tag {4.2}
$$

note that

$$
t (v) _ {i} ^ {2 / p} = \frac {v _ {i} ^ {1 - \frac {2}{p}} a _ {i} ^ {\top} (\mathbf {a} ^ {\top} \mathbf {v} ^ {1 - \frac {2}{p}} \mathbf {a}) ^ {- 1} a _ {i} + \eta}{v _ {i} ^ {1 - \frac {2}{p}}} = a _ {i} ^ {\top} (\mathbf {a} ^ {\top} \mathbf {v} ^ {1 - \frac {2}{p}} \mathbf {a}) ^ {- 1} a _ {i} + \eta v _ {i} ^ {\frac {2}{p} - 1}.
$$

using (4.2), we have

$$
\begin{array}{l} t (v) _ {i} ^ {2 / p} \leq e ^ {| 1 - \frac {2}{p} | \alpha} a _ {i} ^ {\top} (\mathbf {a} ^ {\top} \mathbf {w} ^ {1 - \frac {2}{p}} \mathbf {a}) ^ {- 1} a _ {i} + \eta v _ {i} ^ {\frac {2}{p} - 1} \\ \leq e ^ {| 1 - \frac {2}{p} | \alpha} (a _ {i} ^ {\top} (\mathbf {a} ^ {\top} \mathbf {w} ^ {1 - \frac {2}{p}} \mathbf {a}) ^ {- 1} a _ {i} + \eta w _ {i} ^ {\frac {2}{p} - 1}) = e ^ {| 1 - \frac {2}{p} | \alpha} t (w) _ {i} ^ {2 / p}. \\ \end{array}
$$

similarly, we have  $t(v)_i^{2/p} \geq e^{-|1 - \frac{2}{p}| \alpha} t(w)_i^{2/p}$ . taking  $p/2$  power of both sides, we have

$$
e ^ {- | \frac {p}{2} - 1 | \alpha} t (w) _ {i} \leq t (v) _ {i} \leq e ^ {| \frac {p}{2} - 1 | \alpha} t (w) _ {i}.
$$

hence,  $t(v) \approx_{|p/2 - 1|\alpha} t(w)$ .

consequently, let  $w_0 = \eta 1$  and consider the algorithm  $w_{k + 1} = t(w_k)$ . since  $\eta > 0$  we have that

$$
w _ {0} = w _ {0} \eta^ {- p / 2} \eta^ {p / 2} \leq t (w _ {0}) \leq w _ {0} \eta^ {- p / 2} (1 + \eta) ^ {p / 2} \leq w _ {0} \exp (p \eta^ {- 1} / 2).
$$

consequently,  $t(w_0) \approx_{p\eta^{-1}/2} w_0$  and after  $k$  steps we have that  $t(w_k) \approx_{\exp(|p/2-1|^k p\eta^{-1}/2)} w_k$ . since for  $p \in (0,4)$ , we have that  $|p/2 - 1| < 1$  we see that after  $o(\log(\eta^{-1}/\epsilon))$  steps we have  $w_k \approx_{\epsilon} t(w_k)$ . further, since  $w_k \geq \eta 1$  implies that  $t(w_k) \geq \eta 1$  we have that  $w_k \in \mathbb{r}_{>0}^n$  as desired. to implement the steps, one can check, by the same proof, that it suffices to get a  $\approx_{o(\epsilon)}$  multiplicative approximation to  $t(w)$  in each step, which can be done in  $\widetilde{\mathrm{o}}((\mathrm{nnz}(\mathbf{a}) + d^\omega)/\epsilon^2)$  per step by standard leverage score estimation techniques.

now, we first prove the correctness of the algorithm 2.

lemma 14. algorithm 2 outputs  $x$  such that w.h.p. in  $n$

$$
\begin{array}{l} c ^ {\top} x \leq \min  _ {\mathbf {a} ^ {\top} x = b, x \geq 0} c ^ {\top} x + l r \cdot \delta , \\ \left\| \mathbf {a} ^ {\top} x - b \right\| _ {2} \leq \delta \cdot \left(\left\| \mathbf {a} \right\| _ {f} \cdot r + \left\| b \right\| _ {2}\right), \\ x \geq 0. \\ \end{array}
$$

proof. we define the primal dual point  $(x, s)$  via the formula of lines 12 and 13. we start by showing that our implementation of algorithm 1 in algorithm 2 satisfies all required conditions, i.e., that we can apply theorem 5. throughout this proof, states hold only w.h.p. and therefore the restatement of this is often omitted for brevity.

invariant:  $\overline{x} \approx_{\gamma/4} x$ ,  $\overline{s} \approx_{\gamma/4} s$ ,  $\overline{\tau} \approx_{\gamma/4} \sigma(\overline{\mathbf{s}}^{-1/2-\alpha}\overline{\mathbf{x}}^{1/2-\alpha}\mathbf{a}) + \frac{d}{n} 1$  and  $\psi_{(\mathrm{safe})} \approx_{\epsilon} (\mathbf{a}^{\top}\overline{\mathbf{s}}^{-1}\overline{\mathbf{x}}\mathbf{a})^{-1}$ : we first show that throughout centering, we have the invariant above, assuming the input parameter  $\tau_i^{(\mathrm{init})}$  satisfied  $\tau_i^{(\mathrm{init})} \approx_{\gamma/4} \sigma(\mathbf{s}^{-1/2-\alpha}\mathbf{x}^{1/2-\alpha}\mathbf{a}) + \frac{d}{n} 1$ . theorem 8 shows that  $x^{(\mathrm{tmp})} \approx_{\gamma/8} x$  and  $s^{(\mathrm{tmp})} \approx_{\gamma/8} s$  (line 42 and 43). then, by the update rule (line 29 and 30), we have that  $x^{(\mathrm{tmp})} \approx_{\gamma/8} \overline{x}$  and  $s^{(\mathrm{tmp})} \approx_{\gamma/8} \overline{s}$ . hence, we have the desired approximation for  $\overline{x}$  and  $\overline{s}$ .

next, theorem 10 shows that

$\psi^{(\alpha)} \approx_{\gamma/(512\log n)} (\mathbf{a}^\top \mathbf{s}^{-1 - 2\alpha} \mathbf{x}^{1 - 2\alpha} \mathbf{a})^{-1}$  (line 15) and

-  $\psi_{\mathrm{(safe)}}^{(\alpha)} \approx_{\gamma/(512 \log n)} (\mathbf{a}^\top \mathbf{s}^{-1 - 2\alpha} \mathbf{x}^{1 - 2\alpha} \mathbf{a})^{-1}$  (line 39).

hence, we can apply theorem 9 and get

$$
\tau^ {(\mathrm {t m p})} \approx_ {\gamma / 8} \sigma (\overline {{\mathbf {s}}} ^ {- 1 / 2 - \alpha} \overline {{\mathbf {x}}} ^ {1 / 2 - \alpha} \mathbf {a}) + \frac {d}{n} 1
$$

(line 16 and line 44). again, by update rule (line 31), we have the desired approximation for  $\overline{\tau}$

finally,  $\psi_{\mathrm{(safe)}}\approx_{\epsilon}(\mathbf{a}^{\top}\overline{\mathbf{s}}^{-1}\overline{\mathbf{x}}\mathbf{a})^{-1}$  follows from theorem 10 (line 40). these invariants also imply  $\| \overline{v} -v\|_{\infty}\leq \gamma$  and that  $d_{\mathrm{gradient}}$  (theorem 60) maintains  $\nabla \phi (\overline{v} ')^{b(\overline{\tau})}$  for some  $\| \overline{v}^{\prime} - v\|_{\infty}\leq \gamma$  thus, in summary, centering of algorithm 2 behaves like centering of theorem 5.

invariant:  $xs \approx \mu \cdot \tau(x, s)$ : initially,  $x = 1$  due to the reduction (lemma 12),  $\mu = 1$  and  $s \approx_{\epsilon} \sigma(\mathbf{s}^{-1/2 - \alpha}\mathbf{a}) + \frac{d}{n}1$  (line 13). hence,  $xs \approx_{\epsilon} \mu \cdot (\sigma(\mathbf{s}^{-1/2 - \alpha}\mathbf{x}^{1/2 - \alpha}\mathbf{a}) + \frac{d}{n}1)$  initially.

we change  $x, s, \mu$  in line 20 by calling centering. by theorem 5 we then have  $xs \approx_{\epsilon} \mu \tau$  after this call to centering. next, consider the step where we switch the cost vector on line 21. let  $s^{(\mathrm{new})}$  be the vector  $s$  after line 21 and  $s$  is before line 21. since  $\mathbf{a}y + s = c^{\mathrm{(tmp)}}$  we have that  $\mathbf{a}y + s^{(\mathrm{new})} = c^{\mathrm{(tmp)}} - s + s^{(\mathrm{new})} = c$ , i.e.  $s$  is a valid slack vector for cost  $c$ . further, we have that, by design

$$
\frac {s ^ {\mathrm {(n e w)}} - s}{s} = \frac {c - c ^ {\mathrm {(t m p)}}}{s}.
$$

first, we bound the denominator. we have that  $sx \approx_{1/2} \mu \tau$ , so for all  $i \in [n]$  we have

$$
s _ {i} \geq \frac {\mu}{2 x _ {i}} \frac {d}{n} \geq \frac {\mu}{\omega (n ^ {2})}
$$

where we used  $\| x\|_{\infty}\leq o(n)$  by lemma 12 as we ensure  $x$  is sufficiently close to feasible for the modified linear program by theorem 5. for the numerator, we note that  $\| c\|_{\infty}\leq 1$  for the modified linear program and  $\| c^{(\mathrm{tmp})}\|_{\infty} = \| s\|_{\infty}\leq 3$  for the  $s$  computed by theorem 13 in line 13.again by the definition of  $s$  (line 13) and the modified  $\mathbf{a}$  and  $y$ . hence, we have that

$$
\left\| \frac {s ^ {\mathrm {(n e w)}} - s}{s} \right\| _ {\infty} \leq \frac {1 6 n ^ {2}}{\mu} \leq \frac {\gamma \alpha^ {2}}{c \cdot \sqrt {d}}.
$$

for any constant  $c$  by choosing the constant in the  $o(\cdot)$  in line 20 appropriately. thus  $xs \approx_{2\epsilon} \mu \cdot \tau(x, s)$  and  $\overline{\tau} \approx_{\gamma/4} \sigma(\mathbf{s}^{-1/2 - \alpha}\mathbf{x}^{1/2 - \alpha}\mathbf{a}) + \frac{d}{n}1$ , so when we call centering in line 23, we again obtain  $xs \approx_{\epsilon} \mu \cdot \tau(x, s)$ .

conclusion: before the algorithm ends, we have  $xs \approx_{1/4} \mu \tau$ . in line 24, we reduce  $\phi_b := \| \mathbf{a}^\top x - b \|_{(\mathbf{a}^\top \mathbf{x}\mathbf{s}^{-1}\mathbf{a})^{-1}}^2$  to some small enough  $\phi_b = o(\delta / n^2)$ . by corollary 57 this does not move the vector  $x$  too much, i.e. we still have  $x \cdot s \approx_{1/2} \mu \cdot \tau(x, s)$ . hence, by the choice of the new  $\mu$  in line 23, lemma 12 shows that we can output a point  $\hat{x}$  with the desired properties.

finally, we analyze the cost of the algorithm 2.

lemma 15. algorithm 2 takes  $o((nd + d^3) \log^{o(1)} n \log (n / \delta))$  time with high probability in  $n$ .

proof. for simplicity, we use  $\widetilde{\mathrm{o}} (\cdot)$  to suppress all terms that are  $\log^{o(1)}n$ . we first note that all parameters  $\alpha ,\epsilon ,\lambda ,\gamma$  are either  $\log^{o(1)}n$  or  $1 / \log^{o(1)}n$ . the cost of algorithm 2 is dominated by the repeated calls to centering.

number of iterations: by theorem 5 the calls to centering in lines 20 and 23 perform  $\tilde{\mathrm{o}} (\sqrt{d}\log (1 / \delta))$  many iterations in total.

cost of  $d_{\mathrm{inverse}}$ : note that  $\| \tau^{-1}\delta_{\tau}\|_{\tau +\infty} = 2\epsilon$ ,  $\| \mathbf{s}^{-1}\delta_s\|_{\tau +\infty}\leq \epsilon /2$ , and  $\| \mathbf{x}^{-1}\delta_x\|_{\tau +\infty}\leq \epsilon /2$  by theorem 5. hence, the weight of the vector  $w = \mathbf{s}^{-1 - 2\alpha}x^{1 - 2\alpha}$  satisfies  $\| \mathbf{w}^{-1}\delta_w\|_{\tau} = o(\epsilon)$ . note however, that we need  $d_{\mathrm{inverse}}$ . update to compute the inverse with accuracy  $o(\gamma /\log n)$  and hence theorem 10 requires the relative movement of  $w$  to be less than  $\gamma /\log n$ . this can be fixed by splitting the step into  $\widetilde{\mathrm{o}} (1)$  pieces. now, theorem 10 shows that the total cost is  $\widetilde{\mathrm{o}} (d^{\omega} + \sqrt{d}\log (1 / \delta)(n + d^{\omega -\frac{1}{2}} + d^{2})) = \widetilde{\mathrm{o}} ((\sqrt{d} n + d^{\omega} + d^{2.5})\log (1 / \delta))$  where the term  $d^{\omega}$  is the initial cost and  $d^{\omega -\frac{1}{2}} + d^{2}$  is the amortized cost per step. note that the solver runs with accuracy  $\widetilde{\mathrm{o}} (d^{-1 / 4})$  so the total time for all calls to  $d_{\mathrm{inverse}}$ . solve will be  $\widetilde{\mathrm{o}} ((\sqrt{d} n + d^{3})\log (1 / \delta))$ .

cost of  $d_{\mathrm{leverage}}$ : by lemma 11, the total movement of the projection matrix is

$$
\sum_ {k \in [ k - 1 ]} \left\| \sqrt {\mathbf {w} ^ {(k + 1)}} \mathbf {a} \boldsymbol {\psi} ^ {(k + 1)} \mathbf {a} ^ {\top} \sqrt {\mathbf {w} ^ {(k + 1)}} - \sqrt {\mathbf {w} ^ {(k)}} \mathbf {a} \boldsymbol {\psi} ^ {(k)} \mathbf {a} ^ {\top} \sqrt {\mathbf {w} ^ {(k)}} \right\| _ {f} = \widetilde {\mathrm {o}} (k)
$$

where  $k$  is the number of steps and  $w = \mathbf{s}^{-1 - 2\alpha}x^{1 - 2\alpha}$ . then, theorem 9 shows that the total cost is  $o(\frac{n}{d} k^2 \cdot d) = o(nk^2) = o(nd\log^2(1/\delta))$ .

$\mathbf{cost}$  of  $d_{\mathbf{matvec}}^{(x)}$  and  $d_{\mathbf{matvec}}^{(s)}$ : by theorem 8, the total cost for  $d_{\mathrm{matvec}}^{(x)}$  is bounded by  $\widetilde{\mathrm{o}}(k^2 \max \|\mathbf{x}^{-1}\delta_x\|_2^2 \cdot d + kn)$  where  $\max \|\mathbf{x}^{-1}\delta_x\|_2^2$  is the maximum movement in one step in  $\ell_2$ -norm. note that  $\tau \geq \frac{d}{n}$  and hence  $\| \mathbf{x}^{-1}\delta_x\|_2^2 \leq \frac{n}{d}\| \mathbf{x}^{-1}\delta_x\|_\tau^2 = \widetilde{\mathrm{o}}\left(\frac{n}{d}\right)$ . hence, we have that the cost is  $\widetilde{\mathrm{o}}(d\log^2(1/\delta) \cdot \frac{n}{d} \cdot d) = \widetilde{\mathrm{o}}(nd)$ . the bound for  $d_{\mathrm{matvec}}^{(s)}$  is the same.

cost of maintaining  $\mathbf{a}^{\top}\overline{\mathbf{x}}h$  ( $d_{\mathrm{gradient}}$ ): the cost of maintaining  $\mathbf{a}^{\top}\overline{\mathbf{x}}h$  is exactly equal to  $d$  times the number of coordinates changes in  $\overline{x}, \overline{s}, \overline{\tau}$ . note that we change the exact  $x, s, \tau$  by at most around  $(1 \pm \gamma/8)$  multiplicative factor in every step. so, the total number of entry changes performed to  $\overline{x}, \overline{s}, \overline{\tau}$  is bounded by  $\widetilde{\mathrm{o}}\left(k^{2}\left(\max\|\mathbf{x}^{-1}\delta_{x}\|_{2}^{2} + \max\|\mathbf{s}^{-1}\delta_{s}\|_{2}^{2} + \max\|\tau^{-1}\delta_{\tau}\|_{2}^{2}\right)\right)$ , where max refers to the maximum movement in any step in  $\ell_{2}$ -norm. as we showed before, all the movement terms are bounded by  $\widetilde{\mathrm{o}}\left(\frac{n}{d}\right)$  (see the paragraphs regarding  $d_{\mathrm{matvec}}^{(x)}$ ,  $d_{\mathrm{matvec}}^{(s)}$ , and  $d_{\mathrm{inverse}}$ ). so in total there are  $\widetilde{\mathrm{o}}(n\log^{2}(1/\delta))$  many entry changes we perform to  $\overline{x}, \overline{s}, \overline{\tau}$ . hence, the total cost of maintenance is again  $\widetilde{\mathrm{o}}(nd\log^{2}(1/\delta))$ .

cost of implementing maintainfeasibility (algorithm 10): by theorem 7 we pay  $\widetilde{\mathrm{o}}(nd^{0.5} + d^{2.5})$  amortized time per call to maintainfeasibility. additionally, we must compute  $\widetilde{\mathrm{o}}(n/\sqrt{d} + d^{1.5})$  entries of  $x$  in each iteration (i.e., call  $d_{\mathrm{matvec}}^{(x)}$ .computeract(i)), which costs  $\widetilde{\mathrm{o}}(d)$  per call, so we have total cost of  $\widetilde{\mathrm{o}}(nd + d^3)$  after  $\widetilde{\mathrm{o}}(\sqrt{d})$  iterations.

removing the extra  $\log(1/\delta)$  term: we note that all the extra  $\log(1/\delta)$  terms are due to running the data structures for  $\sqrt{d}\log(1/\delta)$  steps. however, we can reinitialize the data structures every  $\sqrt{d}$  iterations. this decreases the  $k$  dependence from  $k^2$  to  $k\sqrt{d}$ .

independence and adaptive adversaries: randomized data-structures often can not handle inputs that depend on outputs of the previous iteration. for example theorem 10 is such a case, where the input to update is not allowed to depend on the output of any previous calls to update. in our algorithm 2 the input to the data-structures inherently depends on their previous output, so here we want to verify that this does not cause any issues.

the data-structures  $d_{\mathrm{matvec}}^{(x)}$  and  $d_{\mathrm{matvec}}^{(s)}$  are given by theorem 8, which explicitly states that the data-structure works against adaptive adversaries. this means, that the input is allowed to depend on the output of previous iterations. likewise,  $d_{\mathrm{leverage}}$  works against adaptive adversaries by theorem 9 (provided the input  $\psi_{\mathrm{(safe)}}^{(\alpha)}$  are chosen by randomness independent of the randomness chosen for  $\psi^{(\alpha)}$  which is the case by lemma 11). the only issue is with  $d_{\mathrm{inverse}}$  (given by theorem 10), where the input  $w, \tilde{\tau}$  to update is not allowed to depend on the output of previous calls to update (however,  $w$  and  $\tilde{\tau}$  are allowed to depend on the output of solve by lemma 11). also note, that the inputs  $\bar{w}, b, \delta$  to solve are allowed to depend on any previous output of update and solve, as theorem 10 only has issues with the inputs  $w$  and  $\tilde{\tau}$  to update. so to show that our algorithm works, we are only left with verifying that the input  $w, \tilde{\tau}$  to update does not depend on previous results of update. let us prove this by induction.

the result of update is  $\psi^{(\alpha)}\gets d_{\mathrm{inverse}}.\mathrm{update}(\overline{\mathbf{s}}^{-1 - 2\alpha}\overline{x}^{1 - 2\alpha},\overline{\tau})$  , and when executing this line for the very first time, it can obviously not depend on a previous output yet. the matrix  $\psi^{(\alpha)}$  is only used as input to  $d_{\mathrm{leverage}}.\mathrm{query}(\psi^{(\alpha)},\psi_{\mathrm{(safe)}}^{(\alpha)})$  , but by theorem 9 the output of this procedure does not depend on  $\psi^{(\alpha)}$  . hence  $\psi^{(\alpha)}$  does not affect anything else, which also means the input  $\overline{\mathbf{s}}^{-1 - 2\alpha}\overline{x}^{1 - 2\alpha}$  and  $\overline{\tau}$  to the next call of  $d_{\mathrm{inverse}}.\mathrm{update}$  do not depend on previous  $\psi^{(\alpha)}$

algorithm 3: lp algorithm (based on algorithm 1), continuation of algorithm 2.  
26 procedure centering  $(x^{(\mathrm{init})}\in \mathbb{r}_{>0}^n,s^{(\mathrm{init})}\in \mathbb{r}_{>0}^n,\tau^{(\mathrm{init})} > 0,\mu^{(\mathrm{init})} > 0,\mu^{(\mathrm{target})} > 0)$    
27  $\mu \leftarrow \mu^{(\mathrm{init})},\bar{\tau}\leftarrow \tau^{(\mathrm{init})},\tau^{(\mathrm{tmp})}\leftarrow \tau^{(\mathrm{init})},\bar{x}\leftarrow x^{(\mathrm{init})},x^{(\mathrm{tmp})}\leftarrow x^{(\mathrm{init})},\bar{s}\leftarrow s^{(\mathrm{init})},$ $s^{(\mathrm{tmp})}\gets s^{(\mathrm{init})}$    
28 while true do   
29  $\overline{x}_i = x_i^{(\mathrm{tmp})}$  for all  $i$  such that  $\overline{x}_i\not\approx_{\gamma /8}x_i^{(\mathrm{tmp})};$  // ensure  $\overline{x}\approx_{\gamma /4}x$    
30  $\overline{s}_i = s_i^{(\mathrm{tmp})}$  for all  $i$  such that  $\overline{s}_i\not\approx_{\gamma /8}s_i^{(\mathrm{tmp})};$  // ensure  $\overline{s}\approx_{\gamma /4}s$    
31  $\overline{\tau}_i = \tau_i^{(\mathrm{tmp})}$  for all  $i$  such that  $\overline{\tau}_i\not\approx_{\gamma /8}\tau_i^{(\mathrm{tmp})}$    
32  $\overline{w}\gets \overline{\mathbf{x}}\overline{s},\overline{v}\gets \mu \cdot \overline{\mathbf{w}}^{-1}\overline{\tau};$  // ensure  $\| \overline{v} -v\|_{\infty}\leq \gamma$  where  $v = \mu \cdot \tau (x,s) / w$    
33 /* check termination conditions */   
34 let  $\phi (v)\stackrel {\mathrm{def}}{=}\exp (\lambda (v - 1)) + \exp (-\lambda (v - 1))$  for all  $v\in \mathbb{r}^n$    
if  $\mu = \mu^{\mathrm{(target)}}$  and  $\phi (\overline{v})\leq \frac{2^{16}n\sqrt{d}}{\alpha^2}$  then break;   
35 /* update  $h = \gamma \nabla \phi (\overline{v})^b$ $r = a^\top xh$  (see lemma 28 for  $(\cdot)^b$  definition)*/   
36 dgradient. update(i,vi,ri,xi) for i where vi, ri or xi changed   
 $h,r\gets d_{\text{gradient}}.\text{query()}$    
/\* pick any  $\mathbf{h}\in \mathbb{r}^{d\times d}$  with  ${\bf h}\approx_{(c\epsilon) / (d^{1 / 4}\log^3 n)}{\bf a}^{\top}{\bf s}^{-1}{\bf x}{\bf a}$  /\*   
/\* let q  $= \overline{\mathbf{s}}^{-1 / 2}\overline{\mathbf{x}}^{1 / 2}\mathbf{a}\mathbf{h}^{-1}\mathbf{a}^{\top}\overline{\mathbf{x}}^{1 / 2}\overline{\mathbf{s}}^{-1 / 2},$  w=xs /\* update the inverse,  $\psi^{(\alpha)},\psi^{(\alpha)}_{(\mathrm{saf e})}\approx_{\epsilon}(\mathbf{a}^{\top}\overline{\mathbf{s}}^{-1 - 2\alpha}\overline{\mathbf{x}}^{1 - 2\alpha}\mathbf{a})^{-1}$  and   
 $\psi_{(\mathrm{saf e})} = \mathbf{h}^{-1}\approx_{c\epsilon /(d^{1 / 4}\log^3 (n))}(\mathbf{a}^{\top}\overline{\mathbf{s}}^{-1}\overline{\mathbf{x}}\mathbf{a})^{-1}$  /\*   
37  $\psi^{(\alpha)},g\gets d_{\text{inverse}.}.\text{update} (\overline{\mathbf{s}}^{-1 - 2\alpha}\overline{x}^{1 - 2\alpha},\overline{\tau})//$  update the approximate inverse   
38 dleverage. update(√g); // only scale coordinates where g changed.   
//  $\psi_{(\mathrm{saf e})}^{(\alpha)},\psi_{(\mathrm{saf e})}$  are implicit representations of calls to dinverse.solve   
39  $\psi_{(\mathrm{saf e})}^{(\alpha)}(b)\stackrel {\mathrm{def}}{=}d_{\text{inverse}.}.\text{solve}(b,\overline{\mathbf{s}}^{-1 - 2\alpha}\overline{x}^{1 - 2\alpha},\gamma /512\log n)$    
40  $\psi_{(\mathrm{saf e})}(b)\stackrel {\mathrm{def}}{=}d_{\text{inverse}.}.\text{solve}(b,\overline{\mathbf{s}}^{-1}\overline{x},(c\epsilon) / (d^{1 / 4}\log^3 n))$    
/\*  $x\gets x + (1 + 2\alpha)\overline{\mathbf{x}}\overline{\mathbf{w}}^{-1 / 2}(\mathbf{i} - \mathbf{q})\overline{\mathbf{w}}^{1 / 2}h$ $s\gets s + (1 - 2\alpha)\overline{\mathbf{s}}\overline{\mathbf{w}}^{-1 / 2}\mathbf{q}\overline{\mathbf{w}}^{1 / 2}h*$    
41 d(x) matvec.scale(x/s); // only scale coordinates where x or s changed.   
42  $x^{\mathrm{(tmp)}}\gets d_{\mathrm{matvec}.}.\mathrm{query}((1 + 2\alpha)\psi_{(\mathrm{saf e})}r,(1 + 2\alpha)\overline{\mathbf{x}} h)$    
43  $s^{\mathrm{(tmp)}}\gets d_{\mathrm{matvec}.}.\mathrm{query}((1 - 2\alpha)\psi_{(\mathrm{saf e})}r,0_n)$    
44  $\tau^{\mathrm{(tmp)}}\gets d_{\text{leverage}.}.\text{query} (\psi^{(\alpha)},\psi_{(\text{saf e})}^{(\alpha)})$    
45  $\delta_{\lambda}\gets$  maintainfeasibility(d(x),d(s)),// algorithm 10   
46  $x^{\mathrm{(tmp)}}\gets d_{\mathrm{matvec}.}.\mathrm{query}(\delta_{\lambda},0)$    
47 if  $\mu >\mu^{\mathrm{(target)}}$  then  $\mu \leftarrow \max (\mu^{\mathrm{(target)}},(1 - \frac{\gamma\alpha}{2^{15}\sqrt{d}})\mu);$    
48 else if  $\mu <  \mu^{\mathrm{(target)}}$  then  $\mu \leftarrow \min (\mu^{\mathrm{(target)}},(1 + \frac{\gamma\alpha}{2^{15}\sqrt{d}})\mu);$    
49  $x\gets d_{\text{matvec}.}.\text{compuerexact}(1,\dots,n)$ $s\gets d_{\text{matvec}.}.\text{compuerexact}(1,\dots,n)$    
50 return (x,s,τ(tmp),μ)

# 5 robust primal dual ls-path following

here we provide our stable primal-dual  $\widetilde{\mathrm{o}} (\sqrt{d})$ -iteration primal-dual ipm for solving (1.1). in particular we provide the main subroutines for making progress along the central path as discussed in section 2.1 and prove theorem 1 given in section 4.1.

as discussed we assume throughout that  $\mathbf{a}$  is non-degenerate, meaning it has full-column rank and no zero rows and consider the primal dual central path (2.1) induced by the weight function  $\tau_{\mathrm{reg}}(x,s)\stackrel {\mathrm{def}}{=}\sigma (\mathbf{s}^{-1 / 2 - \alpha}\mathbf{x}^{1 / 2 - \alpha}\mathbf{a}) + \frac{d}{n} 1$  where  $\mathbf{x} = \mathbf{diag}(x)$  and  $\mathbf{s} = \mathbf{diag}(s)$  (see definition 4).

rather than precisely following the central path, we follow the approach of [cls19, lsz19, bra20] and instead follow it approximately. the primary goal of this section is to design an efficient primal dual ipm that only maintains  $x$ ,  $s$ ,  $\mathbf{a}^{\top}\mathbf{s}^{-1/2}\mathbf{x}^{1/2}\mathbf{a}$ , and  $\tau_{\mathrm{reg}}(x,s)$  multiplicatively and yet converges in  $\widetilde{\mathrm{o}}(\sqrt{d})$  iterations.

our algorithms maintain a near feasible point  $(x,s)\in \mathbb{r}_{\geq 0}^{n}\times \mathbb{r}_{\geq 0}^{n}$  (see definition 3) and attempt to improve the quality of this point with respect to the central path. formally, we consider the vector  $w = \mathbf{x}s$  , and then attempt to make  $w\approx \mu \cdot \tau_{\mathrm{reg}}(x,s)$  . we measure this quality of approximation or centrality as follows.

definition 16  $(\mu, \epsilon)$ -centered point). we say that point  $(x, s) \in \mathbb{r}_{\geq 0}^n \times \mathbb{r}_{\geq 0}^n$  is  $(\mu, \epsilon)$ -centered if  $\mu > 0$  and  $w \stackrel{\mathrm{def}}{=} \mathbf{x}s$  for  $\mathbf{w} = \mathbf{diag}(w)$  satisfies  $w \approx_{\epsilon} \mu \cdot \tau_{\mathrm{reg}}(x, s)$ .

similar to previous papers [cls19, lsz19, bra20] our algorithms measure progress or the quality of this approximation by

$$
\phi (x, s, \mu) = \sum_ {i \in [ n ]} \phi (\mu \cdot \tau_ {i} \cdot w _ {i} ^ {- 1}) \mathrm {w i t h} \phi (v) \stackrel {\mathrm {d e f}} {=} \exp (\lambda (v - 1)) + \exp (- \lambda (v - 1))
$$

for a setting of  $\lambda = 2\epsilon^{-1}\log (2^{16}n\sqrt{d}\alpha^{-2})$  we derive later. in contrast to these recent papers, here our potential function does not depend solely on  $w$  as  $x$  and  $s$  are used to determine the value of  $\tau$ . for notational convenience, we often let  $v\in \mathbb{r}^n$  denote the vector with  $v_{i} = \mu \cdot \tau_{i}\cdot w_{i}^{-1}$  for all  $i\in [n]$  and overload notation letting  $\phi (v)\stackrel {\mathrm{def}}{=}\sum_{i\in [n]}\phi (v_i)$ .

to update our points and improve the potential we consider attempting to move  $w$  in some direction  $h$  suggested by  $\nabla \phi(v)$ . to do so we solve for  $\delta_s, \delta_x, \delta_y$  satisfying the following

$$
\overline {{\mathbf {x}}} \delta_ {s} + \overline {{\mathbf {s}}} \delta_ {x} = \overline {{\mathbf {w}}} h, \mathbf {a} ^ {\top} \delta_ {x} = 0, \mathrm {a n d} \mathbf {a} \delta_ {y} + \delta_ {s} = 0
$$

where  $\overline{\mathbf{w}} = \overline{\mathbf{x}}\overline{\mathbf{s}}$ . solving this system of equations, we have

$$
\delta_ {x} = \overline {{\mathbf {x w}}} ^ {- 1 / 2} (\mathbf {i} - \mathbf {p} (\overline {{\mathbf {s}}} ^ {- 1 / 2} \overline {{\mathbf {x}}} ^ {1 / 2} \mathbf {a})) \overline {{\mathbf {w}}} ^ {1 / 2} h \mathrm {a n d} \delta_ {s} = \overline {{\mathbf {s w}}} ^ {- 1 / 2} \mathbf {p} (\overline {{\mathbf {s}}} ^ {- 1 / 2} \overline {{\mathbf {x}}} ^ {1 / 2} \mathbf {a}) \overline {{\mathbf {w}}} ^ {1 / 2} h.
$$

often, our algorithms we will not have access to  $\mathbf{a}^{\top}\overline{\mathbf{s}}^{-1}\overline{\mathbf{x}}\mathbf{a}$  exactly and instead we will only have a spectral approximation  $\mathbf{h}$ . in these cases we will instead consider  $\mathbf{q} \stackrel{\mathrm{def}}{=} \overline{\mathbf{s}}^{-1/2}\overline{\mathbf{x}}^{1/2}\mathbf{a}\mathbf{h}^{-1}\mathbf{a}^{\top}\overline{\mathbf{x}}^{1/2}\overline{\mathbf{s}}^{-1/2}$  as a replacement for  $\mathbf{p}$ . further, to account for the change due to  $\sigma$ , we slightly change our steps size amounts. formally, we define our steps as follows:

definition 17 (steps). we call  $(\delta_x, \delta_s)$  an  $\epsilon$ -newton direction from  $(x, s) \in \mathbb{r}_{\geq 0}^n \times \mathbb{r}_{\geq 0}^n$  if  $\overline{x} \approx_{\epsilon} x$ ,  $\overline{s} \approx_{\epsilon} s$ , and  $\mathbf{h} \approx_{\epsilon} \mathbf{a}^\top \overline{\mathbf{s}}^{-1} \overline{\mathbf{x}} \mathbf{a}$  with  $\epsilon \leq 1/80$ , and  $\delta_x, \delta_s \in \mathbb{r}^n$  are given as follows

$$
\delta_ {x} = (1 + 2 \alpha) \overline {{\mathbf {x w}}} ^ {- 1 / 2} (\mathbf {i} - \mathbf {q}) \overline {{\mathbf {w}}} ^ {1 / 2} h \text {a n d} \delta_ {s} = (1 - 2 \alpha) \overline {{\mathbf {s w}}} ^ {- 1 / 2} \mathbf {q} \overline {{\mathbf {w}}} ^ {1 / 2} h
$$

when

$$
\mathbf {q} = \overline {{\mathbf {s}}} ^ {- 1 / 2} \overline {{\mathbf {x}}} ^ {1 / 2} \mathbf {a} \mathbf {h} ^ {- 1} \mathbf {a} ^ {\top} \overline {{\mathbf {x}}} ^ {1 / 2} \overline {{\mathbf {s}}} ^ {- 1 / 2}.
$$

note that, since we use  $\mathbf{h}$  and  $\mathbf{q}$  (rather than a true orthogonal projection) it is not necessarily the case that  $\mathbf{a}^{\top}\delta_{x} = 0$ . consequently, we will need to take further steps to control this error and this is analyzed in section b.

much of the remaining analysis is bounding the effect of such a step and leveraging this analysis to tune  $\phi$  and  $h$ . in section 5.1 we bound the change in the point when we take a newton step, in section 5.2 we bound the effect of a newton step on centrality for arbitrary  $\phi$ , and in section 5.3 we analyze the particular structure of  $\phi$  and use this to prove theorem 1.

# 5.1 stability of weight changes

here we bound the change in  $x$  and  $s$  when we take an newton step from a centered point. for a point  $(x, s) \in \mathbb{r}_{\geq 0}^n \times \mathbb{r}_{\geq 0}^n$  we will wish to analyze the movement both with respect to  $\| \cdot \|_{\infty}$  and  $\| \cdot \|_{\tau}$  for  $\tau = \tau_{\mathrm{reg}}(x, s)$ . to simplify this analysis we define the following mixed norm  $\| \cdot \|_{\tau + \infty}$  by

$$
\| x \| _ {\tau + \infty} = \| x \| _ {\infty} + c _ {\mathrm {n o r m}} \| x \| _ {\tau} \mathrm {f o r} c _ {\mathrm {n o r m}} \stackrel {\mathrm {d e f}} {=} \frac {1 0}{\alpha}.
$$

note that by the definition of  $\alpha$  we have  $c_{\mathrm{norm}} \geq 10$ . we leverage this norm extensively in our analysis and make frequent use of the following simple facts about such norms.

fact 18 (norm facts). for any  $d \in \mathbb{r}^n$ , we have that  $\| \mathbf{diag}(d) \|_{\tau + \infty} = \| d \|_{\infty}$ . for any  $\mathbf{m} \in \mathbb{r}^{n \times n}$ , we have  $\| \mathbf{m} \|_{\tau} = \| \mathbf{t}^{1/2} \mathbf{m} \mathbf{t}^{-1/2} \|_2$  for  $\mathbf{t} \stackrel{\mathrm{def}}{=} \mathbf{diag}(\tau)$ .

next, we relate leverage scores of the projection matrix  $\mathbf{p}(\mathbf{s}^{-1/2}\mathbf{x}^{1/2}\mathbf{a})$  considered in taking a newton step to the leverage scores used to measure centrality, i.e.  $\sigma(\mathbf{s}^{-1/2 - \alpha}\mathbf{x}^{1/2 - \alpha}\mathbf{a})$ .

lemma 19. for  $(\mu, \epsilon)$ -centered point  $(x, s)$  with  $\epsilon \in [0, 1/80)$  we have

$$
\frac {1}{2} \sigma (\mathbf {s} ^ {- 1 / 2 - \alpha} \mathbf {x} ^ {1 / 2 - \alpha} \mathbf {a}) \leq \sigma (\mathbf {s} ^ {- 1 / 2} \mathbf {x} ^ {1 / 2} \mathbf {a}) \leq 2 \sigma (\mathbf {s} ^ {- 1 / 2 - \alpha} \mathbf {x} ^ {1 / 2 - \alpha} \mathbf {a}).
$$

proof. by assumption  $w = \mathbf{x}s$  satisfies  $w \approx_{\epsilon} \mu[\sigma(\mathbf{s}^{-1/2 - \alpha}\mathbf{x}^{1/2 - \alpha}\mathbf{a}) + \frac{d}{n}]$ . since leverage scores lie between 0 and 1, this implies  $e^{-\epsilon} \frac{d}{n} \leq \mu^{-1}w \leq e^{\epsilon}2$ . consequently  $e^{-\alpha \epsilon}2^{-\alpha}\mathbf{i} \preceq \mu^{\alpha}\mathbf{w}^{-\alpha} \preceq e^{\alpha \epsilon}(\frac{d}{n})^{-\alpha}\mathbf{i}$  and since  $\alpha = 1/(4\log(4n/d))$  we have that entrywise

$$
\begin{array}{l} \sigma \left(\mathbf {s} ^ {- 1 / 2} \mathbf {x} ^ {1 / 2} \mathbf {w} ^ {- \alpha}\right) \geq \sigma \left(\mathbf {s} ^ {- 1 / 2} \mathbf {x} ^ {1 / 2}\right) \cdot \frac {e ^ {- 2 \alpha \epsilon} 2 ^ {- 2 \alpha}}{e ^ {2 \alpha \epsilon} \left(\frac {d}{n}\right) ^ {- 2 \alpha}} = \sigma \left(\mathbf {s} ^ {- 1 / 2} \mathbf {x} ^ {1 / 2}\right) \cdot e ^ {- 4 \alpha \epsilon} (2 n / d) ^ {- 2 \alpha} \\ \geq \frac {\sigma (\mathbf {s} ^ {- 1 / 2} \mathbf {x} ^ {1 / 2})}{e ^ {1 / 2 + 4 \alpha \epsilon}} \\ \end{array}
$$

and analogous calculation shows that

$$
\sigma \left(\mathbf {s} ^ {- 1 / 2} \mathbf {x} ^ {1 / 2} \mathbf {w} ^ {- \alpha}\right) \leq \sigma \left(\mathbf {s} ^ {- 1 / 2} \mathbf {x} ^ {1 / 2}\right) \cdot e ^ {4 \alpha \epsilon} (2 n / d) ^ {2 \alpha} \leq \sigma \left(\mathbf {s} ^ {- 1 / 2} \mathbf {x} ^ {1 / 2}\right) e ^ {1 / 2 + 4 \alpha \epsilon}.
$$

the result then follows from the assumption  $\epsilon \leq 1 / 80$

leveraging lemma 19 we obtain the following bounds on  $\mathbf{q}$  and  $\overline{\mathbf{w}}^{-1/2} \mathbf{q} \overline{\mathbf{w}}^{1/2} h$  for newton directions.

lemma 20. let  $(\delta_x, \delta_s)$  denote an  $\epsilon$ -newton direction from  $(\mu, \epsilon)$ -centered  $(x, s)$  with  $\epsilon \in [0,1/80)$ . then  $\mathbf{q} \preceq e^{3\epsilon} \mathbf{p}(\mathbf{s}^{-1/2} \mathbf{x}^{1/2} \mathbf{a})$ . further, for all  $h \in \mathbb{r}^n$ ,

$$
\| \overline {{\mathbf {w}}} ^ {- 1 / 2} \mathbf {q} \overline {{\mathbf {w}}} ^ {1 / 2} h \| _ {\tau} \leq e ^ {4 \epsilon} \| h \| _ {\tau} a n d \| \overline {{\mathbf {w}}} ^ {- 1 / 2} (\mathbf {i} - \mathbf {q}) \overline {{\mathbf {w}}} ^ {1 / 2} h \| _ {\tau} \leq e ^ {3 \epsilon} \| h \| _ {\tau}.
$$

further,  $\| \overline{\mathbf{w}}^{-1 / 2}\mathbf{q}\overline{\mathbf{w}}^{1 / 2}h\|_{\infty}\leq 2\| h\|_{\tau}$  and therefore  $\| \overline{\mathbf{w}}^{-1 / 2}\mathbf{q}\overline{\mathbf{w}}^{1 / 2}\|_{\tau +\infty}\leq e^{4\epsilon} + 2 / c_{\mathrm{norm}}$

proof. since  $\mathbf{h} \approx_{\epsilon} \mathbf{a}^{\top} \overline{\mathbf{s}}^{-1} \overline{\mathbf{x}} \mathbf{a}$ , we have  $\mathbf{h}^{-1} \preceq e^{\epsilon} (\mathbf{a}^{\top} \overline{\mathbf{s}}^{-1} \overline{\mathbf{x}} \mathbf{a})^{-1}$  and

$$
\mathbf {q} \preceq e ^ {\epsilon} \overline {{\mathbf {s}}} ^ {- 1 / 2} \overline {{\mathbf {x}}} ^ {1 / 2} \mathbf {a} (\mathbf {a} ^ {\top} \overline {{\mathbf {s}}} ^ {- 1} \overline {{\mathbf {x}}} \mathbf {a}) ^ {- 1} \mathbf {a} ^ {\top} \overline {{\mathbf {x}}} ^ {1 / 2} \overline {{\mathbf {s}}} ^ {- 1 / 2} = e ^ {\epsilon} \mathbf {p} (\overline {{\mathbf {s}}} ^ {- 1 / 2} \overline {{\mathbf {x}}} ^ {1 / 2} \mathbf {a}) \preceq e ^ {\epsilon} \mathbf {i}.
$$

since  $\mathbf{q}$  is psd this implies  $\| \mathbf{q}\| _2\leq e^\epsilon$  and since  $\overline{w}\approx_{2\epsilon}w$  and  $w\approx_{\epsilon}\mu \tau$  this implies

$$
\| \overline {{\mathbf {w}}} ^ {- 1 / 2} \mathbf {q} \overline {{\mathbf {w}}} ^ {1 / 2} h \| _ {\tau} \leq e ^ {1. 5 \epsilon} \mu^ {- 1 / 2} \| \mathbf {q} \overline {{\mathbf {w}}} ^ {1 / 2} h \| _ {2} \leq e ^ {2. 5 \epsilon} \mu^ {- 1 / 2} \| \overline {{\mathbf {w}}} ^ {1 / 2} h \| _ {2} \leq e ^ {4 \epsilon} \| h \| _ {\tau}.
$$

similarly, we have  $0 \preceq \mathbf{q} \preceq e^{\epsilon} \mathbf{i}$  and hence  $\| \mathbf{i} - \mathbf{q} \|_2 \leq 1$  (using that  $\epsilon \leq 1/80$ ). therefore, we have

$$
\| \overline {{\mathbf {w}}} ^ {- 1 / 2} (\mathbf {i} - \mathbf {q}) \overline {{\mathbf {w}}} ^ {1 / 2} h \| _ {\tau} \leq e ^ {1. 5 \epsilon} \mu^ {- 1 / 2} \| (\mathbf {i} - \mathbf {q}) \overline {{\mathbf {w}}} ^ {1 / 2} h \| _ {2} \leq e ^ {1. 5 \epsilon} \mu^ {- 1 / 2} \| \overline {{\mathbf {w}}} ^ {1 / 2} h \| _ {2} \leq e ^ {3 \epsilon} \| h \| _ {\tau}.
$$

further, by cauchy schwarz and  $\mathbf{q} = \mathbf{q}^{1 / 2}\mathbf{q}^{1 / 2}$  we have

$$
\| \overline {{\mathbf {w}}} ^ {- 1 / 2} \mathbf {q} \overline {{\mathbf {w}}} ^ {1 / 2} h \| _ {\infty} ^ {2} = \max _ {i \in [ n ]} \left[ e _ {i} ^ {\top} \overline {{\mathbf {w}}} ^ {- 1 / 2} \mathbf {q} \overline {{\mathbf {w}}} ^ {1 / 2} h \right] ^ {2} \leq \max _ {i \in [ n ]} \left[ \overline {{\mathbf {w}}} ^ {- 1 / 2} \mathbf {q} \overline {{\mathbf {w}}} ^ {- 1 / 2} \right] _ {i, i} \cdot \left[ h ^ {\top} \overline {{\mathbf {w}}} ^ {1 / 2} \mathbf {q} \overline {{\mathbf {w}}} ^ {1 / 2} h \right]
$$

since  $\mathbf{q} \preceq e^{\epsilon}\mathbf{i}$  we have

$$
h ^ {\top} \overline {{\mathbf {w}}} ^ {1 / 2} \mathbf {q} \overline {{\mathbf {w}}} ^ {1 / 2} h \leq e ^ {\epsilon} h ^ {\top} \overline {{\mathbf {w}}} h \preceq \mu e ^ {4 \epsilon} \| h \| _ {\tau} ^ {2}.
$$

further, by  $\mathbf{q} \preceq e^{\epsilon}\mathbf{p}(\overline{\mathbf{s}}^{-1/2}\overline{\mathbf{x}}^{1/2}) \preceq e^{3\epsilon}\mathbf{p}(\mathbf{s}^{-1/2}\mathbf{x}^{1/2})$  and lemma 19 we have that

$$
\begin{array}{l} \max  _ {i \in [ n ]} \left[ \overline {{\mathbf {w}}} ^ {- 1 / 2} \mathbf {q} \overline {{\mathbf {w}}} ^ {- 1 / 2} \right] _ {i, i} \leq \max  _ {i \in [ n ]} \frac {e ^ {3 \epsilon} \sigma (\mathbf {s} ^ {- 1 / 2} \mathbf {x} ^ {1 / 2}) _ {i}}{\overline {{w}} _ {i}} \\ \leq \frac {e ^ {6 \epsilon}}{\mu} \max _ {i \in [ n ]} \frac {\sigma (\mathbf {s} ^ {- 1 / 2} \mathbf {x} ^ {1 / 2}) _ {i}}{\tau_ {i}} \\ \leq \frac {2 e ^ {6 \epsilon}}{\mu}. \\ \end{array}
$$

combining these and using  $\epsilon \leq 1 / 80$  yields the desired bounds.

![](images/bd731d926f269b693b4ddc485e7cb221484b6dfdf9f95cff86ae0407bee559ac.jpg)

leveraging lemma 19 we obtain the following bounds on the multiplicative stability of newton directions.

lemma 21. let  $(\delta_x, \delta_s)$  denote an  $\epsilon$ -newton direction from  $(\mu, \epsilon)$ -centered  $(x, s)$  and let  $\tau \stackrel{\mathrm{def}}{=} \tau_{\mathrm{reg}}(x, s)$ . then the following hold

$$
\| \mathbf {s} ^ {- 1} \delta_ {s} \| _ {\tau} \leq (1 - 2 \alpha) e ^ {5 \epsilon} \| h \| _ {\tau} \quad \| \mathbf {s} ^ {- 1} \delta_ {s} \| _ {\infty} \leq (1 - 2 \alpha) 3 \| h \| _ {\tau},
$$

$$
\| \mathbf {x} ^ {- 1} \delta_ {x} \| _ {\tau} \leq (1 + 2 \alpha) e ^ {4 \epsilon} \| h \| _ {\tau}, a n d \| \mathbf {x} ^ {- 1} \delta_ {x} \| _ {\infty} \leq (1 + 2 \alpha) \left[ e ^ {\epsilon} \| h \| _ {\infty} + 3 \| h \| _ {\tau} \right].
$$

proof. note that  $\mathbf{s}^{-1}\delta_s = (1 - 2\alpha)\mathbf{s}^{-1}\overline{\mathbf{s}\mathbf{w}}^{-1 / 2}\mathbf{q}\overline{\mathbf{w}}^{1 / 2}h$ . since  $s\approx_{\epsilon}\overline{s}$  and  $\overline{w}\approx_{2\epsilon}w$  the bounds on  $\| \mathbf{s}^{-1}\delta_s\|_{\tau}$  and  $\| \mathbf{s}^{-1}\delta_s\|_{\infty}$  follow immediately from lemma 18 and lemma 20.

similarly,  $\mathbf{x}^{-1}\delta_{x} = (1 + 2\alpha)\mathbf{x}^{-1}\overline{\mathbf{x}\mathbf{w}}^{-1 / 2}(\mathbf{i} - \mathbf{q})\overline{\mathbf{w}}^{1 / 2}h$ . since  $\overline{x}\approx_{\epsilon}x$  and  $\overline{w}\approx_{2\epsilon}w$ , we have  $\| \mathbf{x}^{-1}\overline{\mathbf{x}} h\|_{\tau}\leq e^{\epsilon}\| h\|_{\tau}$  and  $\| \mathbf{x}^{-1}\overline{\mathbf{x}} h\|_{\infty}\leq e^{\epsilon}\| h\|_{\infty}$ . the bounds on  $\| \mathbf{x}^{-1}\delta_x\|_{\tau}$  and  $\| \mathbf{x}^{-1}\delta_x\|_{\infty}$  follow by triangle inequality and the same derivation as for  $\| \mathbf{s}^{-1}\delta_s\|_{\tau}$  and  $\| \mathbf{s}^{-1}\delta_s\|_{\infty}$ .

# 5.2 newton step progress

here we analyze the effect of a newton step from a  $(\mu, \epsilon)$  centered point on the potential  $\phi(x, s, \mu)$ . we show that up to some additive error on  $h$  in the appropriate mixed norm the potential decreases by  $\nabla \phi(v)^{\top} h$ . the main result of this section is the following.

theorem 22. let  $(\delta_x, \delta_s)$  denote an  $\epsilon$ -newton direction from  $(\mu, \epsilon)$ -centered  $(x, s)$  and suppose that  $\gamma \stackrel{\mathrm{def}}{=} \|h\|_{\tau + \infty} \leq \epsilon \leq \frac{\alpha}{4000}$  for  $\tau \stackrel{\mathrm{def}}{=} \tau_{\mathrm{reg}}(x, s)$ . there are  $v', e \in \mathbb{r}^n$  satisfying

$$
\| v ^ {\prime} - \mu \mathbf {w} ^ {- 1} \tau \| _ {\infty} \leq 1 0 \gamma a n d \| e \| _ {\tau + \infty} \leq \left(1 - \frac {\alpha}{2}\right) \gamma
$$

such that

$$
\phi (x + \delta_ {x}, s + \delta_ {s}, \mu) = \phi (x, s, \mu) - \nabla \phi (v ^ {\prime}) ^ {\top} (h + e)
$$

where  $[\nabla \phi(v')]_i$  is the derivative of  $\phi$  at  $(v')_i$  for all  $i \in [n]$ .

we prove this theorem in multiple steps. first, in lemma 23 we directly compute the change in the potential function by chain rule and mean value theorem. then, in lemma 24 and lemma 25 we bound the terms in this change of potential formula and use this to provide a formula for the approximate change in the potential in lemma 26. finally, in lemma 26 we bound this approximate change and use this to prove theorem 22.

lemma 23. in the setting of theorem 22 for all  $t \in [0,1]$  let  $x_{t} = x + t\delta_{x}$  and  $s_t = s + t\delta_s$ . then for some  $t_*$ , we have that

$$
\phi (x + \delta_ {x}, s + \delta_ {s}, \mu) - \phi (x _ {0}, s _ {0}, \mu) = - \nabla \phi (v _ {t _ {*}}) ^ {\top} \mathbf {j} _ {t _ {*}} h
$$

where for all  $t\in [0,1]$  we let  $w_{t}\stackrel {\mathrm{def}}{=}\mathbf{x}_{t}s_{t},v_{t}\stackrel {\mathrm{def}}{=}\mu \mathbf{w}_{t}^{-1}\tau_{t},\tau_{t}\stackrel {\mathrm{def}}{=}\tau_{\mathrm{reg}}(x_{t},s_{t})$ $\mathbf{t}_t\stackrel {\mathrm{def}}{=}\mathbf{d}\mathbf{i}\mathbf{a}\mathbf{g}(\tau_t)$ $\lambda (\mathbf{x}_t^{1 / 2 - \alpha}\mathbf{s}_t^{-1 / 2 - \alpha}\mathbf{a})$  and  $\mathbf{j}_t\stackrel {\mathrm{def}}{=}(1 - 2\alpha)\mathbf{j}_t^s +(1 + 2\alpha)\mathbf{j}_t^x$  with

$$
\mathbf {j} _ {t} ^ {s} \stackrel {\text {d e f}} {=} \left[ \mu \mathbf {w} _ {t} ^ {- 1} \mathbf {t} _ {t} \right] \left[ \mathbf {i} + (1 + 2 \alpha) \mathbf {t} _ {t} ^ {- 1} \boldsymbol {\lambda} _ {t} \right] \mathbf {s} _ {t} ^ {- 1} \overline {{\mathbf {s w}}} ^ {- 1 / 2} \mathbf {q} \overline {{\mathbf {w}}} ^ {1 / 2}, a n d
$$

$$
\mathbf {j} _ {t} ^ {x} \stackrel {\mathrm {d e f}} {=} \left[ \mu \mathbf {w} _ {t} ^ {- 1} \mathbf {t} _ {t} \right] \left[ \mathbf {i} - (1 - 2 \alpha) \mathbf {t} _ {t} ^ {- 1} \boldsymbol {\lambda} _ {t} \right] \mathbf {x} _ {t} ^ {- 1} \overline {{\mathbf {x w}}} ^ {- 1 / 2} (\mathbf {i} - \mathbf {q}) \overline {{\mathbf {w}}} ^ {1 / 2}.
$$

proof. by the mean value theorem, there is  $t \in [0,1]$  such that

$$
\begin{array}{l} \phi (v _ {1}) - \phi (v _ {0}) = \sum_ {i \in [ n ]} \left[ \nabla \phi (\mu \cdot w _ {t} ^ {- 1} \cdot \tau_ {t}) \right] _ {i} \cdot \mu \cdot \left(\frac {1}{[ w _ {t} ] _ {i}} \frac {d [ \tau_ {t} ] _ {i}}{d t} - \frac {[ \tau_ {t} ] _ {i}}{[ w _ {t} ] _ {i} ^ {2}} \frac {d [ w _ {t} ] _ {i}}{d t}\right) \\ = \boldsymbol {\mu} \cdot \nabla \phi (v _ {t}) ^ {\top} \mathbf {w} _ {t} ^ {- 1} \left(\frac {d \tau_ {t}}{d t} - \mathbf {t} _ {t} \mathbf {w} _ {t} ^ {- 1} \frac {d w _ {t}}{d t}\right). \\ \end{array}
$$

now,

$$
\mathbf {w} _ {t} ^ {- 1} \frac {d w _ {t}}{d t} = \mathbf {w} _ {t} ^ {- 1} \left[ \mathbf {x} _ {t} \delta_ {s} + \mathbf {s} _ {t} \delta_ {x} \right] = \mathbf {s} _ {t} ^ {- 1} \delta_ {s} + \mathbf {x} _ {t} ^ {- 1} \delta_ {x}
$$

and lemma 46 combined with chain rule implies that

$$
\frac {d \tau_ {t}}{d t} = 2 \boldsymbol {\lambda} _ {t} \left[ \mathbf {s} _ {t} ^ {- 1 / 2 - \alpha} \mathbf {x} _ {t} ^ {1 / 2 - \alpha} \right] ^ {- 1} \cdot \left[ \frac {d}{d t} \mathbf {s} _ {t} ^ {- 1 / 2 - \alpha} x _ {t} ^ {1 / 2 - \alpha} \right] = \boldsymbol {\lambda} _ {t} \left((1 - 2 \alpha) \mathbf {x} _ {t} ^ {- 1} \delta_ {x} - (1 + 2 \alpha) \mathbf {s} _ {t} ^ {- 1} \delta_ {s}\right).
$$

consequently,

$$
\mathbf {t} _ {t} \mathbf {w} _ {t} ^ {- 1} \frac {d w _ {t}}{d t} - \frac {d \tau_ {t}}{d t} = [ \mathbf {t} _ {t} + (1 + 2 \alpha) \boldsymbol {\lambda} _ {t} ] \mathbf {s} _ {t} ^ {- 1} \delta_ {s} + [ \mathbf {t} _ {t} - (1 - 2 \alpha) \boldsymbol {\lambda} _ {t} ] \mathbf {x} _ {t} ^ {- 1} \delta_ {x}.
$$

combining yields that

$$
\phi (v _ {1}) - \phi (v _ {0}) = - \nabla \phi (v _ {t}) ^ {\top} \left[ \mu \mathbf {w} _ {t} ^ {- 1} \mathbf {t} _ {t} \right] \left(\left[ \mathbf {i} + (1 + 2 \alpha) \mathbf {t} _ {t} ^ {- 1} \boldsymbol {\lambda} _ {t} \right] \mathbf {s} _ {t} ^ {- 1} \delta_ {s} + \left[ \mathbf {i} - (1 - 2 \alpha) \mathbf {t} _ {t} ^ {- 1} \boldsymbol {\lambda} _ {t} \right] \mathbf {x} _ {t} ^ {- 1} \delta_ {x}\right).
$$

the result then follows by the definition of  $\delta_s$  and  $\delta_x$ .

lemma 24. in the setting of lemma 23 for all  $y \in \mathbb{r}^n$ ,  $t \in [0,1]$ , and  $\beta \in [0,1/2]$  we have

$$
\| (\mathbf {t} _ {t} ^ {- 1} \boldsymbol {\lambda} _ {t} - \beta \mathbf {i}) y \| _ {\infty} \leq (1 - \beta) \| y \| _ {\infty} + \min \{\| y \| _ {\infty}, \| y \| _ {\tau_ {t}} \} a n d \| (\mathbf {t} _ {t} ^ {- 1} \boldsymbol {\lambda} _ {t} - \beta \mathbf {i}) y \| _ {\tau_ {t}} \leq (1 - \beta) \| y \| _ {\tau_ {t}}.
$$

consequently,  $\| \mathbf{t}_t^{-1}\pmb {\lambda}_t\|_{\tau_t + \infty}\leq 1 - \beta +(1 + c_{\mathrm{norm}})^{-1}$

proof. let  $\sigma_t \stackrel{\mathrm{def}}{=} \sigma(\mathbf{x}_t^{1/2 - \alpha} \mathbf{s}_t^{-1/2 - \alpha} \mathbf{a})$ ,  $\boldsymbol{\sigma}_t \stackrel{\mathrm{def}}{=} \mathbf{diag}(\sigma_t)$ , and  $\mathbf{p}_t^{(2)} \stackrel{\mathrm{def}}{=} \mathbf{p}^{(2)}(\mathbf{x}_t^{1/2 - \alpha} \mathbf{s}_t^{-1/2 - \alpha} \mathbf{a})$ . since  $\boldsymbol{\lambda}_t = \boldsymbol{\sigma}_t - \mathbf{p}_t^{(2)}$ , lemma 45 and  $\sigma_t \leq \tau_t$  implies that

$$
\begin{array}{l} \| (\mathbf {t} _ {t} ^ {- 1} \boldsymbol {\lambda} _ {t} - \beta \mathbf {i}) y \| _ {\infty} \leq \| (\mathbf {t} _ {t} ^ {- 1} \boldsymbol {\sigma} _ {t} - \beta \mathbf {i}) y \| _ {\infty} + \| \mathbf {t} _ {t} ^ {- 1} \mathbf {p} _ {t} ^ {(2)} y \| _ {\infty} \\ \leq \max  \left\{1 - \beta , \beta \right\} \| y \| _ {\infty} + \min  \left\{\| y \| _ {\infty}, \| y \| _ {\tau_ {t}} \right\}. \tag {5.1} \\ \end{array}
$$

on the other hand, we know that  $\mathbf{0} \preceq \boldsymbol{\lambda}_t \preceq \boldsymbol{\sigma}_t \preceq \mathbf{t}_t$  by lemma 45 and that  $\sigma_t \leq \tau_t$ . consequently,  $\mathbf{0} \preceq \mathbf{t}_t^{-1/2} \boldsymbol{\lambda}_t \mathbf{t}_t^{-1/2} \preceq \mathbf{i}$  and lemma 18 yields

$$
\left\| \mathbf {t} _ {t} ^ {- 1} \boldsymbol {\lambda} _ {t} - \beta \mathbf {i} \right\| _ {\tau_ {t}} \leq \left\| \mathbf {t} _ {t} ^ {- 1 / 2} \boldsymbol {\lambda} _ {t} \mathbf {t} _ {t} ^ {- 1 / 2} - \beta \mathbf {i} \right\| _ {2} \leq \max  \left\{1 - \beta , \beta \right\}. \tag {5.2}
$$

since for all  $c \in [0,1]$  we have  $\min \{\|y\|_{\infty}, \|y\|_{\tau_t}\} \leq c \|y\|_{\infty} + (1 - c) \|y\|_{\tau_t}$  and  $\max \{1 - \beta, \beta\} \leq 1 - \beta$  as  $\beta \leq 1/2$  combining (5.1) and (5.2) yields that for all  $c \in [0,1]$  it is the case that

$$
\left\| \left(\mathbf {t} _ {t} ^ {- 1} \boldsymbol {\lambda} _ {t} - \beta \mathbf {i}\right) y \right\| _ {\tau_ {t} + \infty} \leq (1 - \beta + c) \| y \| _ {\infty} + (1 - c + c _ {\mathrm {n o r m}} (1 - \beta)) \cdot \| y \| _ {\tau_ {t}}.
$$

since for  $c = 1 / (1 + c_{\mathrm{norm}})$  we have  $c_{\mathrm{norm}}c = 1 - c$  we have  $c_{\mathrm{norm}}(1 - \beta + c) = 1 - c + c_{\mathrm{norm}}(1 - \beta)$  and the result follows.

lemma 25. in the setting of lemma 23 we have that  $x_{t} \approx_{\frac{3}{2}\epsilon} x_{0}$  and  $s_{t} \approx_{\frac{3}{2}\epsilon} s_{0}$ . consequently,  $\tau_{t} \approx_{3\epsilon} \tau_{0}$  and for all  $y \in \mathbb{r}^{n}$  we have

$$
\| \overline {{\mathbf {w}}} ^ {- 1 / 2} \mathbf {q} \overline {{\mathbf {w}}} ^ {1 / 2} h \| _ {\tau_ {t}} \leq e ^ {1 0 \epsilon} \| h \| _ {\tau_ {t}} a n d \| \overline {{\mathbf {w}}} ^ {- 1 / 2} \mathbf {q} \overline {{\mathbf {w}}} ^ {1 / 2} h \| _ {\infty} \leq 2 e ^ {3 \epsilon} \| h \| _ {\tau_ {t}}.
$$

for all  $\beta \in [0,1 / 2]$  we have

$$
\| \overline {{\mathbf {w}}} ^ {- 1 / 2} \mathbf {q} \overline {{\mathbf {w}}} ^ {1 / 2} - \beta \mathbf {i} \| _ {\tau_ {t} + \infty} \leq \frac {2 e ^ {3 \epsilon}}{c _ {\mathrm {n o r m}}} + e ^ {6 \epsilon} (e ^ {1 6 \epsilon} - \beta) \leq 2.
$$

proof. by lemma 21,  $c_{\mathrm{norm}} \geq 3$ ,  $\alpha \leq 1/5$ , and  $\| h \|_{\tau + \infty} \leq \epsilon$  we have

$$
\| \mathbf {x} ^ {- 1} \delta_ {x} \| _ {\infty} \leq (1 + 2 \alpha) [ e ^ {\epsilon} \| h \| _ {\infty} + 3 \| h \| _ {\tau} ] \leq \frac {7}{5} \epsilon .
$$

consequently, lemma 47 and  $\epsilon \in (0,1 / 100)$  implies that  $x_{t}\approx_{\frac{3}{2}\epsilon}x_{0}$  (where we used that  $\frac{7}{5}\epsilon +(\frac{7}{5}\epsilon)^2\leq \frac{3}{2}\epsilon$ ). further the same reasoning implies that  $s_t\approx_{\frac{3}{2}\epsilon}s_0$ . consequently,  $\tau_t\approx_{2\cdot (3 / 2)\epsilon}\tau_0$ . as  $(x,s)$  is  $(\mu ,\epsilon)$ -centered we have, lemma 20,  $c_\mathrm{norm}\geq 10$ , and  $\epsilon \in (0,1 / 100)$  imply that for all  $h\in \mathbb{r}^n$

$$
\left\| \overline {{\mathbf {w}}} ^ {- 1 / 2} \mathbf {q} \overline {{\mathbf {w}}} ^ {1 / 2} h \right\| _ {\tau_ {t}} \leq e ^ {1 0 \epsilon} \| h \| _ {\tau_ {t}} \text {a n d} \left\| \overline {{\mathbf {w}}} ^ {- 1 / 2} \mathbf {q} \overline {{\mathbf {w}}} ^ {1 / 2} h \right\| _ {\infty} \leq 2 e ^ {3 \epsilon} \| h \| _ {\tau_ {t}}. \tag {5.3}
$$

next, since by assumptions  $w_0 \approx_{\epsilon} \mu \cdot \tau_0$  and  $\overline{w} \approx_{2\epsilon} w_0$  we have that  $\overline{w} \approx_{6\epsilon} \mu \cdot \tau_t$  and therefore the above implies

$$
\begin{array}{l} \| \overline {{\mathbf {w}}} ^ {- 1 / 2} \mathbf {q} \overline {{\mathbf {w}}} ^ {1 / 2} - \beta \mathbf {i} \| _ {\tau_ {t}} \leq e ^ {6 \epsilon} \| \overline {{\mathbf {w}}} ^ {- 1 / 2} \mathbf {q} \overline {{\mathbf {w}}} ^ {1 / 2} - \beta \mathbf {i} \| _ {\overline {{w}}} = e ^ {6 \epsilon} \| \mathbf {q} - \beta \mathbf {i} \| _ {2} \\ \leq e ^ {6 \epsilon} \max  \left\{\beta , \| \mathbf {q} \| _ {2} - \beta \right\} \leq e ^ {6 \epsilon} \max  \left\{\beta , e ^ {1 6 \epsilon} - \beta \right\} \tag {5.4} \\ \end{array}
$$

combining (5.3) and (5.4) and using that  $\beta \in [0,1 / 2]$  yields that for all  $h\in \mathbb{r}^n$

$$
\begin{array}{l} \| (\overline {{\mathbf {w}}} ^ {- 1 / 2} \mathbf {q} \overline {{\mathbf {w}}} ^ {1 / 2} - \beta \mathbf {i}) h \| _ {\tau_ {t} + \infty} \leq \beta \| h \| _ {\infty} + \left(2 e ^ {3 \epsilon} + c _ {\mathrm {n o r m}} e ^ {6 \epsilon} (e ^ {1 6 \epsilon} - \beta)\right) \| h \| _ {\tau_ {t}} \\ \leq \max \left\{\beta , \frac {2 e ^ {3 \epsilon}}{c _ {\mathrm {n o r m}}} + e ^ {6 \epsilon} (e ^ {1 6 \epsilon} - \beta) \right\} \| h \| _ {\tau_ {t} + \infty} \\ \end{array}
$$

and the result follows from the fact that  $\beta \in [0,1 / 2]$  and the bounds on  $\epsilon$  and  $c_{\mathrm{norm}}$ .

lemma 26. in the setting of lemma 23 there is a matrix  $\mathbf{e}_t$  with  $\| \mathbf{e}_t\|_{\tau_t + \infty}\leq 1000\epsilon$  such that

$$
\mathbf {j} _ {t} = \mathbf {i} + \left((1 - 4 \alpha^ {2}) \mathbf {t} _ {t} ^ {- 1} \boldsymbol {\lambda} _ {t} - 2 \alpha \mathbf {i}\right) (2 \overline {{\mathbf {w}}} ^ {- 1 / 2} \mathbf {q} \overline {{\mathbf {w}}} ^ {1 / 2} - \mathbf {i}) + \mathbf {e} _ {t}.
$$

proof. by lemma 25,  $x_{t} \approx_{\frac{3}{2}\epsilon} x_{0}$ ,  $s_{t} \approx_{\frac{3}{2}\epsilon} s_{0}$ , and  $\tau_{t} \approx_{3\epsilon} \tau_{0}$ . by definition of an  $\epsilon$ -newton direction this further implies that  $x_{t} \approx_{\frac{5}{2}\epsilon} \overline{x}$  and  $s_{t} \approx_{\frac{5}{2}\epsilon} \overline{s}$ . further, this implies that  $w_{t} \approx_{3\epsilon} w_{0}$  and since  $w_{0} \approx_{\epsilon} \mu \cdot \tau_{0}$  by definition of  $(\mu, \epsilon)$ -centered this implies that  $w_{t} \approx_{7\epsilon} \mu \cdot \tau_{t}$ . consequently, fact 18, lemma 47, and  $\epsilon < 1/80$  implies

$$
\| \mu \mathbf {w} _ {t} ^ {- 1} \mathbf {t} _ {t} - \mathbf {i} \| _ {\tau_ {t} + \infty} \leq 8 \epsilon , \| \mathbf {s} _ {t} ^ {- 1} \overline {{\mathbf {s}}} - \mathbf {i} \| _ {\tau_ {t} + \infty} \leq 3 \epsilon , \text {a n d} \| \mathbf {x} _ {t} ^ {- 1} \overline {{\mathbf {x}}} - \mathbf {i} \| _ {\tau_ {t} + \infty} \leq 3 \epsilon .
$$

further,  $\| \overline{\mathbf{w}}^{-1 / 2}\mathbf{q}\overline{\mathbf{w}}^{1 / 2}\|_{\tau_t + \infty}\leq 2$  by lemma 25 with  $\beta = 0$  and  $\| \mathbf{t}_t^{-1}\pmb {\lambda}_t\|_{\tau_t + \infty}\leq 2$  by lemma 24 with  $\beta = 0$ . combining these facts and applying lemma 18 we have that there are  $\mathbf{e}_t^s$  and  $\mathbf{e}_t^x$  with  $\| \mathbf{e}_t^s\|_{\tau_t + \infty}\leq 500\epsilon$  and  $\| \mathbf{e}_t^x\|_{\tau_t + \infty}\leq 500\epsilon$  with

$$
\begin{array}{l} \mathbf {j} _ {t} ^ {s} \stackrel {\mathrm {d e f}} {=} \left(\mathbf {i} + (1 + 2 \alpha) \mathbf {t} _ {t} ^ {- 1} \boldsymbol {\lambda} _ {t}\right) \overline {{\mathbf {w}}} ^ {- 1 / 2} \mathbf {q} \overline {{\mathbf {w}}} ^ {1 / 2} + \mathbf {e} _ {t} ^ {s} \\ \mathbf {j} _ {t} ^ {x} \stackrel {\mathrm {d e f}} {=} \left(\mathbf {i} - (1 - 2 \alpha) \mathbf {t} _ {t} ^ {- 1} \boldsymbol {\lambda} _ {t}\right) \left(\mathbf {i} - \overline {{\mathbf {w}}} ^ {- 1 / 2} \mathbf {q} \overline {{\mathbf {w}}} ^ {1 / 2}\right) + \mathbf {e} _ {t} ^ {x} \\ \end{array}
$$

letting  $\mathbf{e}_t \stackrel{\mathrm{def}}{=} (1 - 2\alpha)\mathbf{e}_t^s + (1 + 2\alpha)\mathbf{e}_t^x$  and leveraging the definitions of  $\mathbf{j}_t$  yields that

$$
\begin{array}{l} \mathbf {j} _ {t} = (1 - 2 \alpha) \mathbf {j} _ {t} ^ {s} + (1 + 2 \alpha) \mathbf {j} _ {t} ^ {x} + \mathbf {e} _ {t} \\ = (1 + 2 \alpha) \mathbf {i} - 4 \alpha \overline {{\mathbf {w}}} ^ {- 1 / 2} \mathbf {q} \overline {{\mathbf {w}}} ^ {1 / 2} - (1 - 4 \alpha^ {2}) \mathbf {t} _ {t} ^ {- 1} \mathbf {\lambda} _ {t} (\mathbf {i} - 2 \overline {{\mathbf {w}}} ^ {- 1 / 2} \mathbf {q} \overline {{\mathbf {w}}} ^ {1 / 2}) + \mathbf {e} _ {t}. \\ \end{array}
$$

lemma 27. in the setting of lemma 26 we have  $\| \mathbf{j}_t - \mathbf{e}_t - \mathbf{i}\|_{\tau_t + \infty}\leq 1 - \alpha$

proof. lemma 26 implies that for  $\beta = 2\alpha (1 - 4\alpha^{2})^{-1}$

$$
\mathbf {j} _ {t} - \mathbf {e} _ {t} - \mathbf {i} = (1 - 4 \alpha^ {2}) \left(\mathbf {t} _ {t} ^ {- 1} \boldsymbol {\lambda} _ {t} - \beta \mathbf {i}\right) (2 \overline {{\mathbf {w}}} ^ {- 1 / 2} \mathbf {q} \overline {{\mathbf {w}}} ^ {1 / 2} - \mathbf {i}).
$$

further, lemma 24 and  $\alpha \in (0,1 / 5)$  implies that  $\beta \in [0,1 / 2]$  and therefore

$$
\left\| \mathbf {t} _ {t} ^ {- 1} \boldsymbol {\lambda} _ {t} - \beta \mathbf {i} \right\| _ {\tau_ {t}} \leq 1 - \beta \leq 1 - 2 \alpha .
$$

further, lemma 25 implies that

$$
\begin{array}{l} \left\| 2 \overline {{\mathbf {w}}} ^ {- 1 / 2} \mathbf {q} \overline {{\mathbf {w}}} ^ {1 / 2} - \mathbf {i} \right\| _ {\tau_ {t} + \infty} \leq 2 \left\| \overline {{\mathbf {w}}} ^ {- 1 / 2} \mathbf {q} \overline {{\mathbf {w}}} ^ {1 / 2} - \frac {1}{2} \right\| _ {\tau_ {t} + \infty} \\ \leq 2 \left(\frac {2 e ^ {3 \epsilon}}{c _ {\mathrm {n o r m}}} + e ^ {6 \epsilon} \left(e ^ {1 6 \epsilon} - \frac {1}{2}\right)\right) \\ \end{array}
$$

combining, and using that  $\epsilon \leq \frac{\alpha}{4000}$ ,  $\alpha \in [0,1/5]$ , and  $c_{\mathrm{norm}} = 10/\alpha$  yields that

$$
\begin{array}{l} \| \mathbf {j} _ {t} - \mathbf {e} _ {t} - \mathbf {i} \| _ {\tau_ {t} + \infty} \leq (1 - 4 \alpha^ {2}) \cdot (1 - 2 \alpha) \cdot 2 \left(\frac {2 e ^ {3 \epsilon}}{c _ {\mathrm {n o r m}}} + e ^ {6 \epsilon} \left(e ^ {1 6 \epsilon} - \frac {1}{2}\right)\right) \\ \leq (1 - 2 \alpha) \cdot ((1 / 2) \alpha + (1 + 0. 0 0 2 \alpha) (2 +. 0 2 \alpha - 1) \leq 1 - \alpha . \\ \end{array}
$$

![](images/e4b65a37ebea01cf6f344c1f2b397df5058c8e61d1123d6b8099ea253dcd349b.jpg)

we now have everything we need to prove the main theorem.

proof of theorem 22. by lemma 23 we know that

$$
\phi (x + \delta_ {x}, s + \delta_ {s}, \mu) - \phi (x _ {0}, s _ {0}, \mu) = - \nabla \phi (v _ {t _ {*}}) ^ {\top} \mathbf {j} _ {t _ {*}} h
$$

where by lemma 26 and lemma 26 we know that for some matrix  $\mathbf{e}_{t_*}$  with  $\| \mathbf{e}_{t_*} \|_{\tau_{t_*} + \infty} \leq 1000\epsilon$  it holds that  $\| \mathbf{j}_{t_*} - \mathbf{e}_{t_*} - \mathbf{i} \|_{\tau_{t_*} + \infty} \leq 1 - \alpha$ . hence, we have  $\mathbf{j}_{t_*} h = h + e$  where

$$
\left\| e \right\| _ {\tau_ {t _ {*}} + \infty} \leq \left\| \left(\mathbf {j} _ {t _ {*}} - \mathbf {e} _ {t _ {*}} - \mathbf {i}\right) h \right\| _ {\tau_ {*} + \infty} + \left\| \mathbf {e} _ {t _ {*}} h \right\| _ {\tau_ {*} + \infty} \leq (1 - \alpha + 1 0 0 0 \epsilon) \| h \| _ {\tau_ {*} + \infty}. \tag {5.5}
$$

hence, we have

$$
\phi (x + \delta_ {x}, s + \delta_ {s}, \mu) - \phi (x _ {0}, s _ {0}, \mu) = - \nabla \phi (v _ {t _ {*}}) ^ {\top} (h + e).
$$

lemma 21 implies that  $x_{t} \approx_{\frac{3}{2}\epsilon} x_{0}$ ,  $s_{t} \approx_{\frac{3}{2}\epsilon} s_{0}$ , and  $\tau_{t} \approx_{3\epsilon} \tau_{0}$ . consequently,  $w_{t} \approx_{3\epsilon} w$  and  $v_{t_{*}} \approx_{6\epsilon} \mu \mathbf{w}^{-1} \tau$ . the definition of  $(\mu, \epsilon)$ -centered implies that  $\| \mu \mathbf{w}^{-1} \tau - 1 \|_{\infty} \leq \epsilon$  and therefore  $\| v_{t_{*}} - \mu \mathbf{w}^{-1} \tau \|_{\infty} \leq 10\epsilon$ . the bound on  $\| e \|_{\tau_{t_{*}} + \infty}$  follows from (5.5) and  $\| h \|_{\tau_{*} + \infty} \leq (1 + 5\epsilon) \| h \|_{\tau + \infty}$ .

# 5.3 following the central path

here we use the particular structure of  $\phi$  to analyze the effect of newton steps and changing  $\mu$  on centrality. first we state the following lemma about the potential function from [ls19]. then we use it to analyze the effect of centering for one step, lemma 31, and we conclude the section by proving a simplified variant of theorem 5.

lemma 28 (specialized restatement of lemma 61 of [ls19]). for all  $v \in \mathbb{r}^n$ , we have

$$
e ^ {\lambda \| v - 1 \| _ {\infty}} \leq \phi (v) \leq 2 n \cdot e ^ {\lambda \| v - 1 \| _ {\infty}} \quad a n d \quad \lambda \phi (v) - 2 \lambda n \leq \| \nabla \phi (v) \| _ {1} \tag {5.6}
$$

furthermore, let  $v^{\flat(\tau)} \stackrel{\mathrm{def}}{=} \operatorname{argmax}_{\| w \|_{\tau + \infty} \leq 1} \langle v, w \rangle$  and  $\| v \|_* = \max_{\| w \|_{\tau + \infty} \leq 1} \langle v, w \rangle$ . then, for all  $v, w \in \mathbb{r}^n$  with  $\| v - w \|_{\infty} \leq \delta \leq \frac{1}{5\lambda}$ , we have

$$
e ^ {- \lambda \delta} \| \nabla \phi (w) \| _ {*} - \lambda n \leq \left\langle \nabla \phi (v), \nabla \phi (w) ^ {b} \right\rangle \leq e ^ {\lambda \delta} \| \nabla \phi (w) \| _ {*} + \lambda e ^ {\lambda \delta} n \tag {5.7}
$$

and consequently,

$$
e ^ {- \lambda \delta} \| \nabla \phi (w) \| _ {*} - \lambda n \leq \| \nabla \phi (v) \| _ {*} \leq e ^ {\lambda \delta} \| \nabla \phi (w) \| _ {*} + \lambda e ^ {\lambda \delta} n. \tag {5.8}
$$

remark. when  $\tau$  is clear from context we also write  $v^{\flat}$  for  $v^{\flat(\tau)}$ .

here we provide a general lemma bounding how much  $\phi$  can increase for a step of bounded size in the mixed norm.

lemma 29 (potential increase). let  $v_{0}, v_{1} \in \mathbb{r}^{n}$  such that  $\| v_{1} - v_{0}\|_{\tau +\infty} \leq \delta \leq \frac{1}{5\lambda}$  for some  $\tau \in \mathbb{r}_{>0}^{n}$ . then for  $\| \cdot \|_{*}$ , the dual norm to  $\| \cdot \|_{\tau +\infty}$  we have  $\phi (v_{1}) \leq \phi (v_{0}) + \delta e^{\delta \lambda}(\| \nabla \phi (v_{0})\|_{*} + \lambda n)$ .

proof. for all  $t \in [0,1]$  let  $v_{t} \stackrel{\mathrm{def}}{=} v_{0} + t(v_{1} - v_{0})$ . note, that this implies that  $\| v_{t} - v_{0} \|_{\tau + \infty} \leq t \delta \leq \delta$  for all  $t \in [0,1]$  and therefore, integrating and applying cauchy schwarz for arbitrary norms (with  $\| \cdot \|_{*}$  as the dual norm of  $\| \cdot \|_{\tau + \infty}$ ) implies that

$$
\begin{array}{l} \phi (v _ {1}) - \phi (v _ {0}) = \int_ {0} ^ {1} \nabla \phi (v _ {t}) ^ {\top} (v _ {1} - v _ {0}) d t \leq \int_ {0} ^ {1} \| \nabla \phi (v _ {t}) \| _ {*} \| v _ {1} - v _ {0} \| d t \\ \leq \int_ {0} ^ {1} e ^ {\lambda \delta} \left(\| \nabla \phi (v _ {0}) \| _ {*} + \lambda n\right) \delta d t \\ \end{array}
$$

where in the second line we applied (5.8) of lemma 28 as  $t\delta \leq 1 / (5\lambda)$ .

lemma 30 (potential parameter stability). let  $(x_0,s)$  be a  $(\mu ,\epsilon)$ -centered point and for all  $t\in [0,1]$  let  $x_{t}\stackrel {\mathrm{def}}{=}x_{0} + t\delta_{x}$  where  $\| \mathbf{x}_0^{-1}\delta_x\|_{\tau_0 + \infty}\leq \epsilon \leq 1 / 100$  . if for all  $t\in [0,1]$  we let  $w_{t}\stackrel {\mathrm{def}}{=}\mathbf{x}_{ts}$ $\tau_t\stackrel {\mathrm{def}}{=}\tau_{\mathrm{reg}}(x_t,s)$  , and  $v_{t}\stackrel {\mathrm{def}}{=}\mu \mathbf{w}_{t}^{-1}\tau_{t}$  then  $\| v_{1} - v_{0}\|_{\tau_{0} + \infty}\leq 16\| \mathbf{x}_{0}^{-1}\delta_{x}\|_{\tau_{0} + \infty}$

proof. following the calculations in lemma 23 we have that for  $\mathbf{t}_t \stackrel{\mathrm{def}}{=} \mathbf{diag}(\tau_t)$

$$
v _ {1} - v _ {0} = \int_ {0} ^ {1} \mu \mathbf {w} _ {t} ^ {- 1} \left(\frac {d}{d t} \tau_ {t} - \mathbf {t} _ {t} \mathbf {w} _ {t} ^ {- 1} \frac {d}{d t} w _ {t}\right) d t
$$

and for  $\lambda_t \stackrel{\mathrm{def}}{=} \lambda(\mathbf{x}_t^{1/2 - \alpha} \mathbf{s}_t^{-1/2 - \alpha} \mathbf{a})$

$$
\mathbf {w} _ {t} ^ {- 1} \frac {d}{d t} = \mathbf {x} _ {t} ^ {- 1} \delta_ {x} \mathrm {a n d} \frac {d}{d t} \tau_ {t} = (1 - 2 \alpha) \boldsymbol {\lambda} _ {t} \mathbf {x} _ {t} ^ {- 1} \delta_ {x}.
$$

consequently,

$$
v _ {1} - v _ {0} = - \int_ {0} ^ {1} \mu \mathbf {w} _ {t} ^ {- 1} \mathbf {t} _ {t} ((1 - 2 \alpha) \mathbf {t} _ {t} ^ {- 1} \boldsymbol {\lambda} _ {t} - \mathbf {i}) \mathbf {x} _ {t} ^ {- 1} \delta_ {x} d t.
$$

further, since  $\| \mathbf{x}^{-1}\delta_x\|_{\infty}\leq \epsilon \leq 1 / (100)$  we have that

$$
\begin{array}{l} \| v _ {1} - v _ {0} \| _ {\tau_ {0} + \infty} \leq \int_ {0} ^ {1} \| \mu \mathbf {w} _ {t} ^ {- 1} \mathbf {t} _ {t} ((1 - 2 \alpha) \mathbf {t} _ {t} ^ {- 1} \boldsymbol {\lambda} _ {t} - \mathbf {i}) \mathbf {x} _ {t} ^ {- 1} \delta_ {x} \| _ {\tau_ {0} + \infty} d t \\ \leq 2 \int_ {0} ^ {1} \| \mu \mathbf {w} _ {t} ^ {- 1} \mathbf {t} _ {t} \| _ {\tau_ {t} + \infty} \| (1 - 2 \alpha) \mathbf {t} _ {t} ^ {- 1} \boldsymbol {\lambda} _ {t} - \mathbf {i} \| _ {\tau_ {t} + \infty} \| \mathbf {x} _ {t} ^ {- 1} \mathbf {x} _ {0} \| _ {\tau_ {t} + \infty} \| \mathbf {x} _ {0} ^ {- 1} \delta_ {x} \| _ {\tau_ {0} + \infty}. \\ \end{array}
$$

now, the proof of lemma 24 shows that  $\| (1 - 2\alpha)\mathbf{t}_t^{-1}\pmb {\lambda}_t - \mathbf{i}\|_{\tau_t + \infty}\leq 2$  and that  $\| \mathbf{x}^{-1}\delta_x\|_{\infty}\leq$ $\epsilon \leq 1 / 100$  and  $(x_0,s)$  is  $(\mu ,\epsilon)$ -centered shows that  $\| \mu \mathbf{w}_t^{-1}\mathbf{t}_t\|_{\tau_t + \infty}\leq 2$  and  $\| \mathbf{x}_t^{-1}\mathbf{x}_0\|_{\tau_t + \infty}\leq 2$  combining yields the result.

lemma 31. let  $(\delta_x, \delta_s)$  denote an  $\epsilon$ -newton direction from  $(\mu, \epsilon)$ -centered  $(x, s)$  with  $h = \gamma \nabla \phi(\overline{v})^{\flat(\overline{\tau})}$  for  $\overline{\tau} \approx_{\epsilon} \tau$  and  $\| \overline{v} - v \|_{\infty} \leq \gamma$  for  $v \stackrel{\mathrm{def}}{=} \mu \mathbf{w}^{-1} \tau_{\mathrm{reg}}(x, s)$  where  $w \stackrel{\mathrm{def}}{=} \mathbf{x} s$ ,  $\gamma \leq \min\{\epsilon, \frac{\alpha}{50\lambda}\}$ ,  $\epsilon \leq \frac{\alpha}{4000}$ , and  $\lambda \geq \log n$ . then

$$
\phi (x + \delta_ {x} + e _ {x}, s + \delta_ {s}, \mu + \delta_ {\mu}) \leq \left(1 - \frac {\gamma \alpha^ {2} \lambda}{6 4 0 \sqrt {d}}\right) \cdot \phi (x, s, \mu) + 4 \gamma \lambda n
$$

for any  $\delta_{\mu} \in \mathbb{r}$  with  $|\delta_{\mu}| \leq \frac{\gamma\alpha}{2^{15}\sqrt{d}}\mu$  and  $e_x \in \mathbb{r}^n$  with  $\| \mathbf{x}^{-1}e_x\|_{\tau + \infty} \leq \frac{\gamma\alpha}{2^{20}}$ .

proof. let  $\delta = \phi(x + \delta_x, s + \delta_s, \mu) - \phi(x, s, \mu)$ . by theorem 22, we have that for  $e, \tau, v' \in \mathbb{r}^n$  given by this theorem it is the case that

$$
\begin{array}{l} \delta = - \nabla \phi (v ^ {\prime}) ^ {\top} (h + e) \\ \leq - \gamma \left\langle \nabla \phi (\bar {v}) ^ {\flat (\bar {\tau})}, \nabla \phi (v ^ {\prime}) \right\rangle + \| \nabla \phi (v ^ {\prime}) \| _ {*} \| e \| _ {\tau + \infty} \\ \leq - \gamma e ^ {- \gamma} \left\langle \nabla \phi (\bar {v}) ^ {\flat (\tau)}, \nabla \phi (v ^ {\prime}) \right\rangle + \| \nabla \phi (v ^ {\prime}) \| _ {*} \| e \| _ {\tau + \infty} \\ \leq - \gamma e ^ {- 1 1 \lambda \gamma - \epsilon} \| \nabla \phi (v ^ {\prime}) \| _ {*} + \gamma \lambda n + \left(1 - \frac {\alpha}{2}\right) \gamma \| \nabla \phi (v ^ {\prime}) \| _ {*} \\ \end{array}
$$

where we used (5.7),  $\| \overline{v} - v' \|_{\infty} \leq \| \overline{v} - v \|_{\infty} + \| v - v' \|_{\infty} \leq 11\gamma$  and theorem 22. since  $\gamma \leq \frac{\alpha}{50\lambda}$  and  $\epsilon \leq \frac{\alpha}{4000}$ , (5.8) implies that

$$
\delta \leq - \gamma \frac {\alpha}{4} \| \nabla \phi (v ^ {\prime}) \| _ {*} + \gamma \lambda n \leq - \gamma \frac {\alpha}{8} \| \nabla \phi (v) \| _ {*} + 2 \gamma \lambda n. \tag {5.9}
$$

now, let  $x_{1} \stackrel{\mathrm{def}}{=} x + \delta_{x}$  and  $x_{2} \stackrel{\mathrm{def}}{=} x + \delta_{x} + e_{x}$ . further, define  $w_{1} \stackrel{\mathrm{def}}{=} \mathbf{x}_{1}(s + \delta_{s})$ ,  $w_{2} = \mathbf{x}_{2}(s + \delta_{s})$ ,  $\tau_{1} = \tau_{\mathrm{reg}}(x_{1}, s + \delta_{s})$ ,  $\tau_{2} = \tau_{\mathrm{reg}}(x_{2}, s + \delta_{s})$ ,  $v_{1} = \mu \mathbf{w}_{1}^{-1} \tau_{1}$ , and  $v_{2} = \mu \mathbf{w}_{2}^{-1} \tau_{2}$ . note, that  $\| \mathbf{x}_{1}^{-1} e_{x} \|_{\tau_{1} + \infty} \leq 2 \| \mathbf{x}^{-1} e_{x} \|_{\tau_{\mathrm{reg}}(x, s) + \infty} \leq 1 / 100$  and therefore  $\| v_{2} - v_{1} \|_{\tau_{1} + \infty} \leq 16 \| \mathbf{x}_{1}^{-1} e_{x} \|_{\tau_{1} + \infty}$  by lemma 30. this implies that,  $v_{3} \stackrel{\mathrm{def}}{=} (\mu + \delta_{\mu}) \mathbf{w}_{2}^{-1} \tau_{2}$  satisfies

$$
\begin{array}{l} \left\| v _ {3} - v _ {1} \right\| _ {\tau_ {1} + \infty} \leq \left\| v _ {3} - v _ {2} \right\| _ {\tau_ {1} + \infty} + \left\| v _ {2} - v _ {1} \right\| _ {\tau_ {1} + \infty} \\ \leq \frac {\delta_ {\mu}}{\mu} \| v _ {2} \| _ {\tau_ {1} + \infty} + 1 6 \| \mathbf {x} ^ {- 1} e _ {x} \| _ {\tau_ {1} + \infty}. \\ \end{array}
$$

further  $\| \tau_1\| _1 = 2d$  and  $\| v_{2}\|_{\infty}\leq 2$  imply that

$$
\begin{array}{l} \left\| v _ {2} \right\| _ {\tau_ {1} + \infty} \leq \left\| v _ {2} \right\| _ {\infty} + \sqrt {\sum_ {i \in [ n ]} \left[ v _ {2} \right] _ {i} ^ {2} \cdot \left[ \tau_ {1} \right] _ {i}} \\ \leq \left(1 + \sqrt {\| \tau_ {1} \| _ {1}}\right) \| v _ {2} \| _ {\infty} \leq 4 \sqrt {d}. \\ \end{array}
$$

using the bounds on  $\delta_{\mu}$  and  $\| \mathbf{x}^{-1}e_x\|_{\tau_1 + \infty}\leq \gamma \alpha 2^{-15}$  this implies that  $\| v_{3} - v_{1}\|_{\tau_{1} + \infty}\leq \gamma \alpha 2^{-10}$ . consequently, applying lemma 29 yields

$$
\begin{array}{l} \phi (x + \delta_ {x} + e _ {x}, s + \delta_ {s}, \mu + \delta_ {\mu}) - \phi (x + \delta_ {x}, s + \delta_ {s}, \mu) \leq \gamma \alpha 2 ^ {- 1 0} e ^ {\lambda \gamma \alpha 2 ^ {- 1 0}} (\| \nabla \phi (v _ {1}) \| _ {*} + \lambda n) \\ \leq \gamma \alpha 2 ^ {- 1 0} e ^ {\lambda \gamma \alpha 2 ^ {- 1 0}} \left(e ^ {1 0 \gamma \lambda} \left(\| \nabla \phi (v) \| _ {*} + \lambda n\right) + \lambda n\right) \\ \leq \gamma \alpha 2 ^ {- 8} \| \nabla \phi (v) \| _ {*} + \gamma \alpha 2 ^ {- 6} n. \\ \end{array}
$$

where in the second line we applied lemma 28 and  $\| v - v_{1} \|_{\infty} \leq 10\lambda$ . combining with (5.9) yields

$$
\phi (x + \delta_ {x} + e _ {x}, s + \delta_ {s}, \mu + \delta_ {\mu}) \geq \phi (x, s, \mu) - \frac {\gamma \alpha}{1 6} \| \nabla \phi (v) \| _ {*} + 3 \gamma \lambda n.
$$

finally, for any vector  $u$ , we have  $\| u \|_* \geq \left\langle u, \frac{\alpha \cdot \mathrm{sign}(u)}{40\sqrt{d}} \right\rangle = \frac{\alpha}{40\sqrt{d}} \| u \|_1$  as

$$
\begin{array}{l} \left\| \frac {\alpha \cdot \operatorname {s i g n} (u)}{4 0 \sqrt {d}} \right\| _ {\tau + \infty} \leq \frac {1}{4} + c _ {\text {n o r m}} \frac {\alpha}{4 0 \sqrt {d}} \left(\sum_ {i \in [ n ]} \tau_ {i}\right) ^ {1 / 2} \\ = \frac {1}{4} + c _ {\mathrm {n o r m}} \frac {\alpha \sqrt {2 d}}{4 0 \sqrt {d}} \leq 1. \\ \end{array}
$$

hence, by lemma 28 we have

$$
\begin{array}{l} \| \nabla \phi (v) \| _ {*} \geq \frac {\alpha}{4 0 \sqrt {d}} \| \nabla \phi (v) \| _ {1} \\ \geq \frac {\alpha \lambda}{4 0 \sqrt {d}} \left(\phi (v) - 2 n\right) \\ \end{array}
$$

and combining yields the desired result.

![](images/bf0081dfde0cfa8964fa4f051b0694dc57ff8e7c56daa1be433a349896d3a94a.jpg)

we now prove theorem 32, which is a simplified variant of theorem 5, where we do not claim that the result  $x^{(\mathrm{final})}$  is nearly feasible. for theorem 32, we analyze algorithm 1 under the assumption that it does not execute line 15 (i.e. the function maintainfeasibility is not called). we show in appendix b that this result can be extended to return a nearly feasible solution, i.e. how to extend theorem 32 to theorem 5 by calling line 15 of algorithm 1.

theorem 32. assume that the movement  $e_x$  of  $x$  induced by line 16 of algorithm 1 satisfies  $\| \mathbf{x}^{-1}e_x\|_{\tau +\infty}\leq \frac{\gamma\alpha}{2^{20}}$ . given  $x^{\mathrm{(init)}},s^{\mathrm{(init)}}\in \mathbb{r}_{>0}^n$ ,  $\mu^{\mathrm{(init)}} > 0$ ,  $\mu^{\mathrm{(target)}} > 0$ , and  $\epsilon \in (0,\alpha /16000)$  with  $x^{\mathrm{(init)}}s^{\mathrm{(init)}}\approx_{2\epsilon}\mu^{\mathrm{(init)}}\cdot \tau (x^{\mathrm{(init)}},s^{\mathrm{(init)}})$  algorithm 1 outputs  $(x^{\mathrm{(final)}},s^{\mathrm{(final)}})$  such that

$$
x ^ {(\mathrm {f i n a l})} s ^ {(\mathrm {f i n a l})} \approx_ {\epsilon} \mu^ {(\mathrm {t a r g e t})} \cdot \tau (x ^ {(\mathrm {f i n a l})}, s ^ {(\mathrm {f i n a l})})
$$

in

$$
o \left(\sqrt {d} \log (n) \cdot \left(\log \left(\frac {\mu^ {(\text {t a r g e t})}}{\mu^ {(\text {i n i t})}}\right) / (\epsilon \alpha) + \frac {1}{\alpha^ {3}}\right)\right)
$$

iterations.

furthermore, during the algorithm 1, we have

-  $xs \approx_{4\epsilon} \mu \cdot \tau(x, s)$  for some  $\mu$  where  $(x, s)$  is immediate points in the algorithms  
-  $\| \mathbf{x}^{-1}\delta_x\|_{\tau +\infty}\leq \frac{\epsilon}{2},\| \mathbf{s}^{-1}\delta_s\|_{\tau +\infty}\leq \frac{\epsilon}{2},$  and  $\| \mathrm{diag}(\tau)^{-1}\delta_{\tau}\|_{\tau +\infty}\leq 2\epsilon$  where  $\delta_{x},\delta_{s}$  and  $\delta_{\tau}$  is the change of  $x$  , s and  $\tau$  in one iteration and  $\| a\|_{\tau +\infty}\stackrel {\mathrm{def}}{=}\| x\|_{\infty} + c_{\mathrm{norm}}\| x\|_{\tau}$  for  $c_\mathrm{norm}\stackrel {\mathrm{def}}{=}\frac{10}{\alpha}$  (see section 5.1).

proof. initially, we have  $\phi(v) \leq 2n \cdot e^{3\epsilon \lambda}$  because of  $x^{(\mathrm{init})} s^{(\mathrm{init})} \approx_{2\epsilon} \mu^{(\mathrm{init})} \cdot \tau(x^{(\mathrm{init})}, s^{(\mathrm{init})})$  and (5.6) of lemma 28.

we proceed to show that in each iteration  $\phi(v) \leq \min\{2n \cdot e^{3\epsilon \lambda}, 2^{16}n\sqrt{d}\alpha^{-2}\}$ . note that, by lemma 28 in any iteration this holds, this implies that  $\|v - 1\|_{\infty} \leq \frac{1}{\lambda}\log(\phi(v))$ . since  $\lambda = \frac{2}{\epsilon}\log(2^{16}n\sqrt{d}/\alpha^2) \geq \frac{2}{\epsilon}\log(2n)$  this implies that  $\|v - 1\|_{\infty} \leq 3.5\epsilon$  which by the choice of  $\epsilon$  implies that  $x, s$  is  $(\mu, 4\epsilon)$  centered. further, lemma 31 and the design of the algorithm then imply that if  $\phi(v) \geq 2^{16}n\sqrt{d}\alpha^{-2}$  in this iteration, then

$$
\phi \left(x + \delta_ {x} + e _ {x}, s + \delta_ {s}, \mu + \delta_ {\mu}\right) \leq \left(1 - \frac {\gamma \alpha^ {2} \lambda}{1 2 8 0 \sqrt {d}}\right) \cdot \phi . \tag {5.10}
$$

consequently, by induction the claim holds for all iterations and  $xs \approx_{4\epsilon} \mu \cdot \tau_{\mathrm{reg}}(x, s)$  for all iterations as desired.

now recall that  $\alpha = 1 / (4\log (4n / d))\geq 1 / (2^{8}n)$  and consequently as  $d\leq n$  we have

$$
\begin{array}{l} \lambda = \frac {2}{\epsilon} \log (2 ^ {1 6} n \sqrt {d} / \alpha^ {2}) \\ \leq \frac {2}{\epsilon} \log \left(2 ^ {2 5} n ^ {4}\right) \\ \leq \frac {8}{\epsilon} \log (7 n) \\ \end{array}
$$

and

$$
\begin{array}{l} \gamma = \min  \left\{\frac {\epsilon}{4}, \frac {\alpha}{5 0 \lambda} \right\} \\ = \frac {\alpha}{5 0 \lambda} \\ \geq \frac {\alpha \epsilon}{4 0 0 \log (7 n)} . \\ \end{array}
$$

consequently, the reasoning in the preceding paragraph implies that in each step  $\mu$  is moved closer to  $\mu^{(\mathrm{target})}$  by a  $1 - \frac{\gamma\alpha}{2^{15}\sqrt{d}} = 1 - \omega \left(\frac{\epsilon\alpha}{\log(n)\sqrt{d}}\right)$  multiplicative factor. hence, it takes  $o\left(\frac{\sqrt{d}\log(n)}{\epsilon\alpha}\log \left(\frac{\mu^{(\mathrm{target})}}{\mu^{(\mathrm{init})}}\right)\right)$  iterations to arrive  $\mu^{(\mathrm{target})}$ . further, (5.10) shows that it takes  $o(\sqrt{d}\log (n) / \alpha^3)$  iterations to decrease  $\phi$  to  $2^{16}n\sqrt{d} /\alpha^2$ . hence, in total, it takes

$$
o \left(\sqrt {d} \log (n) \cdot \left(\frac {1}{\epsilon \alpha} \cdot \log \left(\frac {\mu^ {(\text {t a r g e t})}}{\mu^ {(\text {i n i t})}}\right) + \frac {1}{\alpha^ {3}}\right)\right)
$$

iterations for the algorithm to terminate. further, when the algorithm terminates, we have that  $x^{(\mathrm{final})} s^{(\mathrm{final})} \approx_{\epsilon} \mu^{(\mathrm{target})} \cdot \tau(x^{(\mathrm{final})}, s^{(\mathrm{final})})$  because  $\phi \leq 2^{16} n \sqrt{d} \alpha^{-2}$ ,  $\lambda = 2 \epsilon^{-1} \log(2^{16} n \sqrt{d} \alpha^{-2})$  and (5.6).

finally, the bounds on the movement of  $x$ ,  $s$ , follow from lemma 21, the condition  $\| \mathbf{x}^{-1}e_x\|_{\tau +\infty}\leq \frac{\gamma\alpha}{2^{20}}$ , and the choice of  $\gamma$ . the movement of  $\tau$  follows from lemma 14 in [ls15].

# 6 vector maintenance

in this section we prove theorem 8 (presented in section 4.2 and restated below) by providing and analyzing our vector maintenance data-structure. recall that vector maintenance asks to maintain an approximation of the vector  $y^{(t + 1)} \coloneqq \sum_{k\in [t]} \mathbf{g}^{(k)} \mathbf{a} h^{(k)} + \delta^{(k)}$  for an online sequence of diagonal matrices  $\mathbf{g}^{(1)}, \mathbf{g}^{(2)}, \dots, \mathbf{g}^{(t)} \in \mathbb{r}^{n\times n}$  and vectors  $h^{(1)}, \dots, h^{(t)} \in \mathbb{r}^d$  and  $\delta^{(1)}, \dots, \delta^{(t)} \in \mathbb{r}^n$ . this data-structure is used in section 4 to maintain approximately feasible points.

theorem 8 (vector maintenance). there exists a monte-carlo data-structure (algorithm 6), that works against an adaptive adversary, with the following procedures:

- initializer(a,  $g, x^{(0)}, \epsilon$ ): given matrix  $\mathbf{a} \in \mathbb{r}^{n \times d}$ , scaling  $g \in \mathbb{r}^n$ , initial vector  $x^{(0)}$ , and target accuracy  $\epsilon \in (0, 1/10)$ , the data-structure preprocesses in  $o(\mathrm{nmz}(\mathbf{a}) \log^5 n)$  time.  
-  $\text{scale}(i, u)$ : given  $i \in [n]$  and  $u \in \mathbb{r}$  sets  $g_i = u$  in  $o(d\log^5 n)$  amortized time.  
-  $query(h^{(t)},\delta^{(t)})$ : let  $g^{(t)} \in \mathbb{r}^n$  be the scale vector  $g \in \mathbb{r}^n$  during  $t$ -th call to  $query$  and let  $h^{(t)} \in \mathbb{r}^d$ ,  $\delta^{(t)} \in \mathbb{r}^n$  be the vectors given during that query. define

$$
x ^ {(t)} = x ^ {(0)} + \sum_ {k \in [ t ]} \mathbf {g} ^ {(k)} \mathbf {a} h ^ {(k)} + \sum_ {k \in [ t ]} \delta^ {(k)}.
$$

then, w.h.p. in  $n$  the data-structure outputs a vector  $y \in \mathbb{r}^n$  such that  $y \approx_{\epsilon} x^{(t)}$ . furthermore, the total cost over  $t$  steps is

$$
o \left(t \left(n \log n + \sum_ {k \in [ t ]} \left(\left\| (\mathbf {x} ^ {(k)}) ^ {- 1} \mathbf {g} ^ {(k)} \mathbf {a} h ^ {(k)} \right\| _ {2} ^ {2} + \left\| (\mathbf {x} ^ {(k)}) ^ {- 1} \delta^ {(k)} \right\| _ {2} ^ {2}\right) \cdot \varepsilon^ {- 2} \cdot d \log^ {6} n\right)\right).
$$

- computeexact(i): output  $x_{i}^{(t)} \in \mathbb{r}^{n}$  exactly in amortized time  $o(d\log n)$ .

we split the proof of theorem 8 into three parts. first, in section 6.1, we show how to use sparse-recovery algorithms to build a data-structure for quickly computing the large entries (i.e.  $\ell_2$ -heavy hitters) of the matrix-vector product  $\mathbf{a}h$ . second, in section 6.2, we show how to use the data-structure of section 6.1 to obtain a new data-structure that can quickly approximate the partial sum  $\sum_{k = t_0}^{t_1}\mathbf{g}^{(k)}\mathbf{a}h^{(k)}$ , in time roughly proportional to  $d\cdot \| \sum_{k = t_0}^{t_1}\mathbf{g}^{(k)}\mathbf{a}h^{(k)}\| _2^2$ . the naive way of computing an approximation of  $y^{(t)}$ , by using this data-structure for  $t_0 = 1$  and  $t_1 = t$ , would be prohibitively slow and therefore, in the last section 6.3, we obtain the main result theorem 8 by instead splitting the sum  $\sum_{k = 1}^{t}\mathbf{g}^{(k)}\mathbf{a}h^{(k)}$  into roughly  $o(\log n)$  partial sums, each of which is approximated efficiently by the data-structure of section 6.2.

# 6.1  $\ell_2$ -heavy hitter

here we show how to preprocess any matrix  $\mathbf{a}$ , such that we can build a data structure which supports quickly computing the large entries of the product  $\mathbf{g} \mathbf{a} h$  for changing diagonal  $\mathbf{g}$ .

lemma 33 (approximate matrix-vector product). there exists a monte-carlo data-structure (algorithm 4), that works against an adaptive adversary, with the following procedures:

- initializer(a, g): given a matrix  $\mathbf{a} \in \mathbb{r}^{n \times d}$  and a scaling  $g \in \mathbb{r}^n$ , the data-structure prepro-processes in  $o(\mathrm{nmz}(\mathbf{a}) \log^4 n)$  time.

algorithm 4: approximate matrix-vector product (lemma 33)  
1 members   
2  $\mathbf{a}\in \mathbb{r}^{n\times d},g\in \mathbb{r}^n$  // input matrix a and row scaling g   
3  $\phi_j = \mathrm{sketch}(2^{-j},n)\in \mathbb{r}^{m_j\times n}$  for all  $j\in [\log_2(n) / 2]$  // sketching matrices   
4  $\mathbf{m}_j\in \mathbb{r}^{m_j\times d}$  for all  $j\in [\log_2(n) / 2]$  // sketched input matrix  $\mathbf{m}_j = \phi_j\mathbf{g}\mathbf{a}$    
5  $\mathbf{r} = \mathrm{jl}(1 / 100,n)\in \mathbb{r}^{k\times n}$  with  $k = o(\log n)$  //  $(1\pm \frac{1}{100})$  -approximate jl-matrix   
6  $\mathbf{j}\in \mathbb{r}^{k\times d}$  // sketched input matrix j = rga   
7 procedure initialize(a  $\in \mathbb{r}^{n\times d},g\in \mathbb{r}^n$    
8  $\phi_j\gets \mathrm{sketch}(2^{-j},n)$  for all  $j\in [\log_2(n) / 2]$  // lemma 34   
9  $\mathbf{r}\leftarrow \mathrm{jl}(1 / 100,n)$  // lemma 35   
10  $\mathbf{m}_j\gets \phi_j\mathbf{g}\mathbf{a}$  for  $j\in [\log_2(n) / 2]$    
11 j  $\leftarrow$  rga, a  $\leftarrow$  a,  $g\gets g$    
12 procedure scale(i  $\in [n],u\in \mathbb{r}$    
13 mj  $\leftarrow$  mj + (u - gi)φj1i1a for j  $\in [\log_2(n) / 2]$    
14 j  $\leftarrow$  j + (u - gi)reiea   
15 gi  $\leftarrow u$    
16 procedure query(h  $\in \mathbb{r}^d,\epsilon \in (0,1))$    
17 r  $\leftarrow \| \mathbf{jh}\| _2,j\gets 1 + [\log_2(r / \epsilon)]$  ; // estimate  $\| \mathbf{gah}\| _2$  and which mj to consider   
18 if  $j\geq \log_2(n) / 2$  then return gah;   
19 // otherwise compute ah approximately using mj
