20 compute the list  $l\subset [n]$  of possible heavy hitters of gah from mjh via lemma 34   
21 for i  $\in l$  do   
22 if (gah)i  $\geq \varepsilon$  then vi  $\leftarrow$  (gah)i;   
23 return v

- query(h,ε): given a vector  $h \in \mathbb{r}^d$ , w.h.p. in  $n$ , the data-structure outputs the vector  $v \in \mathbb{r}^n$  such that

$$
v _ {i} = \left\{ \begin{array}{l l} (\mathbf {g a h}) _ {i}, & i f | (\mathbf {g a h}) _ {i} | \geq \varepsilon \\ 0, & o t h e r w i s e \end{array} \right.
$$

for  $\mathbf{g} = \mathbf{diag}(g)$  in time  $o(\| \mathbf{g}\mathbf{a}h\|^{2}\cdot \varepsilon^{-2}\cdot d\log^{3}n)$ .

-  $\operatorname{scale}(i, u)$ : sets  $g_i \gets u$  in  $o(d\log^4 n)$  time.

the proof of lemma 33 is based on  $\ell_2$ -heavy hitter sketches. while there exist many results for sketching and computing heavy hitters with different trade-offs [cm04, nnw14, ccfc02, knpw11, pag13, ch09, lnnt16], we only require the following lemma 34 adapted from [knpw11, pag13].

lemma 34 ( $\ell_2$ -heavy hitter, [knpw11, pag13]). there exists a function  $\mathrm{sketch}(\epsilon, n)$  that given  $\epsilon > 0$  explicitly returns a matrix  $\phi \in \mathbb{r}^{m \times n}$  with  $m = o(\varepsilon^{-2} \log^2 n)$  and column sparsity  $c = o(\log^2 n)$  in  $o(nc + m\mathrm{poly}\log n)$  time, and uses  $o(nc)$  spaces to store the matrix  $\phi$ . there further exists a function  $\mathrm{decode}(y)$  that given a vector  $y = \phi x$  in time  $o(\varepsilon^{-2} \log^2 n)$  reports a list  $l \subset [n]$

of size  $o(\epsilon^{-2})$  that with probability at least 9/10 includes all  $i$  with

$$
\left| x _ {i} \right| \geq \epsilon \min  _ {\| y \| _ {0} \leq \epsilon^ {- 2}} \| y - x \| _ {2}
$$

johnson-lindenstrauss lemma is a famous result about low-distortion embeddings of points from high-dimensional into low-dimension eculidean space. it was named after william b. johnson and joram lindenstrauss [jl84]. the most classical matrix that gives the property is random gaussian matrix, it is known that several other matrices also suffice to show the guarantees, e.g. fastjl[ac06], sparsejl[kn14], count-sketch matrix [ccfc02, tz12], subsampled randomized hadamard/fourier transform [ldfu13].

lemma 35 (johnson-lindenstrauss (jl) [jl84]). there exists a function  $jl(\epsilon, n)$  that given  $\epsilon > 0$  returns a matrix  $\mathbf{j} \in \mathbb{r}^{m \times n}$  with  $m = o(\epsilon^{-2} \log n)$  in  $o(mn)$  time. for any  $v \in \mathbb{r}^n$  this matrix  $\mathbf{j}$  satisfies with high probability in  $n$  that  $\| \mathbf{j}v \|_2 \approx_{\epsilon} \| v \|_2$ .

it is worth to mention that jl lemma is tight up to some constant factor, i.e., there exists a set of points of size  $n$  that needs dimension  $\omega(\epsilon^{-2}\log n)$  in order to preserve the distances between all pairs of points [ln17].

our proof of lemma 33 is through the analysis of algorithm 4 which works as follows. the data-structure creates  $\log_2n$  sketching matrices  $\phi_1,\ldots ,\phi_{\log_2n}$  according to lemma 34, where each  $\phi_j$  is constructed for  $\varepsilon = 2^{-j}$ . the data-structure then maintains the matrices  $\mathbf{m}_j = \phi_j\mathbf{g}\mathbf{a}$ , whenever  $\mathbf{g}$  is changed by calling scale. whenever  $\mathrm{query}(h,\varepsilon)$  is called, the data-structure estimates  $\| \mathbf{g}\mathbf{a}h\| _2$  via a johnson-lindenstrass matrix, and then pick the smallest  $j$ , for which  $\mathbf{m}_jh = \phi_j\mathbf{g}\mathbf{a}h$  is guaranteed to find all  $i$  with  $|(g\mathbf{a}h)_i| > \varepsilon$ , i.e. where  $\phi_j$  was initialized for  $\varepsilon' = 2^{-j}\approx \| \mathbf{g}\mathbf{a}h\| _2$ . to make sure that the algorithm works against an adaptive adversary, we compute these entries  $(\mathbf{g}\mathbf{a}h)_i$  exactly and output only those entries whose absolute value is at least  $\varepsilon$  (as we might also detect a few  $i$  for which the entry is smaller).

we now give a formal proof, that this data-structure is correct.

proof of lemma 33. failure probability: here we prove algorithm 4 succeeds with constant probability. note that lemma 33 states that the result holds w.h.p. in  $n$ , i.e. probability  $1 - n^{-c}$  for any constant  $c > 0$ . to achieve this better probability bound, one can run  $o(c\log n) = o(\log n)$  copies of this algorithm in parallel. this also means that for each query we obtain many vectors  $v_{1},\ldots ,v_{\ell}$  for  $\ell = o(\log n)$ . we combine them into a single vector by taking the union, i.e.  $v_{i} = (\mathbf{g}\mathbf{a}h)_{i}$  if there is some  $j$  with  $(v_{j})_{i}\neq 0$ . this increases the time complexities for preprocessing, query and scaling increase by an  $o(\log n)$  factor. consequently, in what follows we show that the runtimes of the algorithm are an  $o(\log n)$  factor smaller than the runtime stated in lemma33.

initialize: we use lemma 34 to create  $\phi_j \in \mathbb{r}^{m_j \times n}$  for each  $j \in [\log_2(n)/2]$  in  $\widetilde{\mathrm{o}}(\sqrt{n})$  time by lemma 34 as the smallest epsilon is  $2^{-\log_2 n/2} = \omega(1/\sqrt{n})$ . these matrices are then multiplied with  $\mathbf{g}\mathbf{a}$  to obtain  $\phi_j \mathbf{g}\mathbf{a}$ . computing each of these products can be implemented in  $o(\mathrm{nnz}(\mathbf{a}) \cdot \log^2 n)$  time as each column of  $\phi_j$  has only  $o(\log^2 n)$  non-zero elements. since  $\mathrm{nnz}(\mathbf{a}) \geq n$ , as otherwise there would be some empty rows in  $\mathbf{a}$  that could be removed, the initialization takes  $o(\mathrm{nnz}(\mathbf{a}) \log^3 n)$  time.

queries: for query vector  $h \in \mathbb{r}^d$  we want to find  $v \in \mathbb{r}^n$  with  $v_i = (\mathbf{g}\mathbf{a}h)_i$  for all  $i$  where  $|(\mathbf{g}\mathbf{a}h)_i| \geq \varepsilon$  and  $v_i = 0$  otherwise. by lemma 35,  $r = \| \mathbf{j}h\|_2$  is a 2-approximation of  $\| \mathbf{g}\mathbf{a}h\|_2$ .

now, if  $r / \epsilon \geq \sqrt{n}$  then  $\| \mathbf{g}\mathbf{a}h\| _2 / \epsilon \geq \sqrt{n} /2$  and  $o(nd)$  time is within the  $o(\| \mathbf{g}\mathbf{a}h\| ^2\cdot \varepsilon^{-2}\cdot d\log^3 n)$  time-budget for the query. hence, the algorithm simply outputs the vector by direct calculations, which takes  $o(nd)$  time.

otherwise, the algorithm uses lemma 34 to find a list of indices  $l$  which contains all  $i$  such that  $|(\mathbf{g}\mathbf{a}h)_i| \geq 2^{-j} \| \mathbf{g}\mathbf{a}h \|_2$  with probability at least  $9/10$ . since  $j = 1 + \lceil \log_2(r / \epsilon) \rceil$  and  $r \leq 2 \| \mathbf{g}\mathbf{a}h \|_2$ , the heavy hitters algorithm outputs all  $i$  such that  $|(\mathbf{g}\mathbf{a}h)_i| \geq \epsilon$ . hence, the vector  $v$  that we output is correct.

to bound the runtime, note that we find the list  $l$  by first computing  $y := \phi_j \mathbf{g} \mathbf{a} h = \mathbf{m}_j h$  in  $o(m_j d) = o((4^j \log^2 n)d)$  time. then, we use lemma 34 to decode the list in time  $o(4^j \log^2 n)$  time. hence, in total, it takes  $o(\| \mathbf{g} \mathbf{a} h \|_2^2 \varepsilon^{-2} \cdot d \log^2 n)$ .

scaling: when we set  $g_i = u$ , i.e. by adding  $u - g_i$  to entry  $g_i$ , then the product  $\phi_j \mathbf{g} \mathbf{a}$  changes by adding the outer product  $((u - g_i) \cdot \phi_j 1_i)(1_i^\top \mathbf{g} \mathbf{a})$ . this outer product can be computed in  $o(d \log^2 n)$  time, because each column of  $\phi_j$  has only  $o(\log^2 n)$  non-zero entries. since there are  $o(\log n)$  many  $j$ , the total time is  $o(d \log^3 n)$ .

# 6.2 maintaining accumulated sum

here we extend lemma 33 to approximately maintain the sum of products  $\sum_{k\in [t]}\mathbf{g}^{(k)}\mathbf{a}h^{(k)}$ . note that when the  $g^{(k)}$  were all the same, i.e. equal to some vector  $g$ , then it suffices to maintain  $\mathbf{ga}\sum_{k\in [t]}h^{(k)}$ , i.e., a single matrix-vector product, and this can be approximated in  $\ell_{\infty}$  norm using lemma 33. with this in mind, we split the task of approximating  $\sum_{k\in [t]}\mathbf{g}^{(k)}\mathbf{a}h^{(k)}$  into two parts, corresponding to the rows of  $g$  which have changed and those which have not. our algorithm for this problem, algorithm 5, stores  $\mathbf{f}$ , a diagonal 0-1 matrix where  $\mathbf{f}_{ii} = 1$  indicates that  $g_i^{(k)} \neq g_i^{(k')} \text{ for some } k,k'$ . the algorithm then then approximates  $(\mathbf{i} - \mathbf{f})\sum_{k=1}^{t}\mathbf{g}^{(k)}\mathbf{a}h^{(k)} = (\mathbf{i} - \mathbf{f})\mathbf{g}^{(t)}\mathbf{a}\sum_{k=1}^{t}h^{(k)}$  and  $\mathbf{f}\sum_{k=1}^{t}\mathbf{g}^{(k)}\mathbf{a}h^{(k)}$  in two different ways. from line 28 to 29, the second term is maintained directly. this is efficient because there are not too many rows that are rescaled. from line 30 to 32, the algorithm then approximates the first term using lemma 33.

lemma 36 (maintain accumulated matrix-vector product). there exists a monte-carlo data-structure (algorithm 5), that works against an adaptive adversary, with the following procedures:

- initializer(a,g): given a matrix  $\mathbf{a} \in \mathbb{r}^{n \times d}$  and a scaling  $g \in \mathbb{r}^n$ , the data-structure pre-processes in  $o(\mathrm{nnz}(a)\log^4 n)$  amortized time.  
- update(h): the data-structure processes the vector  $h \in \mathbb{r}^d$  in  $o(d)$  amortized time.  
-  $\text{scale}(i, u)$ : sets  $g_i = u$  in  $o(d\log^4 n)$  amortized time.  
- markexact(i): marks the  $i$ -th row to be calculated exactly in the next call to query in  $o(d \log^4 n)$  amortized time.  
-  $query(\epsilon)$ : let  $t$  be the number of updates before the last query,  $h^{(k)} \in \mathbb{r}^d$  and  $g^{(k)} \in \mathbb{r}^n$  are the corresponding vectors and scalings during update number  $k$ . (that means  $h^{(1)}, \ldots, h^{(t)}$ ,  $g^{(1)}, \ldots, g^{(t)}$  are the  $t$  vectors and scalings since the last call to  $query$ .) w.h.p. in  $n$  the data-structure outputs the vector  $v$  where  $v_i = (\sum_{k \in [t]} \mathbf{g}^{(k)} \mathbf{a} h^{(k)})_i$  if  $|\sum_{k \in [t]} [\mathbf{g}^{(k)} \mathbf{a} h^{(k)}]_i| \geq \epsilon$ ,  $i$  is set to be exact or  $g_i$  was rescaled between this and the previous call to  $query$ ; otherwise  $v_i = 0$ . let  $\mathbf{f}$  be the 0-1-matrix with  $\mathbf{f}_{i,i} = 1$  when  $i$  is marked to be exact or  $g_i$  was rescaled

between this and the previous call to query, then the call to query can be implemented in amortized time

$$
o \left(\left\| (\mathbf {i} - \mathbf {f}) \sum_ {k \in [ t ]} \mathbf {g} ^ {(k)} \mathbf {a} h ^ {(k)} \right\| _ {2} ^ {2} \cdot \varepsilon^ {- 2} \cdot d \log^ {3} n\right).
$$

- computeexact(i): let  $w = \sum_{k \in [t]} \mathbf{g}^{(k)} \mathbf{a} h^{(k)}$  be the vector approximated by the last call to query. then computeexact(i) returns  $w_i$  in  $o(d)$  amortized time.

proof. complexity: the time complexity for the initializer procedure follows from lemma 33. the time complexity for update is clearly  $o(d)$ . we note that the amortized time for scale is  $\tilde{o}(d)$  instead of  $o(1)$  because every call of scale will introduce two calls to  $d$ . scale in the calls to query. the same holds for markexact.

the cost of computeexact(i) is  $o(d + sd)$ , where  $s$  is the number of times  $\mathrm{scale}(i, \cdot)$  was called for that specific  $i$ , between the past two calls to query. this is because  $\bar{\delta}^{(k)}$  is set to  $\delta^{(k)}$  in the last call to query (line 27), and the number of  $k$  for which  $\delta_i^{(k)} \neq 0$  is the number of times  $\mathrm{scale}(i, \cdot)$  was called, because of line 18. hence the number of iterations of the loop in line 23 is bounded by  $s$ . without loss of generality, we can assume that computeexact(i) is called only once between two calls to query as we could modify the data-structure to save the result and return this saved result during the second call to computeexact(i). thus we can charge the  $o(sd)$  cost of computeexact to scale instead. in summary,  $\mathrm{scale}$  has amortized cost  $\widetilde{\mathrm{o}}(d)$  and computeexact has amortized cost  $o(d)$ .

finally, the cost of query mainly consists of the following: (1) computation of sums in line 28, (2) the calculation on indices in  $f$  (i.e. calling computeexact), (3) the call of  $d$ .query, and finally (4) the calls to  $d$ .scale.

the cost of (1) is bounded by  $o(td)$ , which we charge as amortized cost to update. the cost of (2) is bounded by  $o(|f|d)$ , which we charge to the amortized cost of scale and markexact. the cost of (3) is exactly the runtime we claimed due to lemma 33 since we zero out some of the rows using scale (line 30) which causes the  $(\mathbf{i} - \mathbf{f})$  term in the complexity.. the cost of (4) is bounded by  $o(|f|)$  times the cost of  $d$ .scale. we charge this cost to the scale and markexact procedure of the current data structure.

correctness: we now prove that the output of query is correct. let  $v$  be the output of the algorithm. the algorithm query consists of two parts. from line 28 to 29, we calculate  $v$  on indices in  $f$ . from line 30 to 32, we calculate  $v$  on indices in  $f^c$ .

for any  $i \notin f$ , we note that  $g_i$  is never changed. hence, we have

$$
\sum_ {k \in [ t ]} (\mathbf {g} ^ {(k)} \mathbf {a} h ^ {(k)}) _ {i} = \left(\mathbf {g} ^ {(k)} \mathbf {a} \sum_ {k \in [ t ]} h ^ {(k)}\right) _ {i}
$$

and lemma 33 yields that we correctly calculate  $v_{i}$  for  $i \notin f$ . now, we prove the correctness of  $v_{i}$  for  $i \in f$  (i.e. the correctness of computeexact). for notational convenience, let  $\mathbf{g}^{(0)} \stackrel{\mathrm{def}}{=} \mathbf{g}^{(\mathrm{old})}$ , and note that

$$
\left(\sum_ {k \in [ t ]} {\bf g} ^ {(k)} {\bf a} h ^ {(k)}\right) _ {i} = \underbrace {\left(\sum_ {k \in [ t ]} ({\bf g} ^ {(k)} - {\bf g} ^ {(0)}) {\bf a} h ^ {(k)}\right) _ {i}} _ {\mathrm {c o m p u t e d b y l i n e 2 3}} + \underbrace {\left({\bf g} ^ {(0)} {\bf a} \sum_ {k \in [ t ]} h ^ {(k)}\right) _ {i}} _ {\mathrm {c o m p u t e d b y l i n e 2 2}}.
$$

it is easy to see that line 22 computes the last term, as  $\bar{g}^{(\mathrm{old})} = g^{(\mathrm{old})} = g^{(1)}$ . the more difficult part is proving that the first term is computed by line 23. however, note that by design of  $\bar{\delta}_k$ ,  $\delta_k$  we have

$$
[ g ^ {(k)} ] _ {i} - [ g ^ {(0)} ] _ {i} = \sum_ {j \in [ k ]} [ g ^ {(j)} ] _ {i} - [ g ^ {(j - 1)} ] _ {i} = \sum_ {j \in [ k ]} \bar {\delta} _ {j}
$$

and therefore we can re-write the  $i$ -th entry of  $\sum_{k \in [t]} \left( \mathbf{g}^{(k)} - \mathbf{g}^{(0)} \right) \mathbf{a} h^{(k)}$  as

$$
1 _ {i} ^ {\top} \sum_ {k \in [ t ]} \left(\mathbf {g} ^ {(k)} - \mathbf {g} ^ {(0)}\right) \mathbf {a} h ^ {(k)} = \sum_ {k \in [ t ]} \sum_ {j \in [ k ]} \bar {\delta} _ {j} 1 _ {i} ^ {\top} \mathbf {a} h ^ {(k)} = \sum_ {j \in [ t ]} \bar {\delta} _ {j} 1 _ {i} ^ {\top} \mathbf {a} \left[ \sum_ {k = j} ^ {t} h ^ {(k)} \right]
$$

by the definition of  $\bar{h}^{(k)}$  this is precisely what is computed in line 23.

in summary, computeexact(i) computes  $(\sum_{k=1}^{t} \mathbf{g}^{(k)} \mathbf{a} h^{(k)})_i$  and the vector  $v$  computed by query is indeed  $\sum_{k=1}^{t} \mathbf{g}^{(k)} \mathbf{a} h^{(k)}$ . as the variables  $\bar{g}^{(\mathrm{old})}$ ,  $\bar{\delta}^{(k)}$ ,  $\bar{h}^{(k)}$  are only changed during query, the function computeexact(i) computes the  $i$ -th entry of the vector approximated by the last call to query.

# 6.3 vector maintenance algorithm and analysis

using the tools from the past two sections we are now ready to provide our vector maintenance data structure, algorithm 6, and prove theorem 8. for simplicity, we assume that the sequence  $x^{(t)} \stackrel{\mathrm{def}}{=} x_{\mathrm{init}} + \sum_{k \in [t]} (\mathbf{g}^{(k)} \mathbf{a} h^{(k)} + \delta^{(k)})$  satisfies

$$
\frac {8}{9} x _ {i} ^ {(t - 1)} \leq x _ {i} ^ {(t)} \leq \frac {9}{8} x _ {i} ^ {(t - 1)} \text {f o r a l l} i \tag {6.1}
$$

we note that this assumption is satisfied for our interior point method. if this assumption is violated, we can detect it easily by lemma 33 and propagate this change through the data-structure with small amortized cost. however, this would make the data structure have more special cases and make the code more difficult to read.

the idea of algorithm 6 is as follows. we split the sum  $\sum_{k\in [t]}\mathbf{g}^{(k)}\mathbf{a}h^{(k)}$  into  $\log t$  partial sums of increasing length. each partial sum is maintained via the algorithms of lemma 36, where copy number  $\ell$  is queried after every  $2^{\ell}$  iterations. the data-structure also stores vectors  $v^{(1)},\ldots,v^{([\log n])}$ , where  $v^{(\ell)}$  is the result of these data-structures from the last time they were queried (see line 25), i.e. each  $v^{(\ell)}$  is an approximation of some partial sum of  $\sum_{k\in [t]}\mathbf{g}^{(k)}\mathbf{a}h^{(k)}$ . note that for theorem 8 we want to have a multiplicative approximation for the entries, whereas lemma 36 only yields an additive error. so the partial sums that are maintained in algorithm 6 by using the data-structure of lemma 36 are actually of the form  $\sum_{k = t_0}^{t_1}\mathbf{z}\mathbf{g}^{(k)}\mathbf{a}h^{(k)}$  for some diagonal matrix  $\mathbf{z}$ . by scaling the entries of  $\mathbf{z}$  turn the absolute error into a relative one. we prove that algorithm 6 can be applied carefully to prove theorem 8.

proof of theorem 8. we show that algorithm 6 can be used to obtain a data-structure with the desired running time. formally, we run algorithm 6 and after every  $n$  calls to query compute  $y \in \mathbb{r}^n$  with  $y_i = \mathrm{computeract}(i)$  for all  $i \in [n]$ , i.e. the exact state of the vector and then re-initialize the datastructure with initializ(a, g, y, ε) for the current value of  $g$ . note that, by lemma 36, the time to initialize the data structure is  $o(\mathrm{nnz}(\mathbf{a})\log^5 n)$  and the additional cost this contributes to query is  $o(\lceil t/n\rceil \cdot \mathrm{nnz}(\mathbf{a})\log^5 n) = o(td\log^5 n)$ . consequently, it suffices to prove the theorem when at most  $n$  queries occur.

algorithm 6: maintain multiplicative approximation (theorem 8)

32 procedure // return exact ith entry.   
33 |computerexact(i)   
34 y-(xinit)i   
35 for l=0,...,[log n] do   
36 if l-th bit of t is 1 then y← y+d(ℓ).computerexact(i); // algorithm 5   
37 return y

to analyze this data structure, recall that

$$
x ^ {(t)} \stackrel {\text {d e f}} {=} x _ {\text {i n i t}} + \sum_ {k \in [ t ]} \left(\mathbf {g} ^ {(k)} \mathbf {a} h ^ {(k)} + \delta^ {(k)}\right)
$$

and let  $y^{(t)}$  be the output of the algorithm at iteration  $t$ . our goal is to prove that the  $x^{(t)} \approx_{\epsilon} y^{(t)}$ . we prove this by induction. we assume  $x^{(s)} \approx_{\epsilon} y^{(s)}$  for all  $s < t$  and proceed to prove this is also true for  $s = t$ .

the vectors  $v^{(0)}, \ldots, v^{(\lceil \log n \rceil)}$ : assume we currently perform query number  $t$  and the data-structure is currently performing the loop of line 18. further assume that  $\ell$  is such that  $t \mod 2^{\ell} = 0$ , so the data-structure will assign a new vector to  $v^{(\ell)}$ . let  $g^{(k)}$  be the current state of vector  $g$  during query number  $k \leq t$ . by lemma 36, the vector

$$
v ^ {(\ell)} \stackrel {\text {d e f}} {=} \mathbf {z} ^ {(\ell)} d ^ {(\ell)}. \mathrm {q u e r y} (\epsilon / 8 \lceil \log n \rceil) + \sum_ {k = t - 2 ^ {\ell} + 1} ^ {t} \delta^ {(k)}
$$

satisfies

$$
\left| v _ {i} ^ {(\ell)} - \sum_ {k = t - 2 ^ {\ell} + 1} ^ {t} \left[ \mathbf {g} ^ {(k)} \mathbf {a} h ^ {(k)} + \delta^ {(k)} \right] _ {i} \right| \leq \frac {\epsilon}{2 \lceil \log n \rceil} \cdot z _ {i} ^ {(\ell)}
$$

where  $z^{(\ell)}$  is the extra scaling data structure  $d^{(\ell)}$  uses.

by line 22 we see that if  $z_i^\ell \notin [\frac{8}{9} y_i, \frac{9}{8} y_i]$  then  $d^{(\ell)}$ . markexact(i) had been called after the last query to  $d^{(\ell)}$ . consequently, if  $v_i^{(\ell)} \neq \sum_{k=t-2^\ell+1}^t [\mathbf{g}^{(k)}\mathbf{a}h^{(k)} + \delta^{(k)}]_i$  (i.e. the error is non-zero) then  $\frac{8}{9} y_i^{(t-1)} \leq z^{(\ell)} \leq \frac{9}{8} y_i^{(t-1)}$ . by (6.1) and the induction hypothesis  $x^{(t-1)} \approx_\epsilon y^{(t-1)}$ , we have that  $z^{(\ell)} \leq e^\epsilon (\frac{9}{8})^2 x^{(t)} \leq \frac{7}{5} x^{(t)}$ . hence, for all  $i \in [n]$  we have

$$
\left| v ^ {(\ell)} - \sum_ {k = t - 2 ^ {\ell} + 1} ^ {t} \left(\mathbf {g} ^ {(k)} \mathbf {a} h ^ {(k)} + \delta^ {(k)}\right) \right| _ {i} \leq \frac {7}{1 0} \frac {\epsilon}{\lceil \log n \rceil} \cdot x _ {i} ^ {(t)}.
$$

now consider the case that  $t \bmod 2^{\ell} \neq 0$  and define  $t' = 2^{\ell} \cdot \lfloor t / 2^{\ell} \rfloor$ . if the error of  $v_{i}^{(\ell)}$  is not zero, then it is still bounded by  $\frac{\epsilon}{2|\log n|} \cdot z_{i}^{(\ell)}$ , because  $z_{i}^{(\ell)}$  was not changed in line 26. we can now repeat the previous argument: by line 21, we see that  $d^{(\ell)}$ . computeexact(i) is called whenever  $z_{i}^{\ell} \notin [\frac{8}{9} y_{i}, \frac{9}{8} y_{i}]$ , which sets the error of  $v_{i}^{(\ell)}$  to zero. hence, if the error is non-zero, we know that  $\frac{8}{9} y_{i}^{(t-1)} \leq z^{(\ell)} \leq \frac{9}{8} y_{i}^{(t-1)}$ . with the same argument as before we thus have

$$
\left| v ^ {(\ell)} - \sum_ {k = t ^ {\prime} - 2 ^ {\ell} + 1} ^ {t ^ {\prime}} \left(\mathbf {g} ^ {(k)} \mathbf {a} h ^ {(k)} + \delta^ {(k)}\right) \right| _ {i} \leq \frac {7}{1 0} \frac {\epsilon}{\lceil \log n \rceil} \cdot x _ {i} ^ {(t)}
$$

for all  $i$ . (note that the sum on the left is using  $t'$  for its indices while the error bound on the right uses  $x^{(t)}$ .)

correctness of  $x$ : we compute the vector exactly every  $n$  iterations and reset  $t$  to 0. hence,  $t \leq n$  and hence  $t = \sum_{i=0}^{\lceil \log n \rceil} a_{\ell} 2^{\ell}$  with each  $a_{\ell} \in \{0,1\}$ , i.e. the binary representation of  $t$ . then

$$
\sum_ {k = 1} ^ {t} \left(\mathbf {g} ^ {(k)} \mathbf {a} h ^ {(k)} + \delta^ {(k)}\right) = \sum_ {i = 0} ^ {\lceil \log n \rceil} a _ {\ell} \sum_ {k = 2 ^ {\ell} \lfloor t / 2 ^ {\ell} \rfloor - 2 ^ {\ell} + 1} ^ {2 ^ {\ell} \lfloor t / 2 ^ {\ell} \rfloor} \left(\mathbf {g} ^ {(k)} \mathbf {a} h ^ {(k)} + \delta^ {(k)}\right).
$$

note that  $\sum_{k=2^{\ell}\lfloor t/2^{\ell}\rfloor-2^{\ell}+1}^{2^{\ell}\lfloor t/2^{\ell}\rfloor}(\mathbf{g}^{(k)}\mathbf{a}h^{(k)}+\delta^{(k)})$  is exactly the vector approximated by  $v^{(\ell)}$ , because this sum only changes whenever  $t$  is a multiple of  $2^{\ell}$ , in which case

$$
\sum_ {k = 2 ^ {\ell} \lfloor t / 2 ^ {\ell} \rfloor - 2 ^ {\ell} + 1} ^ {2 ^ {\ell} \lfloor t / 2 ^ {\ell} \rfloor} (\mathbf {g} ^ {(k)} \mathbf {a} h ^ {(k)} + \delta^ {(k)}) = \sum_ {k = t - 2 ^ {i} + 1} ^ {t} (\mathbf {g} ^ {(k)} \mathbf {a} h ^ {(k)} + \delta^ {(k)}) \approx v _ {i}.
$$

thus for  $y^{(t)} \coloneqq x_{\mathrm{init}} + \sum_{\ell = 0}^{\lceil \log n \rceil} a_{\ell} v^{(\ell)}$ , as computed in line 29,  $x^{(t)} \approx_{\epsilon} y^{(t)}$  due to the following

$$
\begin{array}{l} \left| y ^ {(t)} - x ^ {(t)} \right| _ {i} = \left| \sum_ {\ell = 0} ^ {\lceil \log n \rceil} a _ {\ell} v ^ {(\ell)} - \sum_ {k = 1} ^ {t} (\mathbf {g} ^ {(k)} \mathbf {a} h ^ {(k)} + \delta^ {(k)}) \right| _ {i} \\ \leq \left| \sum_ {\ell = 0} ^ {\lceil \log n \rceil} a _ {\ell} \left(v ^ {(\ell)} - \sum_ {k = 2 ^ {\ell} \lfloor t / 2 ^ {\ell} \rfloor - 2 ^ {\ell} + 1} ^ {2 ^ {\ell} \lfloor t / 2 ^ {\ell} \rfloor} \left(\mathbf {g} ^ {(k)} \mathbf {a} h ^ {(k)} + \delta^ {(k)}\right)\right) \right| _ {i} \\ \leq \sum_ {\ell = 0} ^ {\lceil \log n \rceil} \left| v ^ {(\ell)} - \sum_ {k = 2 ^ {\ell} \lfloor t / 2 ^ {\ell} \rfloor - 2 ^ {\ell} + 1} ^ {2 ^ {\ell} \lfloor t / 2 ^ {\ell} \rfloor} \left(\mathbf {g} ^ {(k)} \mathbf {a} h ^ {(k)} + \delta^ {(k)}\right) \right| _ {i} \\ \leq \sum_ {\ell = 0} ^ {\lceil \log n \rceil} \frac {7 \epsilon}{1 0 \lceil \log n \rceil} \cdot x _ {i} ^ {(t)} \leq \frac {7}{1 0} \epsilon \cdot x _ {i} ^ {(t)}. \\ \end{array}
$$

computeexact: note that  $d^{(\ell)}$ .computerexact(i) =  $\sum_{k=2^{\ell} \lfloor t/2^{\ell} \rfloor - 2^{\ell} + 1}^{2^{\ell} \lfloor t/2^{\ell} \rfloor} [\mathbf{g}^{(k)} \mathbf{a} h^{(k)} + \delta^{(k)}]_i$  and therefore, computerexact computes the  $i$ th entry of  $\sum_{k \in [t]} (\mathbf{g}^{(k)} \mathbf{a} h^{(k)} + \delta^{(k)})$  exactly.

complexity: the runtime of initialization and computeexact are that of lemma 36, but increased by an  $o(\log n)$  factor, as we run that many instances of these data-structures.

to bound the runtime of query, fix some  $\ell \geq 0$ . we first analyze the complexity of the loop in line 18 for that specific  $\ell$ . in every iteration we spend  $o(n)$  time to determine which coordinates need to be added to  $f^{(\ell)}$  (line 20). a coordinate  $i \in [n]$  is added to  $f^{(\ell)}$  at most once every  $2^{\ell}$  calls to query and this happens only when  $y_{i}$  has changed by some  $9/8$  multiplicatively from  $z_{i}^{(\ell)}$ . further, if we let  $k \geq t - 2^{\ell}$  denote the last iteration on which  $k = 0 \mod 2^{\ell}$  then as  $\frac{5}{7} x^{(t - 2^{\ell})} \leq z_{i}^{(\ell)} \leq \frac{7}{5} x^{(t - 2^{\ell})}$  and  $y_{i} \approx x_{i}^{(t)}$ , we can bound the number of indices that were in  $f^{(\ell)}$  during the past  $t - k$  iterations by the number of indices  $i$  for which  $x_{i}$  changed by at least constant factor during the past  $t - k$  iterations. the number of such indices is  $o((t - k) \sum_{k=t-2^{\ell}+1}^{t} \left\| (\mathbf{x}^{(k)})^{-1} \mathbf{g}^{(k)} \mathbf{a} h^{(k)} \right\|^{2} + \left\| (\mathbf{x}^{(k)})^{-1} \delta^{(k)} \right\|^{2})$ . for every index that was in  $f^{(\ell)}$  over the past  $t - k$  iterations, the algorithms call d.l.scale, d.l.markexact and d.l.computerexact at most once. consequently, applying lemma 36 and summing over every time  $t = 0 \mod 2^{\ell}$  held, we have that ignoring the cost of  $d^{(\ell)}$ .query() the runtime of the loop for a single  $\ell$  is

$$
o \left(t n + t d \log^ {4} n \cdot \sum_ {k \in [ t ]} \left(\left\| \left(\mathbf {x} ^ {(k)}\right) ^ {- 1} \mathbf {g} ^ {(k)} \mathbf {a} h ^ {(k)} \right\| ^ {2} + \left\| \left(\mathbf {x} ^ {(k)}\right) ^ {- 1} \delta^ {(k)} \right\| ^ {2}\right)\right). \tag {6.2}
$$

next, we bound the runtime of the calls to  $d^{(\ell)}$ .query(). consider such a call when  $t = 0 \mod 2^{\ell}$ . by lemma 36 and the fact that  $d_{\ell}$ .markexact(i) is called for all  $i \in f^{(\ell)}$  the runtime of this call is bounded by

$$
o (1) \cdot \left\| (\mathbf {i} - \mathbf {f} ^ {(\ell)}) \sum_ {k = t - 2 ^ {\ell} + 1} ^ {t} (\mathbf {z} ^ {(\ell)}) ^ {- 1} \mathbf {g} ^ {(k)} \mathbf {a} h ^ {(k)} \right\| _ {2} ^ {2} \cdot \varepsilon^ {- 2} \cdot d \log^ {5} n,
$$

where the extra  $\log^2 n$  term (compared to lemma 36) is due to the  $\log n$  factor in  $\epsilon/\log n$ . now for  $i \notin f^{(\ell)}$  and  $k \in [t - 2^l + 1, t]$  we have  $z^{(\ell)} \approx_{o(1)} y^{(k-1)}$  by construction and  $y^{(k-1)} \approx_{o(1)} x^{(k)}$  by the preceding correctness proof. consequently,

$$
\begin{array}{l} \left\| (\mathbf {i} - \mathbf {f}) \sum_ {k = t - 2 ^ {\ell} + 1} ^ {t} (\mathbf {z} ^ {(\ell)}) ^ {- 1} \mathbf {g} ^ {(k)} \mathbf {a} h ^ {(k)} \right\| _ {2} ^ {2} \leq 2 ^ {\ell} \sum_ {k = t - 2 ^ {\ell} + 1} ^ {t} \left\| (\mathbf {i} - \mathbf {f}) (\mathbf {z} ^ {(\ell)}) ^ {- 1} \mathbf {g} ^ {(k)} \mathbf {a} h ^ {(k)} \right\| _ {2} ^ {2} \\ \leq 2 ^ {\ell} \sum_ {k = t - 2 ^ {\ell} + 1} ^ {t} \left\| (\mathbf {i} - \mathbf {f}) \left(\mathbf {x} ^ {(k)}\right) ^ {- 1} \mathbf {g} ^ {(k)} \mathbf {a} h ^ {(k)} \right\| _ {2} ^ {2} \\ \leq 2 ^ {\ell} \sum_ {k = t - 2 ^ {\ell} + 1} ^ {t} \left\| \left(\mathbf {x} ^ {(k)}\right) ^ {- 1} \mathbf {g} ^ {(k)} \mathbf {a} h ^ {(k)} \right\| _ {2} ^ {2}. \\ \end{array}
$$

since,  $d^{(\ell)}$ .query() was called only  $o(t / 2^{\ell})$  times this yields that the total runtime contribution of  $d^{(\ell)}$ .query() for a single  $\ell$  is

$$
o \left(t \sum_ {k \in [ t ]} \left\| \left(\mathbf {x} ^ {(k)}\right) ^ {- 1} \mathbf {g} ^ {(k)} \mathbf {a} h ^ {(k)} \right\| ^ {2} \cdot \varepsilon^ {- 2} d \log^ {5} n\right).
$$

summing, over all  $o(\log n)$  different values of  $\ell$  and combining with (6.2) then yields the desired running time for query.

adaptive adversaries: the algorithm works against adaptive adversaries, because the data structures of lemma 36 work against adaptive adversaries.

# 7 leverage score maintenance

the ipm presented in this work requires approximate leverage scores of a matrix of the form  $\mathbf{g}\mathbf{a}$ , where  $\mathbf{g}$  is a non-negative diagonal matrix that changes slowly from one iteration to the next. here we provide a data-structure which exploits this stability to maintain the leverage scores of this matrix more efficiently than recomputing them from scratch every time  $\mathbf{g}$  changes. formally, we provide algorithm 7 and show it proves the following theorem on maintaining leverage scores:

theorem 9 (leverage score maintenance). there exists a monte-carlo data-structure (algorithm 7), that works against an adaptive adversary, with the following procedures:

- initializer(a,  $g, \epsilon$ ): given matrix  $\mathbf{a} \in \mathbb{r}^{n \times d}$ , scaling  $g \in \mathbb{r}^n$  and target accuracy  $\epsilon > 0$ , the data-structure preprocesses in  $o(nd\epsilon^{-2}\log^4 n)$  time.  
-  $\operatorname{scale}(i, u)$ : given  $i \in [n]$  and  $u \in \mathbb{r}$  sets  $g_i = u$  in  $o(d\epsilon^{-2}\log^5 n)$  time.  
-  $query(\psi^{(t)}, \psi_{\mathrm{(safe)}}^{(t)})$ : let  $g^{(t)}$  be the vector  $g$  during t-th call to  $query$ , assume  $g^{(t)} \approx_{1/16} g^{(t-1)}$  and define  $\mathbf{h}^{(t)} = \mathbf{a}^{\top} (\mathbf{g}^{(t)})^2 \mathbf{a}$ . given random input-matrices  $\psi^{(t)} \in \mathbb{r}^{d \times d}$  and  $\psi_{\mathrm{(safe)}}^{(t)} \in \mathbb{r}^{d \times d}$  such that

$$
\psi^ {(t)} \approx_ {\epsilon / (2 4 \log n)} (\mathbf {h} ^ {(t)}) ^ {- 1}, \psi_ {\mathrm {(s a f e)}} ^ {(t)} \approx_ {\epsilon / (2 4 \log n)} (\mathbf {h} ^ {(t)}) ^ {- 1}.
$$

and any randomness used to generate  $\psi_{\mathrm{(safe)}}^{(t)}$  is independent of the randomness used to generate  $\psi^{(t)}$ , w.h.p. in  $n$  the data-structure outputs a vector  $\widetilde{\tau} \in \mathbb{r}^n$  independent of  $\psi^{(1)}, \ldots, \psi^{(t)}$  such that  $\widetilde{\tau}_i \approx_\epsilon \tau_i(\mathbf{g}^{(t)}\mathbf{a})$  for all  $i \in [n]$ . furthermore, the total cost over  $t$  steps is

$$
o \left(\left(\sum_ {t \in [ t ]} \| \mathbf {g} ^ {(t)} \mathbf {a} \boldsymbol {\psi} ^ {(t)} \mathbf {a} ^ {\top} \mathbf {g} ^ {(t)} - \mathbf {g} ^ {(t - 1)} \mathbf {a} \boldsymbol {\psi} ^ {(t - 1)} \mathbf {a} ^ {\top} \mathbf {g} ^ {(t - 1)} \| _ {f}\right) ^ {2} \cdot \epsilon^ {- 4} n \log^ {7} n + t \left(t _ {\psi} + \epsilon^ {- 2} d ^ {2} \log^ {3} n\right)\right)
$$

where  $t_{\psi}$  is the time required to multiply a vector with  $\psi^{(t)}$  (i.e. in case it is given implicitly via a data structure).

the high-level idea for obtaining this data-structure is that when  $\mathbf{g}$  changes slowly, the leverage scores of  $\mathbf{ga}$  change slowly as well. thus not too many leverage scores must be recomputed per iteration, when we are interested in some  $(1 + \varepsilon)$ -approximation, as the previously computed values are still a valid approximation. the task of maintaining a valid approximation can thus be split into two parts: (i) for some small set  $j$ , quickly compute the  $j$ -th leverage score for  $j \in j$ , and (ii) detect which leverage scores should be recomputed, i.e. which leverage scores have changed enough so their previously computed value is no longer a valid approximation.

this section is structured according to the two tasks, (i) and (ii). in section 7.1 we show how to solve (i), i.e. quickly compute a small number of leverage scores. in section 7.2 we then show how to solve (ii) by showing how to detect which leverage scores have changed significantly and must be recomputed. the last section 7.3 then combines these two results to prove theorem 9.

before we proceed to prove (i) and (ii), we first give a more accurate outline of how the algorithm of theorem 9 works. an important observation is, that by standard dimension reduction tricks, a large part of theorem 9 can be obtained by reduction to the vector data-structures of section 6. note that the  $i$ -th leverage score of  $\mathbf{g}^{(t)}\mathbf{a}$  is

$$
\sigma (\mathbf {g} ^ {(t)} \mathbf {a}) _ {i} = 1 _ {i} ^ {\top} \mathbf {g} ^ {(t)} \mathbf {a} (\mathbf {a} ^ {\top} (\mathbf {g} ^ {(t)}) ^ {2} \mathbf {a}) ^ {- 1} \mathbf {a} ^ {\top} \mathbf {g} ^ {(t)} 1 _ {i} = \| e _ {i} ^ {\top} \mathbf {g} ^ {(t)} \mathbf {a} (\mathbf {a} ^ {\top} (\mathbf {g} ^ {(t)}) ^ {2} \mathbf {a}) ^ {- 1} \mathbf {a} ^ {\top} \mathbf {g} ^ {(t)} \| _ {2} ^ {2}
$$

algorithm 7: leverage score maintenance (theorem 9)  
1 members   
2 d; // data-structure of corollary 37, lemma 33, algorithm 4   
3  $(g^{(t)})_{t\geq 0}\in \mathbb{r}^n$  // rows scaling of a at time t   
4  $(\widetilde{\tau})_{t\geq 0}\in \mathbb{r}^n$  // approximate shifted leverage scores at time t   
5  $(\psi^{(t)})_{t\geq 0},(\psi_{\mathrm{(safe)}}^{(t)})_{t\geq 0}\in \mathbb{r}^{d\times d}$  // approximate inverses at time t   
6  $\mathbf{r} = \mathrm{jl}(1 / 100,d)^{\top},(\mathbf{b}^{(t)})_{t\geq 0}\in \mathbb{r}^{d\times b};$  // jl-matrix  $\mathbf{r},\mathbf{b}^{(t)} = \mathbf{a}^{\top}\mathbf{g}^{(t)}\mathbf{r}$    
7 procedure initialize(a  $\in \mathbb{r}^{n\times d}$ $g\in \mathbb{r}^n$ $\varepsilon \in (0,1)$    
8  $\mathbf{r}\leftarrow \mathrm{jl}(1 / 100,d)^{\top};$  //  $\| v^{\top}\mathbf{r}\|_{2}\in (1\pm \frac{1}{100})\| v\|_{2}$  w.h.p. in n   
9 d.initialize(a,g); // algorithm 4   
10  $t\gets 0,\varepsilon \gets \varepsilon ,g^{(t)}\gets g,\mathbf{b}^{(t)}\gets \mathbf{a}^{\top}\mathbf{g}^{(t)}\mathbf{r}$    
11 procedure scale(j∈[n],u∈rn)   
12  $\mathbf{b}^{(t)}\gets \mathbf{b}^{(t)} + (\mathbf{a}^{\top}e_{j})(u - g_{j}^{(t)})(e_{j}^{\top}\mathbf{r});$  // maintain  $\mathbf{b}^{(t)} = \mathbf{a}^{\top}\mathbf{g}^{(t)}\mathbf{r}$    
13 d.scale(j,u),  $g_j^{(t)}\gets u$  // make d represent  $\mathbf{g}^{(t)}\mathbf{a}$  . algorithm 4   
14 procedure estimatescore(jc[n],t∈n,δ∈(0,1)) // internal procedure   
15  $\tilde{\mathbf{r}}\gets \mathrm{jl}(\delta /8,d)^{\top}$    
16 if first step then w  $\leftarrow \psi_{\mathrm{(safe)}}^{(t)}\mathbf{a}^{\top}\mathbf{g}^{(t)}\tilde{\mathbf{r}};$    
17 else // a'g²a ≈/4 a'(g(t))²a w.h.p. in n   
18 w  $\leftarrow \psi_{\mathrm{(safe)}}^{(t)}\mathbf{a}^{\top}\tilde{\mathbf{g}}\tilde{\mathbf{r}}$  where  $\tilde{g}_i = \left\{\frac{g_i^{(t)}}{\sqrt{p_i}}\right.$  with probability pi = θ(logn t i).   
19 return  $v\in \mathbb{r}^j$  with  $vj = \| e_j^\top g(t)aw\| _2^2 +\frac{d}{n}$  for  $j\in j$    
20 procedure findindices() // internal procedure   
21  $j\gets \emptyset$  // list j depends only on  $\psi_{\mathrm{(safe)}}$    
22 for i ∈ {0,..., [log n] s.t. t = 0 mod 2 do // compute candidate update indices.   
23 fj {j: g(t) j g(t') for some t-2i t' < t}.   
24 w ← d.query(ψ(t)b(t)- ψ(t-2i)b(t-2i),fi, ε/48 log n√nb); // algorithm 4   
25 ji fj u {j: wj,ε≠ 0 for some l}   
26 v ← estimatescore(ji,t,ε/(12 log n))   
27 v(old) ← estimatescore(ji,t-2i,ε/(12 log n))   
28 j ← j ∪ {j such that  $vj\not\approx\frac{\epsilon}{3\log n}v_{j}^{(\mathrm{old})}$ }   
29 return j   
30 procedure query(ψ∈rd×d,ψ(safe)∈rd×d)   
31 ψ(t) ← ψ, ψ(t) ← ψ(safe)   
32 if t = 0 mod 2[log n] or first step then   
33 |  $\tilde{\tau}^{(0)}$  ← estimatescore([n],t,ε), t← 1   
34 return  $\tilde{\tau}^{(0)}$    
35 j ← findindices() and v ← estimatescore(j,t,ε)   
36 for all j ∈ [n] set  $\tilde{\tau}_{j}^{(t)}$  vj if j ∈ j and  $\tilde{\tau}_{j}^{(t)}$  ←  $\tilde{\tau}_{j}^{(t-1)}$  otherwise.   
37 b(t+1) ← b(t), g(t+1) ← g(t),  $\tilde{\tau}^{(t+1)}$  ←  $\tilde{\tau}^{(t)}$  , t← t+1   
38 return  $\tilde{\tau}^{(t-1)}$

and the term on the right-hand side can be estimated using a jl-matrix  $\mathbf{r} \in \mathbb{r}^{n \times b}$ . this allows us to greatly reduce the complexity of estimating  $\sigma(\mathbf{g}^{(t)}\mathbf{a})$  as  $\mathbf{g}^{(t)}\mathbf{a}(\mathbf{a}^{\top}(\mathbf{g}^{(t)})^{2}\mathbf{a})^{-1}\mathbf{a}^{\top}\mathbf{g}^{(t)}\mathbf{r}$  is essentially just  $o(b)$  matrix-vector products, where  $\mathbf{g}^{(t)}\mathbf{a}$  is the matrix and the columns of  $(\mathbf{a}^{\top}(\mathbf{g}^{(t)})^{2}\mathbf{a})^{-1}\mathbf{a}^{\top}\mathbf{g}^{(t)}\mathbf{r}$  are the vectors. with this in mind, we use the following corollary to prove theorem 9.

corollary 37. there exists a monte-carlo data-structure, that holds against an adaptive adversary, with the following procedures:

- initialize(a,g): given a matrix  $\mathbf{a} \in \mathbb{r}^{n \times d}$  and a scaling  $g \in \mathbb{r}^n$ , the data-structure pre-processes in  $o(\mathrm{nnz}(\mathbf{a}) \log^4 n)$  time.  
- query  $(\mathbf{h}, f, \epsilon)$ : given a matrix  $\mathbf{h} \in \mathbb{r}^{d \times b}$  and a set  $f \subset [n]$ , w.h.p. in  $n$  the data-structure outputs the matrix

$$
\mathbf {v} _ {i, j} = \left\{ \begin{array}{l l} ((\mathbf {i} - \mathbf {f}) \mathbf {g a h}) _ {i, j}, & i f | (\mathbf {g a h}) _ {i, j} | \geq \varepsilon \\ 0, & o t h e r w i s e \end{array} \right.
$$

where  $\mathbf{f} \in \{0,1\}^{n \times n}$  is a 0-1 diagonal matrix with  $\mathbf{f}_{ii} = 1$  if and only if  $i \in f$  in time

$$
o \left(\| (\mathbf {i} - \mathbf {f}) \mathbf {g a h} \| _ {f} ^ {2} \cdot \varepsilon^ {- 2} \cdot d \cdot \log^ {3} n + | f | \cdot d \cdot \log^ {4} n + d b\right).
$$

-  $\text{scale}(i, u)$ : sets  $g_i \gets u$  in  $o(d\log^4 n)$  time.

proof. the algorithm is directly obtained from lemma 33. let  $\mathbf{h}, f, \epsilon$  be the input to query and let  $d$  be an instance of lemma 33. we perform  $d.\mathrm{scale}(i,0)$  for all  $i \in f$ , so the data-structure  $d$  represents  $(\mathbf{i} - \mathbf{f})\mathbf{g}\mathbf{a}$ . we then obtain  $\mathbf{v}$  by setting the  $i$ -th column of  $\mathbf{v}$  to the result of  $d.\mathrm{query}(\mathbf{h}e_i, \epsilon)$  for  $i = 1, \dots, b$ . afterward, we call  $d.\mathrm{scale}$  again, to revert back to the previous scaling. the correctness and the time complexity of this new data-structure follow from lemma 33.

now that we have corollary 37, we can outline how we obtain theorem 9 from it. given a matrix  $\psi \approx (\mathbf{a}^{\top}(\mathbf{g}^{(t)})^{2}\mathbf{a})^{-1}$ , it is easy to compute individual leverage scores (see section 7.1, lemma 38). however, such computation requires nearly linear work, which is quite slow for our purposes, and therefore we do not want to recompute every leverage score every time  $\mathbf{g}^{(t)}$  changes a bit. instead, we use corollary 37 to detect which scores have changed a lot. this is done in section 7.2, lemma 39 and lemma s40. the idea is that, if  $\|1_i^\top \mathbf{g}^{(t)}\mathbf{a}(\mathbf{a}^\top (\mathbf{g}^{(t)})^2\mathbf{a})^{-1}\mathbf{a}^\top \mathbf{g}^{(t)}\|_2$  and  $\|1_i^\top \mathbf{g}^{(k)}\mathbf{a}(\mathbf{a}^\top (\mathbf{g}^{(k)})^2\mathbf{a})^{-1}\mathbf{a}^\top \mathbf{g}^{(k)}\|_2$  differ substantially, for some  $k < t$ , then

$$
\left\| 1 _ {i} ^ {\top} \left(\mathbf {g} ^ {(t)} \mathbf {a} \left(\mathbf {a} ^ {\top} \left(\mathbf {g} ^ {(t)}\right) ^ {2} \mathbf {a}\right) ^ {- 1} \mathbf {a} ^ {\top} \mathbf {g} ^ {(t)} - \mathbf {g} ^ {(k)} \mathbf {a} \left(\mathbf {a} ^ {\top} \left(\mathbf {g} ^ {(k)}\right) ^ {2} \mathbf {a}\right) ^ {- 1} \mathbf {a} ^ {\top} \mathbf {g} ^ {(k)}\right) \mathbf {r} \right\| _ {2}
$$

should be quite large as well for some jl-matrix  $\mathbf{r}$ , i.e. something we can detect via corollary 37.

# 7.1 computing few leverage scores

we start our proof of theorem 9 by showing how to quickly compute a small set of leverage scores. this corresponds to the function estimatescore in algorithm 7.

lemma 38 (guarantee of estimatescore). assume  $\widetilde{\tau}^{(t)}\approx_{1 / 2}\sigma (\mathbf{g}^{(t)}\mathbf{a}) + \frac{d}{n}$  and  $\psi_{\mathrm{(safe)}}^{(t)}\approx_{\delta /4}$ $(\mathbf{a}^{\top}(\mathbf{g}^{(t)})^{2}\mathbf{a})^{-1}$  for some  $\delta >0$  . then, estimatescore(j,t,δ) returns  $v\in \mathbb{r}^n$  such that

$$
v _ {i} \approx_ {\delta} \sigma (\mathbf {g} ^ {(t)} \mathbf {a}) _ {i} + \frac {d}{n}
$$

w.h.p. in  $n$ . furthermore, the cost for the first iteration is  $o((\mathrm{nnz}(a) + t_{\psi_{(\mathrm{safe})}})\cdot (\log n) / \delta^2)$  and the cost of other iterations is  $o((t_{\psi_{(\mathrm{safe})}} + d^{2} + d|j|)(\log n) / \delta^{2})$ .

proof. correctness: for the first step, we have

$$
\begin{array}{l} \| e _ {j} ^ {\top} \mathbf {g} ^ {(t)} \mathbf {a} \psi_ {\mathrm {(s a f e)}} ^ {(t)} \mathbf {a} ^ {\top} \mathbf {g} ^ {(t)} \| _ {2} ^ {2} = e _ {j} ^ {\top} \mathbf {g} ^ {(t)} \mathbf {a} \psi_ {\mathrm {(s a f e)}} ^ {(t)} \mathbf {a} ^ {\top} (\mathbf {g} ^ {(t)}) ^ {2} \mathbf {a} \psi_ {\mathrm {(s a f e)}} ^ {(t)} \mathbf {a} ^ {\top} \mathbf {g} ^ {(t)} e _ {j} \\ \approx_ {\delta / 4} e _ {j} ^ {\top} \mathbf {g} ^ {(t)} \mathbf {a} \psi_ {\mathrm {(s a f e)}} ^ {(t)} \mathbf {a} ^ {\top} \mathbf {g} ^ {(t)} e _ {j} \\ \approx_ {\delta / 4} e _ {j} ^ {\top} \mathbf {g} ^ {(t)} \mathbf {a} (\mathbf {a} ^ {\top} (\mathbf {g} ^ {(t)}) ^ {2} \mathbf {a}) ^ {- 1} \mathbf {a} ^ {\top} \mathbf {g} ^ {(t)} e _ {j} = \sigma (\mathbf {g} ^ {(t)} \mathbf {a}) _ {j}. \\ \end{array}
$$

where both approximations follow from the assumption  $\psi_{\mathrm{(safe)}}^{(t)}\approx_{\delta /4}(\mathbf{a}^{\top}(\mathbf{g}^{(t)})^{2}\mathbf{a})^{-1}$ . now, by jl, lemma 35, and the choice of  $\widetilde{\mathbf{r}}$ , we have

$$
\| e _ {j} ^ {\top} \mathbf {g} ^ {(t)} \mathbf {a} \psi_ {\mathrm {(s a f e)}} ^ {(t)} \mathbf {a} ^ {\top} \mathbf {g} ^ {(t)} \widetilde {\mathbf {r}} \| _ {2} ^ {2} \approx_ {\delta / 4} \| e _ {j} ^ {\top} \mathbf {g} ^ {(t)} \mathbf {a} \psi_ {\mathrm {(s a f e)}} ^ {(t)} \mathbf {a} ^ {\top} \mathbf {g} ^ {(t)} \| _ {2} ^ {2} \approx_ {\delta / 2} \sigma (\mathbf {g} ^ {(t)} \mathbf {a}) _ {j}.
$$

hence, we have the result.

for the other case, at line 18, we sample row  $i$  with probability at least  $\theta(1)\frac{\log n}{\delta^2}\widetilde{\tau}_i^{(t)} \geq \theta(1)\frac{\log n}{\delta^2}\sigma(\mathbf{g}^{(t)}\mathbf{a})_i$  (using the assumption on  $\widetilde{\tau}_i^{(t)})$ ). hence, the leverage score sampling guarantee shows that  $\mathbf{a}^\top \widetilde{\mathbf{g}}^2\mathbf{a} \approx_{\delta/4} \mathbf{a}^\top (\mathbf{g}^{(t)})^2\mathbf{a}$  by choosing large enough constant in the  $\theta(1)$  factor in the probability. hence, by the same argument as above, we have the result.

complexity for the first step, it takes  $o(nd(\log n) / \delta^2)$  time to compute  $\mathbf{a}^\top \mathbf{g}^{(t)}\widetilde{\mathbf{r}}$  and takes  $o(d^2 (\log n) / \delta^2)$  time to compute  $\psi_{\mathrm{(safe)}}^{(t)}\mathbf{a}^\top \mathbf{g}^{(t)}\widetilde{\mathbf{r}}$ . after computing  $\psi_{\mathrm{(safe)}}^{(t)}\mathbf{a}^\top \mathbf{g}^{(t)}\widetilde{\mathbf{r}}$ , it takes  $|j|\log n / \delta^2$  times to compute the norm for each  $j$ . since both  $d$  and  $|j|$  is smaller than  $n$ , the cost is dominated by  $o(nd(\log n) / \delta^2)$ .

for the other case, we note that  $\sum_{i}\widetilde{\tau}_{i}^{(t)} = o(d)$  and hence  $\| \widetilde{g}\| _0 = o(d\log n / \delta^2)$ . therefore, it takes  $o(d^{2}\log n / \delta^{2})$  time to compute  $\mathbf{a}^{\top}\widetilde{\mathbf{g}}\widetilde{\mathbf{r}}$ . similar as above, the total cost is  $o((t_{\psi_{\mathrm{(safe)}}} + d^2 +d|j|)\log n / \delta^2)$ .

# 7.2 detecting leverage scores change

now that we know how to quickly compute a small set of leverage scores, we must figure out which scores to compute. we only want to recompute the scores, that have changed substantially within some time-span. more accurately, we want to show that findindices (algorithm 7) does indeed find all indices that changed a lot. the proof is split into two parts: we first show that we can detect large changes that happen within a sequence of updates of length  $2^i$  in lemma 39. we then extend the result to all large leverage score changes in lemma 40.

lemma 39. consider an execution of line 25 (algorithm 7) at iteration  $t$ . suppose  $\psi^{(t)} \approx_{\epsilon /12\log n}(\mathbf{a}^{\top}(\mathbf{g}^{(t)})^{2}\mathbf{a})^{-1}$  and  $\psi^{(t - 2^i)} \approx_{\epsilon /36\log n}(\mathbf{a}^{\top}(\mathbf{g}^{(t - 2^i)})^{2}\mathbf{a})^{-1}$ . then  $j \in j$  w.h.p. in  $n$  if

$$
\sigma (\mathbf {g} ^ {(t - 2 ^ {i})} \mathbf {a}) _ {j} + \frac {d}{n} \not \approx_ {\epsilon / 2 \log n} \sigma (\mathbf {g} ^ {(t)} \mathbf {a}) _ {j} + \frac {d}{n}. \tag {7.1}
$$

further, the set  $j$  remains the same w.h.p. in  $n$  if we replace line 25 by  $j_{i}\gets [n]$

proof. first, we prove that for any  $j \in [n]$  with

$$
\sigma \left(\mathbf {g} ^ {(t)} \mathbf {a}\right) _ {j} + \frac {d}{n} \not \approx_ {\frac {\epsilon}{6 \log n}} \sigma \left(\mathbf {g} ^ {(t - 2 ^ {i})} \mathbf {a}\right) _ {j} + \frac {d}{n}, \tag {7.2}
$$

we have  $j \in j_i$  (see line 25). to do this, consider any  $j \notin j_i$ , i.e.  $j$  is not in  $f_i$  and not picked by the function  $d.query$ . by line 25 and corollary 37,  $j$  not picked by the function  $d.query$  implies that

$$
\big | ((\mathbf {i} - \mathbf {f} _ {i}) \mathbf {g} ^ {(t)} \mathbf {a} (\psi^ {(t)} \mathbf {b} ^ {(t)} - \psi^ {(t - 2 ^ {i})} \mathbf {b} ^ {(t - 2 ^ {i})})) _ {j, \ell} \big | \leq \epsilon_ {\psi} \stackrel {\mathrm {d e f}} {=} \frac {\epsilon}{4 8 \log n} \sqrt {\frac {d}{n b}}
$$

for all  $\ell$ . since  $j$  is not in  $f_{i}$ , it means that  $g_{j}$  does not change in the last  $2^{i}$  iterations. hence, we have

$$
((\mathbf {i} - \mathbf {f} _ {i}) \mathbf {g} ^ {(t)} \mathbf {a} (\boldsymbol {\psi} ^ {(t)} \mathbf {b} ^ {(t)} - \boldsymbol {\psi} ^ {(t - 2 ^ {i})} \mathbf {b} ^ {(t - 2 ^ {i})})) _ {j, \ell} = ((\mathbf {g} ^ {(t)} \mathbf {a} \boldsymbol {\psi} ^ {(t)} \mathbf {a} \mathbf {g} ^ {(t)} - \mathbf {g} ^ {(t - 2 ^ {i})} \mathbf {a} \boldsymbol {\psi} ^ {(t - 2 ^ {i})} \mathbf {a} \mathbf {g} ^ {(t - 2 ^ {i})}) \mathbf {r}) _ {j, \ell},
$$

using this and that there are only  $b$  many columns in  $\mathbf{r}$  (namely, there are only  $b$  many  $\ell$ ), we have that

$$
\| 1 _ {j} ^ {\top} (\mathbf {g} ^ {(t)} \mathbf {a} \boldsymbol {\psi} ^ {(t)} \mathbf {a} \mathbf {g} ^ {(t)} - \mathbf {g} ^ {(t - 2 ^ {i})} \mathbf {a} \boldsymbol {\psi} ^ {(t - 2 ^ {i})} \mathbf {a} \mathbf {g} ^ {(t - 2 ^ {i})}) \mathbf {r} \| _ {2} ^ {2} \leq \epsilon_ {\psi} ^ {2} b.
$$

hence, we have that w.h.p. in  $n$

$$
\begin{array}{l} \left(\| e _ {j} ^ {\top} \mathbf {g} ^ {(t)} \mathbf {a} \psi^ {(t)} \mathbf {a g} ^ {(t)} \| _ {2} ^ {2} + \frac {d}{n}\right) ^ {1 / 2} - \left(\| e _ {j} ^ {\top} \mathbf {g} ^ {(t - 2 ^ {i})} \mathbf {a} \psi^ {(t - 2 ^ {i})} \mathbf {a g} ^ {(t - 2 ^ {i})}\right) \| _ {2} ^ {2} + \frac {d}{n}\left. \right) ^ {1 / 2} \\ \leq \left\| e _ {j} ^ {\top} \mathbf {g} ^ {(t)} \mathbf {a} \psi^ {(t)} \mathbf {a g} ^ {(t)} - e _ {j} ^ {\top} \mathbf {g} ^ {(t - 2 ^ {i})} \mathbf {a} \psi^ {(t - 2 ^ {i})} \mathbf {a g} ^ {(t - 2 ^ {i})} \right\| _ {2} \\ \leq 2 \| e _ {j} ^ {\top} (\mathbf {g} ^ {(t)} \mathbf {a} \psi^ {(t)} \mathbf {a g} ^ {(t)} - \mathbf {g} ^ {(t - 2 ^ {i})} \mathbf {a} \psi^ {(t - 2 ^ {i})} \mathbf {a g} ^ {(t - 2 ^ {i})}) \mathbf {r} \| _ {2} \leq 2 \epsilon_ {\psi} \sqrt {b} \\ \end{array}
$$

where the second inequality follows from jl (lemma 35) and the choice of  $\mathbf{r}$ . by the definition of  $\approx$  and that  $\epsilon_{\psi}$  is small enough, we have

$$
\left(\| e _ {j} ^ {\top} \mathbf {g} ^ {(t)} \mathbf {a} \psi^ {(t)} \mathbf {a g} ^ {(t)} \| _ {2} ^ {2} + \frac {d}{n}\right) ^ {1 / 2} \approx_ {4 \epsilon_ {\psi} \sqrt {b} / \sqrt {\frac {d}{n}}} \left(\| e _ {j} ^ {\top} \mathbf {g} ^ {(t - 2 ^ {i})} \mathbf {a} \psi^ {(t - 2 ^ {i})} \mathbf {a g} ^ {(t - 2 ^ {i})}) \| _ {2} ^ {2} + \frac {d}{n}\right) ^ {1 / 2}.
$$

squaring both sides, using the fact that

$$
\begin{array}{l} \left\| e _ {j} ^ {\top} \mathbf {g} ^ {(t)} \mathbf {a} \psi^ {(t)} \mathbf {a} ^ {\top} \mathbf {g} ^ {(t)} \right\| _ {2} ^ {2} = e _ {j} ^ {\top} \mathbf {g} ^ {(t)} \mathbf {a} \psi^ {(t)} \mathbf {a} ^ {\top} (\mathbf {g} ^ {(t)}) ^ {2} \mathbf {a} \psi^ {(t)} \mathbf {a} ^ {\top} \mathbf {g} ^ {(t)} e _ {j} \\ \approx_ {\epsilon / (3 6 \log n)} e _ {j} ^ {\top} \mathbf {g} ^ {(t)} \mathbf {a} \boldsymbol {\psi} ^ {(t)} \mathbf {a} ^ {\top} \mathbf {g} ^ {(t)} e _ {j} \approx_ {\epsilon / (3 6 \log n)} \sigma (\mathbf {g} ^ {(t)} \mathbf {a}) _ {j} \\ \end{array}
$$

and similarly the fact that  $\| e_j^\top \mathbf{g}^{(t - 2^i)}\mathbf{a}\psi^{(t - 2^i)}\mathbf{a}\mathbf{g}^{(t - 2^i)})\| _2^2\approx_{\epsilon /(18\log n)}\sigma (\mathbf{g}^{(t - 2^i)}\mathbf{a})_j$  , we have

$$
\sigma (\mathbf {g} ^ {(t)} \mathbf {a}) _ {j} + \frac {d}{n} \approx_ {8 \epsilon_ {\psi} \sqrt {\frac {n b}{d}} + \frac {2 \epsilon}{1 8 \log n}} \sigma (\mathbf {g} ^ {(t - 2 ^ {i})} \mathbf {a}) _ {j} + \frac {d}{n}.
$$

using  $\epsilon_{\psi} = \frac{\epsilon}{144\log n}\sqrt{\frac{d}{nb}}$ , we showed that  $j \notin j_i$  implies (7.2) is false. this proves the claim that for any  $j$  such that (7.2) holds, we have that  $j \in j_i$ .

now, we use the claim to prove the statement of this lemma. fix any  $j$  such that  $\sigma(\mathbf{g}^{(t)}\mathbf{a})_j + \frac{d}{n}\not\approx_{\epsilon/(2\log n)}\sigma(\mathbf{g}^{(t-2^i)}\mathbf{a})_j + \frac{d}{n}$ . by the claim, we have that  $j \in j_i$ , namely,  $j$  will be detected by  $d.\text{query}$ . now, lemma 38 shows that  $v_j \not\approx_{\epsilon/(3\log n)} v_j^{(\text{old})}$  and hence  $j$  will be put into  $j$  by line 28. this proves that  $j \in j$ .

finally, we prove that the output remains the same without using  $d$ .query. note that  $j \in j$  implies  $v_{j} \not\approx_{\epsilon/(3\log n)} v_{j}^{(\mathrm{old})}$  by line 28. again, by lemma 38, we see that this implies  $\sigma(\mathbf{g}^{(t)}\mathbf{a})_{j} + \frac{d}{n} \not\approx_{\epsilon/(6\log n)} \sigma(\mathbf{g}^{(t-2^{i})}\mathbf{a})_{j} + \frac{d}{n}$  w.h.p. in  $n$ . hence, the claim in the beginning shows that  $j \in j_{i}$ . therefore, regardless of whether we use the current definition of  $j_{i}$  or the alternative definition that  $j_{i} = [n]$ , the output  $j$  remains the same w.h.p. in  $n$ .

the previous lemma only showed how to detect leverage scores that changed within some time-span of length  $2^i$ , we now show that this is enough to detect all changes, no matter if they happen slowly (over some long time-span) or radically (within one iteration), or something in-between.

lemma 40 (guarantee of findindices). under the assumption of lemma 38 and lemma 39 let  $j^{(t)}$  be the output of findindices (algorithm 7) at  $t$ -th iteration and

$$
j ^ {* (t)} = \left\{j: \widetilde {\tau} _ {j} ^ {(t)} \not \approx_ {\epsilon} \sigma (\mathbf {g} ^ {(t)} \mathbf {a}) _ {j} + \frac {d}{n} \right\}.
$$

then, we have that  $j^{*(t)} \subset j^{(t)}$  w.h.p. in  $n$ .

proof. to show that  $j^{*(t)} \subset j^{(t)}$ , we consider any  $j \notin j^{(t)}$ . let  $t$  be the current iteration and  $\bar{t}$  be the last time  $\widetilde{\tau}_j^{(\bar{t})}$  is updated. since the algorithm detects changes of shifted leverage scores at  $2^{\ell}$  iterations, we consider a sequence

$$
t _ {0} = t \geq t _ {1} \geq \dots \geq t _ {u} = \bar {t}
$$

such that  $t_z \mod 2^{\ell_z} = 0$  and  $t_{z+1} = t_z - 2^{\ell_z}$  for all  $z$  (namely, the algorithm is trying to detect the change between  $t_z$  and  $t_{z+1}$  in line 24). we can obtain such a sequence of length at most  $2\log t$  as follows: let  $t' = \lfloor t/2^i \rfloor \cdot 2^i$ , for the largest  $i$  such that  $1 \leq 2^i \leq t - k$ . then  $k \leq t' \leq t$ . we now create a sequence where we first flip the 1s to 0s in the binary representation of  $t$ , from least significant to greatest significant bit. this is a sequence  $t_0 = t \geq t_1 \geq \dots \geq t_v = t'$  of length  $v \leq \log t$ . next, we continue the sequence  $t' = t_v \geq t_{v+1} \geq \dots \geq t_u = \bar{t}$  as follows: consider the sequence in reverse, then we always add  $2^{\ell_{z-1}}$  to  $t_z$  to obtain  $t_{z-1}$ , where  $\ell_{z-1}$  is the position of the least significant 1-bit in  $t_z$ . by adding this  $2^{\ell_{z-1}}$ , we flip the 1-bit of  $t_z$  to a 0, but might flip a few more significant 1-bits via the carrier. this creates a sequence that will reach  $t'$  after at most  $\log t'$  steps. since we recompute all shifted leverage score every  $n$  (or less) iterations, we have  $t \leq n$  and hence  $u \leq 2\log n$ .

next, we note that  $\widetilde{\tau}_j^{(t_z)}$  is not updated implies  $j\notin j^{(t_z)}$ . by lemma 39, we see that  $j\notin j^{(t_z)}$ ,  $t_z\bmod 2^{\ell_z} = 0$  and  $t_{z + 1} = t_z - 2^{\ell_z}$  implies

$$
\sigma \left(\mathbf {g} ^ {(t _ {z})} \mathbf {a}\right) _ {j} + \frac {d}{n} \approx_ {\epsilon / 2 \log n} \sigma \left(\mathbf {g} ^ {(t _ {z + 1})} \mathbf {a}\right) _ {j} + \frac {d}{n} \tag {7.3}
$$

for  $z = 0,1,\dots ,u - 1$ . since there are only  $2\log n$  steps, (7.3) shows that

$$
\sigma (\mathbf {g} ^ {(\bar {t})} \mathbf {a}) _ {j} + \frac {d}{n} \approx_ {\epsilon / 2} \sigma (\mathbf {g} ^ {(t)} \mathbf {a}) _ {j} + \frac {d}{n}. (7. 4)
$$

finally, we note that

$$
\widetilde {\tau} _ {j} ^ {(t)} = \widetilde {\tau} _ {j} ^ {(\bar {t})} \tag {7.5}
$$

because  $\widetilde{\tau}$  has not been updated and that lemma 38 shows

$$
\widetilde {\tau} _ {j} ^ {(\bar {t})} \approx_ {\epsilon / 2} \sigma \left(\mathbf {g} ^ {(\bar {t})} \mathbf {a}\right) _ {j} + \frac {d}{n} \tag {7.6}
$$

for the last update. combining (7.4), (7.5) and (7.6), we see that  $\widetilde{\tau}_j^{(t)}\approx_{\epsilon}\sigma (\mathbf{g}^{(t)}\mathbf{a})_j + \frac{d}{n}$  and hence  $j\notin j^{*(t)}$ . this shows that  $j\notin j^{(t)}$  implies  $j\notin j^{*(t)}$ . hence,  $j^{*(t)}\subset j^{(t)}$ .

# 7.3 leverage score maintenance

we can now combine the previous results lemma 38, lemmas and lemma 40 to show that algorithm 7 yields a proof of theorem

lemma 41 (correctness of theorem 9). assume  $\epsilon \in [0,1/4]$ ,  $\psi^{(t)} \approx_{\epsilon/(12\log n)} (\mathbf{a}^\top (\mathbf{g}^{(t)})^2\mathbf{a})^{-1}$  and  $\psi_{\mathrm{(safe)}}^{(t)} \approx_{\epsilon/(24\log n)} (\mathbf{a}^\top (\mathbf{g}^{(t)})^2\mathbf{a})^{-1}$  for all  $t$ . after each call to query, the returned vector  $\tilde{\tau}_j^{(t)}$  satisfies

$$
\widetilde {\tau} ^ {(t)} \approx_ {\varepsilon} \sigma (\mathbf {g} ^ {(t)} \mathbf {a}) + \frac {d}{n}.
$$

proof. we prove this statement by induction. at the start of the algorithm the vector  $\widetilde{\tau}^{(0)}$  is a good enough approximation of the shifted leverage scores because of lemma 38. we are left with proving that with each call to query,  $\widetilde{\tau}^{(t)}$  is updated appropriately.

by the induction, we have that  $\widetilde{\tau}^{(t - 1)}\approx_{\varepsilon}\sigma (\mathbf{g}^{(t - 1)}\mathbf{a}) + \frac{d}{n}$ . furthermore, we know that  $g^{(t)}\approx_{1 / 16}g^{(t - 1)}$ . hence, we have  $\sigma (\mathbf{g}^{(t)}\mathbf{a})\approx_{1 / 4}\sigma (\mathbf{g}^{(t - 1)}\mathbf{a})$ . using  $\epsilon \in [0,1 / 4]$ , we have that  $\widetilde{\tau}^{(t - 1)}\approx_{\varepsilon}\sigma (\mathbf{g}^{(t)}\mathbf{a}) + \frac{d}{n}$ . hence, the  $\widetilde{\tau}^{(t - 1)}$  is a good enough approximation and satisfies the assumption of lemma 38 and hence the assumption of lemma 40. lemma 40 shows that findindices() finds all indices that is  $\epsilon$  far away from the shifted leverage score. lemma 38 then shows that those  $\widetilde{\tau}$  will be correctly updated. hence, all  $\widetilde{\tau}$  is at most  $\epsilon$  far away from the target. this finishes the induction.

proof of theorem 9. the correctness for the approximation ratio of theorem 9 was already proven in lemma 41. here we bound the time complexity and prove that the data-structure works against an adaptive adversary.

adaptive adversary: lemma 39 shows that the output  $\widetilde{\tau}^{(t)}$  of the data-structure is independent to findindices and d.qery w.h.p. in  $n$ . hence, the output  $\widetilde{\tau}^{(t)}$  of the data-structure depends only on estimatescore. note that estimatescore is calculated using  $\psi_{\mathrm{(safe)}}$  and some independently sampled  $\widetilde{\mathbf{r}}$ . so, it cannot leak information about the random sketch  $\mathbf{r}$  nor the input  $\psi$ . hence, the data structure works against an adaptive adversary.

to bound the complexity, we first bound how many leverage scores the data structure estimate, namely the size of  $j$ .

size of all  $j$ : an index  $j$  may be added to  $j$  in line 28. these  $j$  must satisfy  $\mathbf{w}_{j,\ell} \neq 0$  or  $g_{j}^{(t)} \neq g_{j}^{(t - 2^{i})}$ , where  $\mathbf{w}$  is the result of line 24 (d.query). let  $\mathbf{w}^{(i,t)}$  be the matrix  $\mathbf{w}$  during the  $t$ th call to findindices on scale  $i$ , then the sum of the size of all  $j$  outputs by findindices over  $t$  iterations is bounded by

$$
\sum_ {i \in \log t} \sum_ {k \in t / 2 ^ {i}} \| \mathbf {w} ^ {(i, k 2 ^ {i})} \| _ {0} + \sum_ {i \in \log t} \sum_ {k \in t / 2 ^ {i}} \| g ^ {(k 2 ^ {i})} - g ^ {(k 2 ^ {i} - 2 ^ {i})} \| _ {0} \tag {7.7}
$$

for the first term in (7.7), corollary 37 and the definition of  $\mathbf{b}$  shows that  $\mathbf{w}_{j,\ell} \neq 0$  if

$$
| (\mathbf {g} ^ {(k 2 ^ {i})} \mathbf {a} \psi^ {(k 2 ^ {i})} \mathbf {a} ^ {\top} \mathbf {g} ^ {(k 2 ^ {i})} \mathbf {r} - \mathbf {g} ^ {(k 2 ^ {i} - 2 ^ {i})} \mathbf {a} \psi^ {(k 2 ^ {i} - 2 ^ {i})} \mathbf {a} ^ {\top} \mathbf {g} ^ {(k 2 ^ {i} - 2 ^ {i})} \mathbf {r}) _ {j, \ell} | = \omega \big (\frac {\epsilon}{\log n} \sqrt {\frac {d}{n b}} \big).
$$

hence, we have that

$$
\begin{array}{l} \sum_ {k = 1} ^ {t / 2 ^ {i}} \left\| \mathbf {w} ^ {(i, k 2 ^ {i})} \right\| _ {0} \\ \leq o \left(\frac {n b \log^ {2} n}{d \epsilon^ {2}}\right) \sum_ {k = 1} ^ {t / 2 ^ {i}} \| \mathbf {g} ^ {(k 2 ^ {i})} \mathbf {a} \psi^ {(k 2 ^ {i})} \mathbf {a} ^ {\top} \mathbf {g} ^ {(k 2 ^ {i})} \mathbf {r} - \mathbf {g} ^ {(k 2 ^ {i} - 2 ^ {i})} \mathbf {a} \psi^ {(k 2 ^ {i} - 2 ^ {i})} \mathbf {a} ^ {\top} \mathbf {g} ^ {(k 2 ^ {i} - 2 ^ {i})} \mathbf {r} \| _ {f} ^ {2} \\ = o \left(\frac {n b \log^ {2} n}{d \epsilon^ {2}}\right) \left(\sum_ {k = 1} ^ {t / 2 ^ {i}} \| \mathbf {g} ^ {(k 2 ^ {i})} \mathbf {a} \psi^ {(k 2 ^ {i})} \mathbf {a} ^ {\top} \mathbf {g} ^ {(k 2 ^ {i})} \mathbf {r} - \mathbf {g} ^ {(k 2 ^ {i} - 2 ^ {i})} \mathbf {a} \psi^ {(k 2 ^ {i} - 2 ^ {i})} \mathbf {a} ^ {\top} \mathbf {g} ^ {(k 2 ^ {i} - 2 ^ {i})} \mathbf {r} \| _ {f}\right) ^ {2} \\ = o \left(\frac {n b \log^ {2} n}{d \epsilon^ {2}}\right) \left(\sum_ {k = 1} ^ {t} \| \mathbf {g} ^ {(t)} \mathbf {a} \psi^ {(t)} \mathbf {a} ^ {\top} \mathbf {g} ^ {(t)} \mathbf {r} - \mathbf {g} ^ {(t - 1)} \mathbf {a} \psi^ {(t - 1)} \mathbf {a} ^ {\top} \mathbf {g} ^ {(t - 1)} \mathbf {r} \| _ {f}\right) ^ {2} \\ = o \left(\frac {n b \log^ {2} n}{d \epsilon^ {2}}\right) \left(\sum_ {k = 1} ^ {t} \| \mathbf {g} ^ {(t)} \mathbf {a} \psi^ {(t)} \mathbf {a} ^ {\top} \mathbf {g} ^ {(t)} - \mathbf {g} ^ {(t - 1)} \mathbf {a} \psi^ {(t - 1)} \mathbf {a} ^ {\top} \mathbf {g} ^ {(t - 1)} \| _ {f}\right) ^ {2} \\ \end{array}
$$

where we used johnson-lindenstrauss lemma 35 at the end. now, summing up over  $\log t$  scales and using that we restart every  $o(n)$  iterations, we have

$$
\sum_ {i = 1} ^ {\log t} \sum_ {k = 1} ^ {t / 2 ^ {i}} \| \mathbf {w} ^ {(i, k 2 ^ {i})} \| _ {0} = o \big (\frac {n b}{d \epsilon^ {2}} \log^ {3} n \big) \cdot \left(\sum_ {k = 1} ^ {t} \| \mathbf {g} ^ {(t)} \mathbf {a} \boldsymbol {\psi} ^ {(t)} \mathbf {a} ^ {\top} \mathbf {g} ^ {(t)} - \mathbf {g} ^ {(t - 1)} \mathbf {a} \boldsymbol {\psi} ^ {(t - 1)} \mathbf {a} ^ {\top} \mathbf {g} ^ {(t - 1)} \| _ {f}\right) ^ {2}.
$$

for the second term in (7.7), we bound it simply by  $o(\log n) \cdot \sum_{t=1}^{t} \| g^{(t)} - g^{(t-1)} \|_0$ . hence, the sum of the size of all  $j$  is bounded by

$$
\begin{array}{l} \sum_ {t = 1} ^ {t} | j ^ {(t)} | \leq o \left(\frac {n}{d \epsilon^ {2}} \log^ {4} n\right) \cdot \left(\sum_ {t = 1} ^ {t} \| \mathbf {g} ^ {(t)} \mathbf {a} \psi^ {(t)} \mathbf {a} ^ {\top} \mathbf {g} ^ {(t)} - \mathbf {g} ^ {(t - 1)} \mathbf {a} \psi^ {(t - 1)} \mathbf {a} ^ {\top} \mathbf {g} ^ {(t - 1)} \| _ {f}\right) ^ {2} \\ + o (\log n) \cdot \sum_ {t = 1} ^ {t} \| g ^ {(t)} - g ^ {(t - 1)} \| _ {0}. \tag {7.8} \\ \end{array}
$$

complexity the cost of query is dominated by the cost of estimatescore, the cost of computing  $\psi^{(t)}\mathbf{b}^{(t)}$  and the cost of d.query. lemma 38 shows that the total cost of estimatescore is bounded by

$$
\underbrace {n d \delta^ {- 2} \log n} _ {\text {f i r s t s t e p}} + \underbrace {t \cdot \left(t _ {\psi} + d ^ {2}\right) \cdot \delta^ {- 2} \log n} _ {\text {c o s t o f t s t e p}} + \underbrace {\sum_ {t = 1} ^ {t} | j ^ {(t)} |} _ {\text {s i z e o f} | j |} \cdot \underbrace {d \delta^ {- 2} \log n} _ {\text {c o s t p e r s c o r e}}. \tag {7.9}
$$

the cost of computing  $\psi^{(t)}\mathbf{b}^{(t)}$  is simply  $o(t_{\psi}\log n)$  because by assumption we can multiply  $\psi^{(t)}$  with a vector in  $t_{\psi}$  time, and  $\mathbf{b}^{(t)}$  is a  $d\times o(\log n)$  matrix. finally, by corollary 37, the total cost of d.query is bounded by

$$
\begin{array}{l} \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {\log t} \sum_ {k = 1} ^ {t / 2 ^ {i}} \left\| \mathbf {g} ^ {(k 2 ^ {i})} \mathbf {a} \psi^ {(k 2 ^ {i})} \mathbf {a} ^ {\top} \mathbf {g} ^ {(k 2 ^ {i})} \mathbf {r} - \mathbf {g} ^ {(k 2 ^ {i} - 2 ^ {i})} \mathbf {a} \psi^ {(k 2 ^ {i} - 2 ^ {i})} \mathbf {a} ^ {\top} \mathbf {g} ^ {(k 2 ^ {i} - 2 ^ {i})} \mathbf {r} \right\| _ {f} ^ {2} \cdot \left(\frac {\log n}{\epsilon} \sqrt {\frac {n b}{d}}\right) ^ {2} \cdot d \log^ {3} n \\ + \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {\log t} \sum_ {k = 1} ^ {t / 2 ^ {i}} \| g ^ {(k 2 ^ {i})} - g ^ {(k 2 ^ {i} - 2 ^ {i})} \| _ {0} \cdot d \log^ {4} n \\ \end{array}
$$

simplify the term by the similar calculation on bounding  $|j|$ , we have

$$
\left(\sum_ {t = 1} ^ {t} \left\| \mathbf {g} ^ {(t)} \mathbf {a} \psi^ {(t)} \mathbf {a} ^ {\top} \mathbf {g} ^ {(t)} - \mathbf {g} ^ {(t - 1)} \mathbf {a} \psi^ {(t - 1)} \mathbf {a} ^ {\top} \mathbf {g} ^ {(t - 1)} \right\| _ {f}\right) ^ {2} \cdot \epsilon^ {- 2} n \log^ {7} n + \sum_ {t = 1} ^ {t} \| g ^ {(t)} - g ^ {(t - 1)} \| _ {0} \cdot d \log^ {5} n. \tag {7.10}
$$

combining (7.9) and (7.10) and using (7.8) and  $\delta = \frac{\epsilon}{6\log n}$ , we have the total cost bounded by

$$
\begin{array}{l} n d \epsilon^ {- 2} \log^ {3} n + t ((t _ {\psi} + d ^ {2}) \epsilon^ {- 2} \log^ {3} n + t _ {\psi} \log n) \\ + \left(\sum_ {t = 1} ^ {t} \| \mathbf {g} ^ {(t)} \mathbf {a} \boldsymbol {\psi} ^ {(t)} \mathbf {a} ^ {\top} \mathbf {g} ^ {(t)} - \mathbf {g} ^ {(t - 1)} \mathbf {a} \boldsymbol {\psi} ^ {(t - 1)} \mathbf {a} ^ {\top} \mathbf {g} ^ {(t - 1)} \| _ {f}\right) ^ {2} \cdot n \epsilon^ {- 4} \log^ {7} n \\ + \left(\sum_ {t = 1} ^ {t} \| g ^ {(t)} - g ^ {(t - 1)} \| _ {0}\right) \cdot \left(d \epsilon^ {- 2} \log^ {5} n\right) \\ \end{array}
$$

we charge the first term to initialize and the last term to scale. this completes the proof for the time complexity.

# 8 inverse maintenance with leverage score hints

in this section, we show how to approximately maintain the inverse  $(\mathbf{a}^{\top}\mathbf{w}\mathbf{a})^{-1}$  where  $\mathbf{a} \in \mathbb{r}^{n \times d}$  with  $n \gg d$  in amortized  $\widetilde{\mathrm{o}}(d^{\omega - (1/2)} + d^2)$  per step under changes to  $\mathbf{w}$  using estimates of the leverage scores of  $\mathbf{w}^{1/2}\mathbf{a}$ . when  $n \approx d$ , this is a well-studied problem that has been solved in [ls15, akps19, cls19, bns19, san04]; no information of leverage score is required by these algorithms and it is possible to achieve a runtime of roughly  $n^{\omega - (1/2)} + n^2$ . however, when  $n \gg d$ , we need leverage scores to avoid reading the whole matrix  $\mathbf{a}$  every step and achieve the (possibly sublinear) time bound of  $\widetilde{\mathrm{o}}(d^{\omega - (1/2)} + d^2)$ .

one approach to obtain this result is to first approximate  $\mathbf{a}^{\top}\mathbf{w}\mathbf{a}$  by  $\mathbf{a}^{\top}\mathbf{v}\mathbf{a}$  for some sparse diagonal matrix  $\mathbf{v}$  with  $\widetilde{\mathrm{o}}(d)$  non-zeros using leverage score sampling each step and then maintain  $\mathbf{a}^{\top}\mathbf{v}\mathbf{a}$  via woodbury matrix identity. unfortunately, this does not immediately work as the diagonal matrices  $\mathbf{v}$  may then differ widely in every step, inhibiting our ability to perform smaller low-rank updates per step.

to handle this issue of changing  $\mathbf{v}$ , [ls15] resampled rows of  $\mathbf{a}$  only when the corresponding leverage score changed significantly. with this trick, the diagonal matrix  $\mathbf{v}$  changes slowly enough to maintain. however, this approach only works if the input of the algorithm is independent on the output. in [ls15], this issue was addressed by using the maintained inverse as a pre-conditioner to solve linear systems in  $\mathbf{a}^{\top}\mathbf{w}\mathbf{a}$  to high precision and then added noise to the output. however, we cannot afford to read the whole matrix  $\mathbf{a}$  each step and therefore need to modify this technique.

to avoid reading the entire matrix  $\mathbf{a}$ , we instead make further use of the given given leverage score to sample the matrix  $\mathbf{a}^{\top}\mathbf{u}\mathbf{a} \approx \mathbf{a}^{\top}\mathbf{w}\mathbf{a}$ . instead of solving  $\mathbf{a}^{\top}\mathbf{w}\mathbf{a}x = b$ , we use the maintained inverse  $(\mathbf{a}^{\top}\mathbf{v}\mathbf{a})^{-1}$  as a pre-conditioner to solve  $\mathbf{a}^{\top}\mathbf{u}\mathbf{a}x = b$ . by solving  $\mathbf{a}^{\top}\mathbf{u}\mathbf{a}x = b$  very accurately and by adding a small amount of noise, we can hide any information about  $\mathbf{v}$ . this is formalized by algorithm 8 (and its second half algorithm 9).

theorem 10 (inverse maintenance). given a full rank matrix  $\mathbf{a} \in \mathbb{r}^{n \times d}$  with  $n \geq d$  and error tolerance  $\epsilon \in (0, 1/10)$ , there is a data structure that approximately solves a sequence of linear systems  $\mathbf{a}^{\top} \widetilde{\mathbf{w}} \mathbf{a} y = b \in \mathbb{r}^{d}$  for positive diagonal matrices  $\widetilde{\mathbf{w}} \in \mathbb{r}^{n \times n}$  through the following operation:

- initializer(a,  $\tilde{w},\tilde{\tau},\epsilon$ ): given matrix  $\mathbf{a}\in \mathbb{r}^{n\times d}$ , scaling  $\tilde{w}\in \mathbb{r}_{>0}^n$ , shifted leverage score estimates  $\tilde{\tau}\in \mathbb{r}_{>0}^n$ , and accuracy  $\epsilon \in (0,1 / 10)$ , the data-structure preprocesses in  $o(d^{\omega})$  time.  
-  $\mathsf{update}(\widetilde{w},\widetilde{\tau})$ : output a matrix  $\psi \in \mathbb{r}^{d\times d}$  and vector  $\widetilde{w}^{(\mathrm{alg})}$  where  $\psi^{-1}$  is close to  $\mathbf{a}^\top \widetilde{\mathbf{w}}\mathbf{a} \in \mathbb{r}^{d\times d}$  and  $\widetilde{w}^{(\mathrm{alg})}$  is close to  $\widetilde{w}$ .  
-  $solve(b, \overline{w}, \delta)$ : input  $\overline{w} \approx_1 \tilde{w}$  and  $\delta > 0$ , output  $y = \psi b \in \mathbb{r}^d$  for some random matrix  $\psi^{-1} \in \mathbb{r}^{d \times d}$  that is close to  $\mathbf{a}^\top \overline{\mathbf{w}} \mathbf{a} \in \mathbb{r}^{d \times d}$ .

let  $\tau(w) \stackrel{\mathrm{def}}{=} \tau(\mathbf{wa})$ . suppose that all estimate shifted leverage scores  $\widetilde{\tau}_i \in (1 \pm \frac{1}{16[\log d]})\tau(w)_i$  for  $i \in [n]^4$  and that there is a sequence  $w^{(0)}, w^{(1)}, \dots, w^{(k)} \in \mathbb{r}^n$  such that the  $w^{(k)}$  satisfy

$$
\frac {1}{\epsilon^ {2}} \| \left(\mathbf {w} ^ {(k)}\right) ^ {- 1} \left(w ^ {(k + 1)} - w ^ {(k)}\right) \| _ {\tau (w ^ {(k)})} ^ {2} + \| \left(\mathbf {t} \left(w ^ {(k)}\right)\right) ^ {- 1} \left(\tau \left(w ^ {(k + 1)}\right) - \tau \left(w ^ {(k)}\right)\right) \| _ {\tau (w ^ {(k)})} ^ {2} \leq o (1) \tag {4.1}
$$

for  $k = 0,1,\dots ,k - 1$  with  $k = n^{o(1)}$ . further assume that the update sequence  $\tilde{w}^{(0)},\tilde{w}^{(1)},\dots ,\tilde{w}^{(k)}\in \mathbb{r}^n$  satisfies  $\tilde{w}^{(k)}\approx_{\epsilon /(16\log d)}w^{(k)}$  for all  $k$  and the  $\tilde{w}^{(k)}$  are independent to the output of update and solve. (the input  $b^{(k)}$  can depend on the previous output of the data structure.) then, we have the following:

21 return ψ, w(alg)

- the amortized time per call of update is  $o(n + \epsilon^{-2} \cdot (d^{\omega - \frac{1}{2}} + d^{2}) \cdot \log^{3/2}(n))$ .  
the time per call of solve is  $o(n + \delta^{-2} \cdot d^2 \cdot \log^2 (n / \delta))$ .  
- update outputs some  $\psi$ ,  $\widetilde{w}^{(\mathrm{alg})}$  where  $\psi^{-1} = \mathbf{a}^{\top}\widetilde{\mathbf{w}}^{(\mathrm{alg})}\mathbf{a} \approx_{\epsilon} \mathbf{a}^{\top}\widetilde{\mathbf{w}}\mathbf{a}$  with probability  $1 - 1/\mathrm{poly}(n)$  and  $\widetilde{w}^{(\mathrm{alg})} \approx_{\epsilon} \widetilde{w}$ .  
- solve outputs some  $y = \psi b$  where  $\psi^{-1} \approx_{\delta} \mathbf{a}^{\top}\overline{\mathbf{w}}\mathbf{a}$  with probability  $1 - 1 / \mathrm{poly}(n)$  and  $\mathbb{e}[\psi b] = (\mathbf{a}^{\top}\overline{\mathbf{w}}\mathbf{a})^{-1}b$ .

before we prove theorem 10 we prove two helper lemmas. first, note that theorem 10 asks for the solver to return a solution  $y = \psi b$  where  $\psi^{-1} \approx_{\delta} \mathbf{a}^{\top} \overline{\mathbf{w}} \mathbf{a}$  and  $\mathbb{e}[\psi b] = (\mathbf{a}^{\top} \overline{\mathbf{w}} \mathbf{a})^{-1} b$ . the following lemma shows how we can satisfy this condition on the expectation while having only a small additive error to our solution. the later lemma 43 then shows that this small additive error can be interpreted as a small spectral error of the inverse used to solve the system.

lemma 42. let  $\mathbf{m} \in \mathbb{r}^{d \times d}$  be a positive definite matrix and let  $\mathbf{m}^{(1)}, \mathbf{m}^{(2)}, \ldots$  be a sequence of independent random  $d \times d$  matrices, each with  $\mathbb{e}[\mathbf{m}^{(k)}] = \mathbf{m}$  that satisfy with high probability  $(1 - \epsilon)\mathbf{m} \prec \mathbf{m}^{(k)} \prec (1 + \epsilon)\mathbf{m}$  for some  $\frac{1}{8} \geq \epsilon > 0$ . further let  $\mathbf{n}^{(1)}, \mathbf{n}^{(2)}, \ldots$  be a sequence

algorithm 9: inverse maintenance with leverage score hints (continuation of algorithm 8)  
22 procedure  $solve(b \in \mathbb{r}^d, \overline{w} \in \mathbb{r}_{>0}^n, \delta > 0)$  // secure and unbiased version  
23  $y \gets$  securesolve $(b, \overline{w}, \delta/2^{20})$ .  $y^{(0)} \gets y$ .  
24 let  $x \in \mathbb{r}_{\geq 0}$  be geometrically distributed such that  $\mathbb{p}[x \geq k] = 2^{-k}$ .
